Med Hard?

O'Make Way for Taylor



O'Make Way for Taylor
Med Hard?
An O'Make Way, O'Malley! story

An homage to Ack's I, Panacea and Security! and their passenger-seat premise.

Fandom: Die Hard 2 x Worm

---

The Legal Part (Such As It Is)

Die Hard 2 and John McClane belong to 20th Century Studios, by way of Gordon Company and Silver Pictures, based on characters originated by Roderick Thorp and carried forward by Walter Wager, and yes, that's currently a Disney property too, same as half the industry these days. Worm and everyone in Brockton Bay belong to Wildbow, who wrote several million words of it into existence on his own steam and owes no studio anything for the privilege. One side of this crossover answers to a mouse. The other answers to a guy on the internet with an extremely punishing update schedule. They have never met, and this story is the closest they're going to get.

No infringement is intended and no profit is being made. If anyone from Fox, Disney, or Wildbow's own inbox happens to be reading this: it was Loki's idea again. He gets around.

This is a fan work, offered freely, for love of both properties, an homage to Ack's I, Panacea and Security! and their passenger-seat premise, per the series-wide note above, and to Rob O'Make O'Malley, who does not, as far as anyone can prove, actually exist, and who would very much like it kept that way.

The Restless Clause

O'Make Way, O'Malley! is my own sandbox, not Ack's; the debt to I, Panacea and Security! is one of inspiration and premise, not continuation, and this story is not set in Ack's continuity or written with his characters.

That said, per Rule 9a: I, Maestro, as the creator and sandbox owner of O'Make Way, O'Malley!, give explicit, fully open permission for any author to write additional stories set in this sandbox, the ROB O'Make O'Malley passenger-seat premise, its core mechanics, and the anthology format described in the series bible. No further conditions apply, beyond the standard disclaimer and credit requirements already required of you, and beyond whatever additional terms may be imposed at the sole discretion of Mr. O'Malley, who cannot currently be reached for comment and has historically been unreliable about explaining himself even when he could be.

The Second Opinion Clause

Mr. O'Malley, despite considerable and largely unearned confidence in his own recruiting instincts, would welcome a critical eye on his work. Beta readers, canon nitpickers, and anyone willing to point out that a chokepoint isn't actually a chokepoint are warmly invited to reach out before a chapter posts, not after. He finds retroactive correction far less charming than most reviewers seem to assume he would.

Reviews and thoughts are genuinely welcome. Flames will be filed, calmly, under Things That Do Not Rattle a Man Who Has Already Walked Across Broken Glass Barefoot, and answered with all the urgency the building's air traffic control tower generally reserves for a plane that's still got fuel to spare.

---



Chapter 1: The Gate Closes



The cargo hold under Terminal C smelled like jet fuel and wet cardboard, and forty feet up, past two floors of drywall and bad acoustics, somewhere in a control tower McClane hadn't been able to get anyone to listen to him inside of, seven planes were circling Dulles on fumes because a bunch of ex-military lunatics wanted their general back badly enough to let a few hundred strangers freeze to death thirty thousand feet up in the process.

Somewhere above him, muffled through concrete and rebar, he heard it again: the low, rolling concussion of something big enough to rattle the light fixtures, followed a half-second later by the flat silence of an alarm that had been triggered and then, deliberately, cut. Third one tonight. He'd stopped counting which parts of the terminal each blast had come from an hour ago, mostly because counting implied he had a plan for doing anything about it, and right now his plan began and ended with the guy currently trying to introduce a knife to his kidneys.

He tried not to think about the fuel gauges. Thinking about the fuel gauges made his hands shake, and his hands were currently the only thing standing between him and a knife.

The luggage belt kept grinding along on its own schedule above him, oblivious, hauling somebody's golf clubs toward a carousel nobody was going to be around to claim them from if this went much longer. McClane had noticed that about airports tonight: nothing in the building seemed to know there was a war on. The PA system kept apologizing for delays. A cart of duty-free liquor sat abandoned twenty feet away, glowing faintly under an emergency light, like it was waiting for somebody to remember it existed.

The hold had been built for machines and people who knew better than to stand where the machines wanted to go. Two belts crossed at different heights, one carrying bags in from the apron, the other feeding them toward the terminal. Steel guide rails divided the concrete floor into lanes. Yellow cages waited beside a roll-up door that had jammed three feet above the ground, leaving a strip of night beneath it and blowing needling snow across the floor. Beyond that gap, blue taxiway lights burned through the weather. Every few seconds a jet passed somewhere outside, unseen but close enough to push a deep vibration through the door and into McClane's teeth.

There were exits. There were always exits. A fire door behind the mercenary led toward the service stairs. The jammed cargo door might lift if someone reached the chain control on the wall. A maintenance ladder climbed to a grated catwalk above the belts. All three were useless while the man with the knife owned the middle of the room, but useless and nonexistent were different categories. McClane kept counting them because counting was what he had left.

The mercenary had counted too. He had kicked the fire door's crash bar loose before closing in, jamming the latch with a strip of torn baggage tag. He had put himself between McClane and the wall control. He had glanced once toward the ladder and never needed to glance again. That made four pieces of information McClane didn't enjoy and one he could use: the guy thought the ladder mattered.

He didn't have a gun anymore. He'd had one twenty minutes ago, back when this had still felt like a plan instead of a fistfight he was losing. Somewhere under the Chivas Regal, probably, if anybody ever bothered to look. What he had instead was a length of pipe he'd torn off a luggage cart rail, a shirt with more of him showing through it than fabric left to cover, and a guy in Army surplus fatigues who fought like he'd actually earned the uniform once, before he'd started renting himself out to whoever was willing to hold an airport hostage this Christmas.

It had started, technically, with a phone call. Holly's flight, a delay he could've lived with, a plan to grab coffee and pretend the last however-many months apart hadn't happened. Somewhere between the coffee and the second delay announcement, a bunch of mercenaries with a grudge and a general to spring had decided Dulles's tower made a fine hostage, and McClane, who had never once in his career managed to be somewhere bad things weren't also happening, had gone looking for a working radio and found himself, four fights and one dead priest impersonator later, exactly here: underground, bleeding, out of good ideas, with nobody topside inclined to believe a word he said about any of it.

The guy had a knife. Military-issue, well-kept, the kind of blade a man sharpened on his own time because he respected it.

*"Ah, the well-maintained blade: cinema's oldest shorthand for 'this one's a professional, folks,'"* the reviewer noted, warming up.

*"Two in one chapter?!"* the reviewer said, sounding almost personally offended by his own pacing.

McClane had opinions about people who brought knives to airports. He'd had opinions about people who brought guns to airports too, twenty minutes ago, and about people who impersonated priests forty minutes before that, and by this point in the evening he was starting to think Dulles just attracted a certain kind of clientele.

He was in the process of sharing exactly one of those opinions, wordlessly, with the pipe, when the knife came in under his guard and caught him low along the ribs. A line of cold, not pain yet, pain took a second to catch up to news like that, and for one useless heartbeat all he registered was the temperature.

"Okay," he said, mostly to himself, mostly to keep from thinking about the part where thinking about it would slow him down. "Okay. That's gonna be a thing later."

The guy didn't answer. He wasn't the talking kind, this one, unlike the last three, who'd all wanted McClane to know exactly how outmatched he was before they tried to prove it. This one just reset his stance, unhurried, like a man who'd done this enough times to know panic cost more than it bought.

McClane let the end of the pipe dip.

The mercenary's eyes followed it for a fraction of a second. Not much. Enough.

McClane threw the pipe at the cargo-door control instead of at him.

It struck the metal housing with a flat clang and mashed the green button beneath its end. The motor above the door coughed awake. Chain rattled through a guide. The jammed door lurched six inches upward, caught, and shook hard enough to dump a shelf of packed snow down through the opening. Wind punched into the hold, lifted loose baggage tags into the air, and sent the nearest empty luggage tub rolling across the concrete.

The mercenary moved before the tub reached him. He stepped onto its low plastic lip, let its momentum carry him sideways past the obstruction, and came off it with the knife already driving forward.

"Yeah," McClane muttered, backing toward the belts. "Had to be the one guy who knows how to use public transportation."

He came in low, testing, a feint toward McClane's ribs that wasn't really a feint at all, more an invitation to flinch wrong, and McClane didn't take it, brought the pipe around instead in a short, ugly arc that connected with the guy's forearm hard enough to numb it for exactly as long as it took him to switch the knife to his other hand and keep coming, unbothered, like the arm had belonged to somebody else the whole time. That was the part that worried McClane most, more than the knife, more than the blood he could feel soaking warm down one side. Pain that didn't slow a man down wasn't the same animal as bravery. It was just a different, more patient kind of dangerous.

The second cut opened his sleeve above the elbow. Shallow. Expensive anyway. McClane gave ground, caught the edge of a yellow baggage cage with one hand, and shoved it between them. The mercenary didn't waste time trying to go around. He drove a boot into the wire side. The whole cage jumped backward and trapped McClane's wrist against a guide rail.

The knife came through one of the squares.

McClane turned his head. Steel scraped the rail beside his cheek with a sound like somebody sharpening a kitchen knife directly inside his ear. He let go of the cage, dropped below the second thrust, and hauled the emergency release mounted at its base.

Three hundred pounds of suitcases spilled out.

Hard shells, soft canvas, garment bags, a child's pink case with one wheel already missing. They hit the floor and spread between the two men. The mercenary had to shorten his next step. McClane didn't. He planted one foot on the pink case, rode it half a yard like the world's worst skateboard, and hit the man in the mouth with the heel of his hand.

It bought him blood, one broken front tooth, and no meaningful improvement in the mercenary's attitude.

The man caught McClane by the shirt and drove him backward into the steel cage. Wire punched a grid into his spine. The knife rose between them. McClane trapped the wrist with both hands, but the man's other forearm pressed across his throat, compressing everything down to a hot, airless contest measured in inches.

Outside, landing lights passed low across the strip beneath the cargo door. A jet engine thundered close enough to shake snow out of the opening and make the hanging fluorescent fixtures sway. For one second the whole room pulsed with moving shadows.

The mercenary looked toward the door.

McClane bit him.

Not dignified. Not in any manual. Effective enough to get the forearm off his throat.

He wrenched the knife hand down against the cage, once, twice. On the third impact the fingers opened. The knife dropped among the luggage and vanished beneath a black duffel.

Both men went after it.

McClane kicked the duffel onto the lower belt. The bag vanished through the rubber curtain toward the terminal with the knife underneath it, a little gift for whichever baggage handler came back to work after the airport stopped being occupied by terrorists.

The mercenary hit him before he could enjoy that thought. A fist caught him behind the ear. The hold tilted. McClane stumbled across the moving belt, grabbed at a suspended sign, tore it loose instead, and came down with the aluminum panel in both hands.

NO RIDERS, it said in red block letters.

He hit the mercenary with it.

The panel folded around the man's shoulder. The man tore it away, and McClane got one good second to wish regulations were made of stronger material.

McClane got a hand on his wrist before the knife could come back around for a better angle. The guy got a knee into McClane's stomach that emptied every ounce of air he owned and put a bright white flash behind his eyes that had nothing to do with the fluorescents overhead. They went down together into a stack of unclaimed suitcases, hard enough that something inside one of them, glass, maybe, or a bottle wrapped for gifting, gave way with a soft, apologetic crunch.

They came up together too, tangled, neither one willing to let go first, and staggered a full three steps sideways into the belt itself, McClane's shoulder blades slamming down onto cold rubber that immediately, indifferently, started trying to carry him toward the terminal wall at the same slow, patient speed it had been carrying luggage all night. The guy rode him down onto it, weight pinning, knife hand straining against McClane's grip an inch at a time, close enough now that McClane could see, past the blade, a face that wasn't angry at all, just professionally disappointed, a man annoyed at how long a job was taking rather than afraid of losing it. McClane got a boot up against a support strut and shoved, hard, sending them both rolling off the belt's edge in a graceless tangle of limbs and a second, louder crunch that this time was somebody's ankle finding the concrete wrong, and for one confused half-second neither of them was entirely sure whose.

It wasn't his. He found that out the hard way, trying to stand on it, and only the guy's own hiss of pain, quickly swallowed, told him whose bad luck it had actually been.

He didn't wait for the guy to recover from it. Some lessons Dulles had already taught him twice tonight and he wasn't about to need a third reminder, and he drove a shoulder into the guy's chest while the bad ankle was still buckling under him, both of them going down again, harder this time, no belt to soften it, just concrete and a fresh stack of suitcases that hadn't asked to be involved in any of this.

He landed wrong. He landed on the ribs the knife had already found, and for a second the whole hold went narrow and bright and very far away, sound arriving late, like the room had put itself on a half-second delay just to spite him.

He got the pipe up anyway. Some things you did on muscle memory once thought stopped being fast enough to keep up.

His last clear thought, the kind he'd have been embarrassed to say out loud if there'd been anyone around who wasn't currently trying to open him up like carry-on luggage, was that Holly was going to kill him worse than this guy was even attempting to, if he missed her flight over something as stupid as bleeding out next to Baggage Claim 4. Twelve years married, most of it apart, and this was going to be the thing that finally did it. Not the distance. Not the job. A rented mercenary with good posture and a knife he clearly loved more than most people loved their kids.

He got the pipe moving.

It never landed.

There wasn't a sound so much as an absence of one, every grinding belt and every distant, oblivious PA announcement and the guy's breathing and his own, all of it switched off at once, like someone had found the master breaker for the whole building and thrown it without so much as a warning flicker first. The cold from the knife wound didn't finish arriving. The pipe didn't finish swinging, hung instead in some half-completed arc that no longer had a target worth completing it for. McClane didn't fall, because falling required a floor, and the floor had just stopped being a promise anybody was in a position to keep him.

*"'So much as'? That's a trifecta, right there in the very first chapter,"* the reviewer said, visibly pleased with himself for counting.

He didn't lose consciousness so much as lose the room around it. The suitcases went first, then the light, then the personal weight of a knife wound that had been, up until a second ago, the most urgent piece of information his body had to offer him. It all just stopped mattering, all at once, the way nothing ever actually stopped mattering in real life, which was how he knew, somewhere underneath the part of him still trying to finish a punch, that whatever this was, it wasn't anything he'd trained for.

He'd been shot. He'd been stabbed, technically, currently, ongoing. He'd fallen off a building once and lived to make a joke about it. None of that had ever felt like this: not pain, not fear, just a very quiet, very total unhooking, like a plane easing off a jet bridge with the door already sealed behind it.

The last thing that made it through, thin and already fading, wasn't a threat assessment or an exit count or any of the dozen things thirty years on the job had trained him to reach for first. It was simpler than that, and stupider, and entirely his own, delivered in the same flat, running-commentary voice he used on everything else life had thrown at him tonight:

Hell of a time for the gate to close.



Chapter 2: Now Boarding



There was no cargo hold in the room he woke into. No suitcases, no knife, no floor that had any business holding him up after what had just happened to it.

There was, unaccountably, a radar scope. Green light pulsing off it in a slow, patient sweep, tracking blips that didn't correspond to any airspace McClane had ever flown a commercial ticket through. A bank of consoles ran along one wall, humming the low electrical hum of every ops room he'd ever leaned in the doorway of and been told to get out of, and a coffee pot sat half-full on a burner that had clearly been on too long, because the smell of it had gone from coffee to something closer to punishment.

He didn't remember sitting down in the chair he found himself in. He was fairly sure he hadn't.

McClane didn't reach for anything. Didn't touch the door behind him, though he clocked it, the hinge side, the fact that its little illuminated EXIT sign was in English but spelled in a font no airport he'd ever worked would have paid a sign contractor to install. He counted exits before he counted his own pulse, always had, and the fact that his hands weren't shaking told him less about how calm he was than it told him this room hadn't earned a reaction out of him yet.

It felt wrong the way a forged boarding pass felt wrong. Not obviously. Not on the first pass. Everything present and accounted for, right down to the burnt coffee, and somehow still off, like a man who'd only ever heard Dulles Ops described over a radio and gotten every last detail half an inch out of true.

The wall clock ran, which was its own small wrongness once he noticed it, hands sweeping a steady, unhurried twelve hours that didn't match anything McClane's own body was telling him about how long he'd been unconscious. A corkboard hung crooked by the door, pinned with memos about runway deicing schedules and a holiday party nobody would ever attend, every word of it perfectly legible and perfectly beside the point, like a stage dressed by somebody who'd read the script once and skipped the parts that mattered.

He clocked exits again anyway. Old habit didn't care that the room made no sense.

Then he checked himself.

The cut along his ribs was gone. Not bandaged. Gone. His shirt remained sliced and wet with blood, but the skin beneath it was unbroken when he pressed two fingers there. The smaller cut above his elbow had vanished too. His knuckles were still split. The old scar on his shoulder remained. Whoever had cleaned up the new damage had either been selective or had rules about what counted as part of him.

His ankle supported his weight. His lungs drew a full breath without complaint. He could taste blood from the mercenary's punch, but when he ran his tongue across his teeth, none were loose.

McClane crossed to the door and opened it.

The same room waited on the other side.

Not a copy. The same room, viewed from the opposite wall. He could see his own hand holding the door open across twenty feet of impossible air. When he leaned through, the other version of him leaned in too, not a reflection but the back of his own head and shoulders occupying the place where the corridor should have been.

He shut the door.

"Okay," he told the burnt coffee. "Door's decorative. Good to know."

The radar swept again. Seven green returns moved around the outer edge, each slow and dim. An eighth remained near the center, brighter than the rest. When McClane stepped closer, the central return pulsed in time with his heartbeat.

He reached through the glass. Cold closed around his fingers, followed by a sensation like radio static running beneath his skin. For an instant he heard a crowd, a string instrument, a glass touching a tray. Then it vanished.

Somebody was already on the other end.

He got up, testing the floor first with one boot before he trusted it with his full weight, the way you tested ice you didn't trust, and crossed to the radar scope on legs that felt steadier than a man who'd just been stabbed had any right to expect. The blips on the screen didn't behave like weather or traffic. They pulsed, slow and rhythmic, less like aircraft and more like something with a heartbeat, and when he reached out, half out of curiosity and half out of the old cop's need to touch a thing before he believed in it, his fingers passed through the glass like it wasn't there at all, cold air on the other side of a screen that had every visual right to stop him and didn't.

He pulled his hand back. Some doors you didn't need opened twice to know you didn't want to walk through them yet.

"You're doing the thing," a voice said, from somewhere behind his left shoulder. "Casing the joint before you've even said hello."

*"'You're doing the thing,'"* Fury repeated, flat. *"I said that. Word for word. Does he not have his own opening line?"*

*"Maybe it's a company script,"* Gladly offered. *"Laminated card, one per operative."*

McClane didn't startle. He'd trained that particular reflex out of himself somewhere around his second year on the job, in rooms considerably less strange than this one. He turned, unhurried, and found a man in a windbreaker with DULLES OPS stenciled across the chest, standing at the radar scope with a coffee cup that had definitely not been in his hand a second ago, reading a strip of paper off the console with the unbothered attention of a guy who'd never once in his career had to explain a missing airplane to anybody who outranked him.

"Trudeau."

"That's the name on the jacket." The man didn't look up from the strip. "You gonna ask me how I know yours, or you want to skip to the part where you tell me it doesn't fit?"

"The face fits fine." McClane kept his voice level, cataloging again, not the room this time but the man: the half-second delays in how he moved, a stillness underneath the windbreaker that didn't track with a guy whose whole job was managing chaos on a headset. The real Trudeau, from the two minutes McClane had spent trading orders with him over a radio tonight, ran hot, snapped at subordinates, chewed through cigarettes like they owed him money. This one hadn't touched the pack sitting open on the console once. "The guy wearing it's the part that doesn't."

Something that might have been approval crossed the borrowed face. "There it is." He set the coffee down, made a small unhurried mark on the strip without glancing down to find the line for it, and McClane caught it anyway, upside down, before the man folded it away: SITUATIONAL AWARENESS, checked off like a controller confirming separation on two aircraft that had, against expectation, missed each other. "Most people spend the first five minutes arguing with the furniture. You went straight for the actor. I respect that."

"What happened to me."

"You dropped. Middle of a knife fight, I understand, which is a hell of a place to have a scheduling conflict." He said it like the punchline of a joke he'd already told himself once and liked. "Real Trudeau's still up in that tower right now, fighting his own war with a bunch of very serious men in very serious jackets, and he's not gonna notice you're gone, because from where he's standing, you never left. That's not a comfort. I just find people ask less follow-up if I get it out of the way early."

Something in McClane's chest did a slow, cold turn that had nothing to do with the knife wound he could no longer feel. "The guy I was fighting."

"Still fighting. Time back there isn't holding its breath for you." A shrug, easy, like the subject bored him slightly. "You'll get your rematch. Not tonight, and not with me standing here explaining the paperwork."

"You're telling me a man's still bleeding out on a concrete floor right now, this exact second, and you want to talk paperwork."

"I'm telling you time doesn't work the way your gut wants it to right now, and no amount of you being upset about that changes the math." No apology in it, just a flat, patient correction, the kind a man gave when he'd had this exact conversation with other people before and expected to have it again. "He gets handled. Not by you tonight. That's the part I need you to actually hear, because I've had this go sideways before with guys who couldn't let it go."

"What paperwork."

"Wrong question, but I'll allow it, you're new." Trudeau, or the thing wearing him, pulled the strip back out and held it up, and McClane, despite every instinct telling him not to give a stranger the satisfaction, looked.

INCIDENT REPORT. SUBJECT: ONE (1) JOHN MCCLANE. LAST KNOWN POSITION: TERMINAL C, SUBLEVEL, ARMED AND UNCOOPERATIVE. STATUS: EXTRACTED. DESTINATION: PASSENGER SEAT, SEE ATTACHED. PRIORITY: URGENT, RETROACTIVELY. CLEARANCE: APPROVED BY:

The line wasn't blank. A signature sat in the approval box, cramped and impatient, the kind of scrawl a man used when he'd already decided the form was a waste of everybody's time before he'd finished signing it.

*"Oh, hang on,"* Gladly said, delighted with himself. *"'The kind of scrawl a man used when'... didn't we already do 'the kind of blade a man sharpened' back in Chapter One? That's a callback! That's very structurally intentional of him!"*

*"It's not intentional,"* Fury said. *"It's a tic."*

McClane knew it the way he knew his own reflection, immediately, without having to check twice.

It was his.

He had never seen this form in his life.

"You want to explain that," he said, and was quietly satisfied to hear his own voice hadn't moved an inch, "or should I just take it up with the guy who apparently already signed off on it?"

"Keeps me honest, having you countersign yourself. Cuts down on complaints later." He tapped the box without looking at it, entirely unbothered by the question underneath the question. "Names, if you want a few. Rob O'Make O'Malley, if you want the one that's actually mine. Facilitator. Occasional air traffic controller for narratives that can't find their own runway. On my better nights, I like to think of it as search and rescue." He said the last part like a line he'd used enough times to still enjoy it, unbothered by how little of it would land with the man across from him. "Call me whatever's comfortable. I answer to most things, long as the voice sells it."

"Passenger seat," McClane said. "Your form. What does it mean?"

O'Malley picked up the coffee, considered it, and put it down again without drinking. "It means you go where she goes. You see what she sees, hear what she hears, and talk to her without anybody else hearing unless she speaks your words aloud. You bring your memory, judgment, instincts, and every bad habit you've spent a lifetime making look useful."

"And control?"

"Hers. Always."

"You put me in a fifteen-year-old's head and I can't move a hand if somebody comes at her."

"Not unless she lends it to you. If she does, she can take it back at any moment. No contest, no tug-of-war, no clever exception hidden in the fine print. Her body remains hers."

McClane looked toward the radar return at the center of the scope. It had begun moving between two of the dimmer lights.

"Can she get me out?"

"No. Neither can you."

"Can you?"

"Eventually. That answer is less useful than you want it to be."

"Can she shut me up?"

"You can both withdraw. Think around each other instead of at each other. Sleep helps. Distance does not, because there won't be any."

"Privacy."

"Possible. Learned. Imperfect at first." O'Malley met his eyes. "You will know more about each other than either of you would choose to hand a stranger. That is part of the cost, and I am telling you before you agree because consent offered without the ugly parts is advertising, not consent."

McClane gave him a long look. "You practice that line?"

"The ugly parts taught it to me."

For the first time, O'Malley's borrowed face looked older than Trudeau's actual face had any right to look.

"What happens here?" McClane asked. "Dulles. Holly. The planes."

"You return to the instant you left. Your body finishes the fight. Your wife lands. The rest of your life remains yours, including the parts you keep making harder than they need to be."

"And I remember her."

"Yes."

"She remembers me."

"Yes."

"Then 'return' isn't really the word, is it?"

"No," O'Malley said. "It usually isn't."

"The voice sells it fine. The guy behind it doesn't." McClane studied him, not the uniform this time, the man underneath it, the beat of stillness where a control tower chief should've been reaching for a radio, a phone, anything with a wire attached to a crisis. "Trudeau doesn't stand still. Not tonight. Not with seven planes stacked up on fumes and half his tower held at gunpoint. You've been parked in that same spot since I looked up."

"No," O'Malley agreed, and for a second the easy amusement thinned enough to show something underneath it that wasn't unkind exactly, but wasn't comfortable either. "I haven't got anywhere to be. That's the whole difference between us, if you want it in one sentence."

"What do you want."

"Wrong question again. Getting better, though."

*"There's the 'wrong question' bit again,"* Fury said, dry. *"He does this to everybody. It's practically a hazing ritual."*

*"I got graded on a curve too,"* Gladly said, wistful. *"Took me three tries to land on the right one."*

O'Malley set the coffee cup down for good and came around the console, and the borrowed face, for the first time since McClane had turned to look at it, went entirely serious. "There's a girl. Not a soldier, not a suspect, not anybody your line of work has ever had a file open on, or ever will, not through any door you'd recognize. She's about to find out the hard way that the people running things in her city aren't who they say they are, and she's got the tools to do something about it and not one day of the training that usually comes bundled with tools like that."

"How old."

"Fifteen." O'Malley let that sit a second before he went on, no theatrics in it, just weight. "Old enough to know when a room's lying to her. Young enough that nobody with any actual power in that city's bothered learning her name yet. That's about to change tonight, whether either of you wants it to or not."

"And if I say no. If I sit right here in this chair and tell you to find somebody else."

"Then I find somebody else, and you go back to a knife fight that's already decided how it ends without you in it, and somewhere else, tonight, a girl finds out the hard way what it costs to be right and alone at the same time." No cruelty in the delivery, just an accounting, laid out flat. "I'm not gonna threaten you into a yes. Never have, never will. I just don't think you're the kind of man who hears the rest of that sentence and still says no."

McClane walked to the radar scope. The bright return moved near the center while the seven dim lights continued circling far beyond it. He put his palm through the glass this time.

The room on the other side came in pieces. White light. Music. A man's laugh polished smooth by repetition. Beneath all of it, a girl's attention moving across exits, hands, doors, and distances with the anxious precision of someone who expected rooms to turn against her because enough rooms already had.

Fifteen, O'Malley had said.

McClane thought about Lucy at fifteen. Thought about how much of her life he had missed while telling himself work was the reason and not the shelter. Thought about Holly circling above Dulles while strangers decided what her fuel gauge was worth. He had spent the whole night furious that nobody in authority would believe him. The idea of leaving a kid inside that sentence by herself landed harder than the pitch deserved.

He pulled his hand back.

"Does she know I'm coming?"

"Not yet."

"That's lousy procedure."

"You can open with an apology."

"I'm not great at those."

"One of several reasons I chose you."

McClane said nothing. It was, though he had no way of knowing it yet, the first professional silence of an entirely new career.

He'd have said no, if there'd been a version of this where saying no meant anything. Thirty years on the job had taught him the shape of a pitch, and this was a pitch, dressed up in borrowed gravitas and a coffee cup he was pretty sure hadn't existed twenty minutes ago. It was also, he had to admit, working. Not because O'Malley had made it sound easy. Because he hadn't.

"You've spent thirty years being the only guy in the room who noticed the thing everybody with more rank than you decided wasn't worth noticing," O'Malley said. "Right about the part that mattered, wrong about every channel you were supposed to go through to say so. Nobody ever thanks you for it in the moment. I'd know. I've been wearing faces that outrank you for longer than you've been alive, and every single one of them eventually had to admit you'd clocked it first." A beat, and something almost gentle crept into the words despite how dry the delivery stayed. "She's going to need somebody who reads a room that way. Not somebody who tells her what to do with what she sees. Somebody who just, finally, believes her when she says something's wrong."

He folded the strip closed on that line and stamped something across it in the same unhurried motion, a single word McClane caught only as a smear of red ink, upside down, gone before he could be sure he'd read it right.

CLEARED.

The sentence wasn't finished landing before the room was gone.

*"Wait,"* Gladly said, sounding personally hurt. *"That's the exact same last line Xander's got."*

*"He's got a type,"* Fury said. *"Efficient. I respect it. Doesn't mean I love watching him reuse my own material twice in one chapter."*



Chapter 3: Now Arriving



They nearly turned around at the curb.

Danny had borrowed Alan Barnes's sedan because the truck made a noise above forty-five that he did not trust on the highway, and the sedan chose the moment a valet opened Taylor's door to display an orange CHECK ENGINE light across the dashboard. Danny stared at it. The valet stared politely past it. Taylor stood in the cold wearing a dark green dress bought for Emma's cousin's wedding two years earlier and altered at the waist by a tailor who had asked too many questions about why it fit differently now.

"We could go home," Danny said.

He tried to make it a joke. The invitation in his inside pocket had spent three weeks on the kitchen counter while he invented reasons not to answer it, then another week beneath a magnet on the refrigerator after Taylor told him he should go. He had agreed only after she agreed to come.

"We could," Taylor said.

For one second they both meant it.

Then the valet held out a claim ticket, Danny took it, and the choice closed behind them with the sedan door.

MedHall's entrance rose above the drive in glass and pale stone, its name cut into a steel canopy that reflected the rotating blue light from a decorative fountain. Guests moved through revolving doors in pairs, coats surrendered before the cold had finished leaving them. A photographer waited beyond the vestibule with a wall of sponsor logos. Taylor and Danny stepped around him at the same time without discussing it.

Security occupied the next bottleneck. Two men checked invitations against a printed list while a woman in a MedHall blazer directed handbags through a portable scanner. It was discreet enough to reassure donors and visible enough to remind everyone else whose room they were entering.

Danny gave his name.

The man found it immediately. "Mr. Hebert. Dockworkers Association. Welcome."

Danny's shoulders eased by less than an inch. Taylor saw it because she had spent years learning the movements he thought nobody noticed.

The scanner swallowed her small purse. She felt three half-dormant flies clinging to the underside of a planter near the outer doors, a moth tucked behind the sponsor wall, and nothing else within easy reach. November had stripped the city down to whatever insects could find heat and wait. Her power felt like an enormous empty room with four quiet occupants.

Then the inner doors opened, warm air rolled over her, and hundreds of small lives lit at the edge of her awareness from kitchens, flower arrangements, wall cavities, coat hems, and loading crates.

The room became crowded before she stepped into it.

MedHall did charity galas the way it did everything else, which was to say competently, expensively, and with just enough clinical white in the color scheme that Taylor couldn't decide if the building wanted to look like a hospital or was just embarrassed to admit it already did. Chrome railings. Frosted glass panels lit from behind in a soft blue that was probably supposed to read as calming and instead made everyone's champagne look like it belonged in a specimen cup. A string quartet worked through something she didn't recognize in the atrium, competing politely with the low roar of two hundred conversations she wasn't part of.

The atrium alone could have swallowed her school's entire gymnasium and had room left over for the parking lot, three full stories of glass curtain wall rising up toward a ceiling strung with lights arranged to look, from below, like a slow-motion snowfall that never quite finished landing. A grand staircase swept up one side of the room toward a mezzanine where a second, smaller party seemed to be happening entirely for its own sake, donors important enough to merit their own altitude. Somewhere overhead, a chandelier the size of a small car hung suspended over the dance floor, and Taylor found herself doing the math on it before she'd even meant to, tonnage and cable tension and how many people would need to be under it for that to matter, old habits reaching for new numbers.

She'd counted the exits before she'd finished her first lap of the room. Three that mattered, two fire doors flanking the kitchen and the main entrance behind the coat check, and a service corridor she'd clocked mostly by the staff traffic moving in and out of it, not because anyone had told her it was there. She hadn't decided to do that. It was just what her eyes did now, lately, without asking her first.

"You're doing the thing," Danny said, low, not looking at her directly, the way he did most things that were actually about her.

*"Dammit, I said that was my thing!"* Fury said. *"I think I need to start making sure my fly's up. Everybody's walking off with my material tonight."*

"What thing."

"The thing where you case a room like you're planning to rob it." He said it fondly, which somehow made it worse. "Nobody else here's doing that. Look around. Everybody else is just standing here being rich at each other."

He wasn't wrong. The gala's actual guests moved through the space unhurried, at ease in a way that came from never once in their lives needing to know where the exits were, because nothing bad had ever happened to them in a room this nice. Taylor recognized maybe a third of the faces from the newspaper, city council members and hospital board chairs and men who ran shipping concerns Danny had spent twenty years fighting for a fair contract against, all of them here tonight in the same room, pretending for one evening that they liked each other.

Somewhere behind her a waiter navigated the crowd with a tray of something wrapped in prosciutto, and Taylor caught herself doing the math on him too, out of pure habit, before she remembered he was actually supposed to be there. That was the trouble with a room like this. Everyone in it looked, at a glance, like they belonged, which meant belonging stopped being useful information the second you actually needed it.

"I'm allowed to be nervous," she said. "This isn't really my crowd."

"It's not mine either." Danny adjusted a cuff that didn't need adjusting, a tell she'd learned to read years before she'd learned to read anything else about him. "I keep waiting for somebody to check the invitation and realize there's been a mistake."

*"There it is,"* said a dry voice from the gallery. *"The 'surely they'll realize I don't belong here' beat. Every gala movie has one. I'd set my watch by it, if I had a wrist."*

Danny Hebert belonged here more than he'd ever admit to himself. The Dockworkers Union carried real weight in this city, whatever he privately thought about his own standing, and the invitation sitting in his coat pocket hadn't been a clerical error. He just wasn't built to believe that about himself, not out loud, not even to his own daughter at a party neither of them particularly wanted to be at.

"Danny! Glad you could make it."

Max Anders arrived the way he did everything, smoothly, at exactly the right moment to be noticed without looking like he'd been working for the effect. Good suit, better haircut, a handshake that lasted precisely as long as a handshake was supposed to and not one second more. He had a politician's gift for remembering names, warm and practiced, the kind of attention that made you feel, briefly, like the only person in the room, right up until he moved on to make the next person feel exactly the same way.

"Mr. Anders." Danny's grip was firm, a little stiff, a workingman's handshake in a room full of men who'd never worked with their hands a day in their lives.

"Max, please. And this must be your daughter." Anders turned the warmth on Taylor without missing a beat, easy, practiced, entirely unthreatening. "Welcome. I hope Danny's told you what a difference the Union's made keeping this city's docks running, because half this room wouldn't have a supply chain to complain about without him."

Taylor said something polite. She didn't remember what, later, because it didn't matter, because Anders had already moved on to the next handshake, the next name recalled from some internal ledger, the whole performance so smooth it barely registered as one.

*"Give it a rest,"* said someone else, anonymous, bored. *"Sometimes a rich guy's just a rich guy."*

*"Sometimes,"* the first voice agreed, entirely unconvinced.

Taylor drifted toward the edge of the room once Anders had moved on, the way she always did at things like this, close enough to Danny that leaving wouldn't look like leaving, far enough that she could watch the room instead of being watched by it. Old habit. Older than the bugs, even, older than any of it. She'd been doing this at school for years before her power ever gave her a better reason to.

The councilwoman had a laugh like a hinge that needed oiling, sharp and a little too frequent, deployed at points in the conversation that didn't obviously call for it. Taylor filed that away too, the way she filed everything, without deciding to, without quite knowing yet what any of it would end up being for.

She was three sips into a ginger ale she didn't want, half-listening to the woman explain something about zoning to a man who clearly didn't care, when the voice arrived.

It didn't knock. It didn't ease in at the edges the way her own thoughts usually did, drifting up from somewhere she couldn't always name the source of. It just arrived, mid-thought, complete and unmistakably not hers, disoriented and sharp all at once, like someone had shoved a stranger through a door into the middle of a conversation they hadn't been part of thirty seconds ago.

For one full second, the whole room tilted. Not physically, nothing anyone standing near her would have seen, but everywhere at once inside her own skull, a vertigo with no direction to fall in, two sets of instincts trying to parse the same room through the same eyes at the same time and mostly just tripping over each other in the doorway. The string quartet's melody smeared, briefly, into something almost unrecognizable, familiar notes arriving a half-beat out of step with themselves, and the whole bright, glittering atrium seemed, for that one second, very far away and much too close all at once.

*Okay. Okay, this is new.*

Taylor's glass didn't quite make it back to her mouth. She set it down instead, carefully, both hands, over-controlled in a way she hoped read as manners and not as a girl trying very hard not to look like she'd just heard a man's voice inside her own skull at a party full of Brockton Bay's most respectable liars.

*Who,* she thought, as loud and as flat as she could make a thought, *are you.*

*Lady, I was gonna ask you the same thing.* A beat, and she could feel him taking inventory the way she took inventory, except faster, harder, a lifetime of habit doing in half a second what had taken her months to learn to do at all. *You're, what, sixteen? Fifteen? And I'm apparently riding shotgun in your head at a party. This somebody's idea of a joke, or should I be more worried than I already am?*

*I don't know what's happening.* Her heart was going hard enough that she was half convinced the councilwoman would hear it over the string quartet. *I don't know who you are. I don't know how you're in my* head*, I don't*

*Hey. Hey, slow down.* Something in the voice shifted, dropped half a register, the same tone she imagined a cop used on someone standing too close to a ledge. *Panicking's fine. Panicking's normal. Just do it quiet, because we are apparently standing in the middle of two hundred people who are gonna notice if you start talking to yourself out loud.*

She hadn't realized her lips had moved until he said it. She pressed them together, hard, and forced her eyes back onto the councilwoman's face, nodding at something about parking variances she hadn't heard a word of.

*Good. That's good. Keep doing that.* A pause, and she felt him doing something she didn't have a name for yet, some quick, practiced survey of the room through her own eyes, cataloging in a rhythm that didn't match hers at all, faster, blunter, built for a different kind of danger than the one she'd trained herself to watch for. *Okay. Talk to me. Where are we, what is this, and why do half these people look like they'd rather be anywhere else.*

*It's a charity gala.* She kept her voice down to something that barely qualified as a whisper, angled toward her ginger ale like she was talking to the ice. *My dad's here. I'm here because he's here.*

*Somebody make you come, or you volunteer for this?*

*Neither. It's complicated.*

*You got a name, or should I just keep calling you 'kid' in my head, because I gotta tell you, that's gonna get old for both of us fast.*

*Taylor.* She hesitated, half a second, the reflex of a girl who'd learned the hard way not to hand things out for free, even a name, even to a voice she had no way to verify wasn't some new, worse kind of trouble. *You?*

*McClane. John McClane.* Something dry crept into the words, a weary humor that had clearly gotten plenty of practice, a man who'd introduced himself under stranger circumstances than this one and lived to make a joke about it. *NYPD. Or I was, about ten minutes ago, before somebody decided to reroute my whole evening without asking.*

*A cop.* Taylor felt something in her chest tighten, an old, familiar reflex, the one that had nothing to do with tonight and everything to do with every conversation she'd never had with an adult who might have actually listened. *Great. That's great. That's exactly what I need right now.*

She closed her right hand.

It closed.

She opened it, lifted one finger, then another. McClane followed the movements without causing them. She could feel his attention settle on her hand, but no pressure joined hers, no second set of muscles competing beneath the skin.

*What are you doing?*

*Checking whether this is still my body.*

The answer silenced him for half a beat.

*It is,* he said. *Guy who sent me made that very clear. I don't get a vote on your hands unless you hand me one, and you can take it back whenever you want.*

*The guy who sent you.*

*Long story. Bad coffee. Weird paperwork. We can do it after we're not pretending to listen to a zoning speech.*

Taylor moved her swarm. Every insect within range shifted toward the outside walls at once.

McClane reacted like the floor had dropped beneath him.

*What the hell was that?*

She stopped the movement. *What was what?*

*The room got bigger. No, not the room. You. There were... points. Hundreds of them.*

His confusion was real. Taylor felt it without wanting to, accompanied by an involuntary flash of another place: concrete, a moving belt, blood warm beneath a torn shirt, seven airplanes turning circles somewhere overhead. The impression vanished before she could separate memory from sensation.

McClane felt her catch it.

They both went quiet.

*Bugs,* Taylor said finally. *I control bugs.*

*Of course you do.*

*What does that mean?*

*Means I finally get dropped into a teenager's head, and she's got the one superpower guaranteed to make every ventilation duct in the city feel personal.*

Despite herself, a short breath escaped Taylor that might have become a laugh under better circumstances. The councilwoman glanced toward her. Taylor covered it by lifting the ginger ale.

*You believe me,* she thought.

*Kid, I'm currently deadheading behind your eyes while you feel a moth on the other side of the room. Tonight's way past my usual standard of proof.*

*Relax. I'm not here to write anybody a ticket.* A pause, and she felt him settle, some part of him going quiet and focused, nothing to do with calm and everything to do with a man deciding, deliberately, to stop panicking because panicking wasn't going to fix anything. *I don't know what you are, or what just happened to either of us, and near as I can tell we're both stuck figuring that out together, whether we like it or not. So how about this. You keep smiling at Councilwoman Whoever, and I'll do what I'm actually good at.*

*Which is?*

*You're not gonna believe me if I just say it.*

*Try me. Tonight's already been weirder than most of my week.*

*Fair.* Something that might have been the ghost of a laugh. *Reading a room.* Something in the words sharpened, and for just a second, underneath the confusion and the dry humor, she felt the edge of something that might have been useful, might have been dangerous, might, she'd learn later, have been both at once. *Because I gotta tell you, kid, thirty seconds in, and I already don't like something about the guy who just walked off with your dad's hand still half-shook.*

Taylor's stomach dropped, quiet and cold, underneath a smile she didn't remember choosing to hold onto.

*What guy.*

*"And there it is, ladies and gentlemen! The villain is clocked!"* the gallery said, delighted.

*"Yeah, we should have Clockblocker here,"* Gladly said.

*"Shut up, you,"* Fury said.



Chapter 4: Baggage Check



*Your host.* McClane's attention had gone somewhere past her, tracking a man in a good suit working his way toward the bar with the easy, unhurried confidence of somebody who owned the room and had never once had to raise his voice to prove it. *Anders, you said his name was.*

*Max Anders.* Taylor kept her eyes on the councilwoman, nodding at a sentence she hadn't heard the end of. *He's, like, actually important. He funds half this hospital's outreach program. My dad thinks he's too slick too.*

*Yeah, I bet he does.* Something in the voice had gone flat, professional, all the dry humor from a minute ago folded away someplace McClane clearly kept for exactly this purpose. *Watch him for me. Not the smile. The eyes, when nobody's looking at them.*

She didn't want to. Wanting to was almost worse than actually doing it, some traitor part of her already turning to find Max Anders in the crowd before she'd finished deciding whether a total stranger riding around in her skull deserved that kind of trust.

*You don't have to believe a word I say,* McClane added, like he'd caught the hesitation without her needing to say it out loud. *Just watch. Costs you nothing to look at a guy for five seconds.*

*And if I don't want to.*

*Then you don't, and we sit here drinking bad soda until your dad's ready to go home, and I never bring it up again.* No pressure in it, which was somehow worse than pressure would have been. *Your call, kid. Always gonna be your call.*

She looked anyway.

McClane did not tell her what to find. That surprised her. He asked her to pick three people who belonged in the room and watch how they moved through it first.

Taylor chose a waiter, the woman at the silent-auction table, and Danny.

The waiter looked toward faces and empty glasses. His route changed whenever a hand rose or a tray came back full. The auction volunteer looked toward clipboards, bid sheets, and the clock above the bar. Danny looked toward whoever was speaking to him, then searched for Taylor whenever the conversation broke.

*Baseline,* McClane said. *Everybody looks somewhere. The question is whether they're looking where their job says they should.*

Max Anders's job was to host.

He watched the room like it was something he owned and something he expected to resist ownership. His eyes touched the main doors whenever they opened, the mezzanine whenever someone leaned over its rail, and the service corridor whenever one of the men in dark catering uniforms passed through. He did not stare. He did not need to. Each glance landed once and moved on, another item checked.

A waiter approaching from behind changed direction before reaching him. Anders had not turned or spoken. His left hand had dropped beside his jacket with two fingers extended, the gesture small enough to pass as nothing. The waiter carried his tray toward the east wall instead and stopped beside a man wearing an earpiece.

*Did he just redirect that waiter?* Taylor asked.

*He redirected somebody.* McClane's attention tightened. *Question is whether that guy was ever a waiter.*

The man with the tray did not offer a drink for the next four minutes.

He was refilling a woman's glass near the bar, laughing at something she'd said, head tipped back just enough to sell it. And then, for less than a second, so fast Taylor would have missed it completely if she hadn't been told exactly where to look, his gaze cut sideways toward the service corridor, flat and assessing, the exact same read she gave a room when she was counting exits.

It was gone before she could be sure she'd seen it. He was laughing again, warm, easy, already turning to greet somebody else, a hand finding a passing councilman's shoulder with the same practiced ease he'd used on Danny's, the same warmth turned on and off like a switch nobody else in the room seemed to notice had a switch at all.

He moved through the crowd like water finding the lowest point, never rushed, never idle, always exactly where the next conversation needed him to be a half second before it started. Taylor watched him work a full circuit of the room in the time it took her to finish a single sentence with the councilwoman, touching down with a state senator near the ice sculpture, a hospital administrator by the silent auction table, a woman in red who might have been a judge, each conversation lasting exactly as long as it needed to and not a second longer, a hundred small transactions of warmth handed out like currency he never seemed to run low on. Taylor had watched people work rooms before, teachers doing it badly at school assemblies, her own father doing it worse, stiff and earnest and visibly uncomfortable the whole time. She'd never watched anyone do it like this, effortless in a way that made effortless look like the wrong word for it, too easy to be natural and too natural to be only practice.

The route brought him past every public entrance without ever appearing to inspect one. At the main doors, he greeted a late-arriving couple while his gaze checked the coat room behind them. Near the staircase, he stopped to admire a donor's necklace while taking in the mezzanine landing over her shoulder. At the kitchen doors, he bent to retrieve a napkin before a server could reach it and came upright facing the service corridor.

Each act was gracious. Together they formed a patrol.

Taylor felt the pattern settle into her awareness beside the map of insects warming in the walls. She had spent years noticing danger by watching for the person who looked directly at her. McClane noticed the person who had arranged never to need to.

*There.* McClane's voice had an edge to it now, the satisfaction of a man who'd just watched a hunch confirm itself. *You catch that?*

*Catch what? He looked at a hallway.*

*"'Catch what,' she says,"* the gallery said, delighted. *"Get a load of this girl!"*

*He looked at a hallway the way a cop looks at a hallway. Or a guy who used to run something that needed exits covered.* A pause, and she felt him doing the thing again, that fast, blunt cataloging that didn't match her own instincts at all, sharper in some places, cruder in others, built for a completely different kind of danger than the one she'd trained herself to watch for. *Guy like that, hosting his own party, in his own building? He doesn't check exits. He's got people for that. Unless he doesn't trust the people, or the exits are the kind of thing he never learned to stop checking himself.*

*"Or he just doesn't like crowds,"* said the same bored, anonymous voice from earlier, unconvinced as ever. *"Some people check exits because a fire actually happened to them once. Not everybody's secretly evil, you know."*

*"Statistically," the first voice allowed, "you're not wrong. Dramatically, you're about to be very wrong."*

*This is insane.* Taylor kept her voice down to something that barely qualified as breath, angled at her ginger ale like she'd developed a sudden fascination with ice cubes. *You've been here fifteen minutes. You looked at a guy for one second. You don't know anything about him.*

*I know enough.* No heat in it, just certainty, flat and immovable, something that made her chest go tight for reasons she didn't fully understand yet. *I've spent thirty years reading rooms full of people who didn't want to be read. You get good at it or you get dead. That guy's running something under all this charity-gala varnish, and I'd bet my pension, if I've still got one, that not one person in this building's clocked it, because he's good, and because nobody here's actually looking.*

*"I told you we needed Clockblocker here,"* Gladly said.

*People don't just know things like that. Not from a glance.*

*Sure they do. Ask any beat cop who's still alive to complain about it. You spend enough years watching people lie to your face, you stop needing the whole sentence. You just need the first word and the way somebody's shoulders move while they say it.* A beat, and something almost self-deprecating crept into the certainty. *Doesn't mean I'm right. Means I've been right often enough that I stopped being able to afford ignoring the feeling.*

*You don't know that.*

*Kid, I watched him check a hallway like a man clearing a room, and then I watched two hundred people keep right on drinking champagne like nothing happened.* Something almost gentle crept into the flatness, underneath the certainty, the patience of a man who'd had this exact argument before, with other people, in other rooms, and lost most of them. *That's not a guess. That's just what I saw. You can decide what to do with it. I'm not gonna make you believe me.*

Taylor didn't answer right away. Across the room, Max Anders had circled back toward Danny, one hand landing warm and easy on her father's shoulder, saying something that made Danny laugh, actually laugh, the forced one, not the one he saved for people he liked.

*"Everyone in this city got issued blinders at birth,"* the gallery muttered, and the phrasing landed hard enough that Taylor nearly missed McClane saying almost the same thing a half-beat later.

*"Bleedthrough,"* Gladly said, alarmed. *"She's not supposed to hear us!"*

*Everybody in this town's got the same pair of blinders on,* McClane said, watching Anders work the room with the weary contempt of a man who'd met a hundred versions of him under a hundred different names. *And I gotta be honest with you, kid, that scares me a lot more than the guy does.*

*"Nobody's writing anything down,"* Fury said. *"This isn't a seminar."*

*"It could be. I'd attend."*

Taylor watched her father laugh again. The same forced laugh as the first time. And felt something small and cold settle into her stomach that had nothing to do with the ginger ale.

*So what do we do.*

*Nothing. Not yet.* McClane's certainty had a shape to it now, something almost like a plan, or the outline of one. *You don't move on a read this thin. Not in a room full of his own people, at his own party, with your dad standing three feet from him. You watch. You let me watch, through you, since apparently that's how this works now. And if I'm wrong, you never have to think about any of this again, and we can both go back to whatever we were doing before some jackass decided to reroute my whole night.*

*And if you're right?*

*Then we've got a problem a lot bigger than one bad hallway glance, and we're gonna need a better plan than standing here drinking a soda you don't even like.*

*What kind of plan.*

*Don't know yet. Haven't earned one.* He said it simply, no frustration in it, the flat patience of a man who'd built enough plans from nothing to know better than to rush the part where you actually had something to build with. *Right now all we've got is a feeling and a hallway. You don't build a plan on a feeling and a hallway. You build a plan on facts, and facts take time, and time's the one thing neither of us gets to rush without paying for it later.*

*That's not much of a pep talk.*

*Wasn't trying to give you one. Trying to give you the truth instead. Figured you'd rather have that.*

She would, and hated a little that he'd guessed it correctly on the first try.

Taylor set the ginger ale down on a passing tray, mostly full, and didn't reach for another one.

*You feel that?* McClane asked, and there was something almost gentle underneath the flatness, the tone of a man checking on a rookie after their first real scene. *Setting the drink down. That's not nothing. That's a decision.*

*It's a drink.*

*It's never just a drink.* No lecture in it, just an observation, offered plainly. *You put it down because some part of you already knows you're not going back to being a girl who drinks ginger ale and watches a string quartet tonight. Whatever's coming, you already agreed to it. Your body just beat your mouth to the announcement.*

Taylor didn't have an answer for that, mostly because she suspected he was right, and admitting it out loud felt like signing something she hadn't finished reading yet.

Across the room, Max Anders excused himself from Danny with a warm word and a hand on the shoulder, and turned, unhurried, toward the service corridor Taylor had clocked on her very first lap of the room, the one she was fairly sure, now, he'd checked without meaning to be seen doing it.

*"Dammit,"* Fury said. *"What a time to be missing a character."*

She found Danny with her eyes before she let herself follow Anders any further, an old reflex now on top of an old reflex, and found him fine, laughing at something a hospital board member had said, three feet and a whole world away from whatever his daughter had just quietly agreed to.

Somewhere above them both, the chandelier over the dance floor caught the light and threw it back down across two hundred unbothered faces, and Taylor found herself doing that math again, tonnage and cable tension, without quite knowing yet why some part of her had already started filing it away as useful.

She watched Anders go, and for the first time all night, the watching wasn't just habit.



Chapter 5: Diverted



*Okay,* McClane said, once Anders had disappeared past the frosted glass doors at the end of the service corridor. *Small test. Nothing that gets you noticed. You said you can send bugs places without anybody clocking it, that still true at a party like this?*

*It's true anywhere.* Taylor kept her back to the corridor, eyes on the room, working a slow circuit toward the coat check the way a person did when they were bored, not hunting. *Bringing them in is the hard part. This place has been swept for pests. Probably twice, given who's paying for the catering.*

*Then don't bring the whole army. Just enough to listen.*

She found three that had ridden in on somebody's coat, half-frozen and sluggish from the cold outside, and walked them the long way around the room, along the baseboard, behind a curtain, under the gap beneath the service door. It took longer than she wanted it to. Cold insects moved like cold insects, no matter how badly she wanted them to move like something else.

*You could warm them up faster,* McClane offered, watching her work through the effort of it. *Right? You said something about that earlier.*

*Not without moving them somewhere warm first, and the only warm places near that baseboard are people's ankles. I'm not doing that tonight. Not for a test run.*

*Fair. Didn't mean to backseat drive your own power.*

*You kind of are, though.*

*Yeah,* he admitted, and there was something almost rueful in it. *Occupational hazard. I'll work on it.*

*"This is the least cinematic surveillance sequence this reviewer has ever witnessed,"* the gallery observed, unimpressed. *"No suction cup on a window. No guy in a van with headphones. Just three sad, chilly bugs."*

*"I know who you are, Gib,"* Fury said. *"But now's not the time."*

*"Give her a break,"* said another voice, newer, warmer, overeager in the exact way of a substitute teacher trying to get the whole class excited about a pop quiz. *"This is great tradecraft, actually! Working with what you have! I'd give this a solid B-plus!"*

*"Nobody asked you to grade it, Gladly."*

*"I grade things unprompted. It's part of my whole thing."*

The bugs made it as far as the gap under the door before the trail went cold, literally, a draft of outside air rolling under the service corridor's far end that told her, more than anything she could actually hear, that the door at the other end was open to the loading dock. Voices, muffled, two of them, one she was fairly sure was Anders, the other lower, harder to place. No words she could make out. Just cadence, back and forth, unhurried.

Taylor pushed one fly farther into the gap and built the corridor from fragments. Painted cinderblock held the day's warmth. A rubber mat ran down the center. Air moved steadily from the loading dock, carrying diesel exhaust, wet pavement, and the sour remains of produce boxes broken down after the kitchen delivery.

Two pairs of shoes stood beyond the turn. Anders wore polished black oxfords. Taylor recognized them because she had watched them cross the atrium for half an hour. The second pair were heavy, square-toed, planted shoulder-width apart and never shifting through the whole conversation.

Something passed between them. Paper brushed cloth. A metal clip clicked shut.

The heavy shoes moved away toward the loading dock. Anders remained for three seconds, no more, then returned toward the party.

Taylor pulled the fly behind a pipe before the frosted door opened. From the atrium, she watched Anders emerge alone, adjusting one cuff. Nobody greeted him. Nobody appeared to notice he had been gone.

*Exchange,* McClane said.

*You don't know that.*

*No. I know paper moved from one man to another where the cameras couldn't see it. Exchange is the short word until we learn the long one.*

*That's it. That's all I've got.*

*It's something.* McClane didn't sound satisfied, exactly, but there was an edge of something like approval underneath the disappointment. *Loading dock conversation, private, away from his own party. Guy hosting two hundred people doesn't step out to take a call unless the call matters more than the party does.*

*Or he stepped out for a cigarette.*

*Anders doesn't smoke. You'd have smelled it on him already, standing that close all night.*

That was true, and Taylor hated that it was true, hated that a stranger she'd known for less than an hour had already built a more detailed profile of Max Anders than she had after years of watching him shake her father's hand at every Union function that mattered.

*So what do we do with "something."*

*We find someone who'll actually listen to it.*

That was where it fell apart.

She tried the closest thing MedHall had to security, a broad-shouldered man in an earpiece stationed near the coat check, wide as a doorway and twice as unmovable, and got exactly as far as "I think there's something weird happening near the loading dock" before his expression did the practiced thing adult faces did when a teenager said something they'd already decided not to take seriously. He told her, kindly, that the loading dock was for deliveries, that Mr. Anders sometimes stepped out to take calls his event planner would rather he didn't take at all, and that she should go enjoy the dessert table, there was a chocolate fountain, had she seen it.

"Someone met him there," Taylor said.

The guard's smile remained in place. His eyes changed, not alarmed, just more attentive to her than before. "A lot of people meet Mr. Anders. He's hosting the event."

"They passed something."

"Did you see what?"

"No."

"Did you see either person carrying a weapon?"

"No."

"Did you hear a threat? An argument? Someone asking for help?"

Each no made the next question easier for him and harder for her.

The guard touched two fingers to his earpiece, listened, and glanced toward the service doors. "Loading supervisor says Mr. Anders approved a late floral invoice. Paperwork, okay? Nothing dangerous."

*That was fast,* McClane said.

Taylor looked at the guard. "You checked?"

"That's what we're here for." His smile warmed, reassured by his own answer. "Everything's fine."

McClane's attention stayed on the earpiece. *He didn't call anybody. Somebody was already listening and fed him the explanation.*

*Security listens to security.*

*Then why'd they need an explanation ready for a conversation nobody expected you to have?*

Taylor stepped away before the guard could ask her name.

She almost tried again with a different word for it, something that might land better, weird replaced with suspicious, suspicious replaced with something more specific, and gave up somewhere in the middle of composing the sentence, because she could already hear how it would go. There wasn't a version of "a rich man looked at a hallway funny" that sounded like anything but a bored teenager inventing a mystery to feel important at her father's work party.

She thought, briefly and uselessly, about all the other adults in that atrium who might listen if she framed it right, the state senator, the hospital administrators, the woman who might have been a judge, two hundred people with badges and titles and the kind of institutional weight that could actually move something, and understood, with a clarity that tasted like defeat, that every single one of them would hear the exact same thing the guard had: a nervous kid, a rich man, a hallway. Money didn't just buy charm. It bought the benefit of the doubt from a whole room at once, and Max Anders had apparently been buying it for years.

She tried once more anyway.

A hospital administrator stood near the auction table, silver name badge identifying her as Marianne Holt, Vice President of Community Relations. Taylor waited until the woman finished speaking to a donor and delivered the account without saying bugs, voices, or the man inside her head. Mr. Anders had met someone at the loading dock. Something had changed hands. Security had supplied an explanation before checking with her.

Holt listened. That almost made it worse.

"Max handles half a dozen private pledges at every event," she said when Taylor finished. "Some donors don't want their names announced. Some vendors need signatures. I appreciate you paying attention, but discretion can look strange from the outside when you don't have the context."

"What context would make security know I was asking before they called anyone?"

Holt's expression tightened by a fraction. "I'm sure they followed procedure. Now, are you here with someone?"

There it was. Not what did you see. Who is responsible for you.

Taylor pointed toward Danny before the woman could summon him by name.

"Then I think you should enjoy the evening with your father," Holt said. "These events can be overwhelming the first time."

Taylor thanked her because failing to thank her would become the only part of the conversation anyone remembered.

*"To be fair,"* Gladly offered, *"'weird stuff by the loading dock' is not, strictly, a police report."*

*"You're supposed to be on our side, Gladly."*

*"I contain multitudes."*

McClane's frustration came through clean and sharp, no dry humor left in it at all. *That's the whole problem with a read like this. It's real. I'd stake my badge on it, if I still had one that meant anything tonight. But it's not evidence, it's not a crime in progress, and nobody in this building's gonna move on a hunch from a fifteen-year-old and a voice in her own head that she can't exactly cite as a source.*

*So we just watch. Same as before.*

*Same as before.* A beat, and something in him settled, not calm, exactly, but controlled, a man deciding, deliberately, that a closed door was information too. *We just watch harder.*

She was three steps back toward the main room, already turning the problem over, already half-formed on some idea about the caterers, about whether the loading dock had a window she could put a bug through instead of under, when a hand closed gently around her elbow.

"Hey." Danny's voice, low, not sharp, but with an edge underneath the gentleness that told her he'd been watching her for longer than she'd realized. "Where are you headed?"

"Just looking around."

"You've been looking around by that service door for five minutes." He wasn't angry. That was almost worse. He had the look he got when he was worried about her in a way he didn't know how to say out loud, the same look from every parent-teacher conference where somebody had used the word "isolated" like it was a diagnosis. "You feeling okay? You want to head out early?"

"I'm fine, Dad."

"You don't look fine. You look like you're about to go do something." He said it lightly, almost like a joke, but his grip on her elbow didn't loosen, and his eyes had gone to the same service corridor she'd been circling for the better part of ten minutes, not with suspicion exactly, just a father's blunt, uncomplicated instinct that something near that door was pulling his daughter toward it, and that whatever it was, he didn't like it.

"Do what, exactly?"

"I don't know. That's what worries me." He almost smiled, the tired, fond kind he saved for moments when he knew he was being overprotective and had decided not to care. "You've had this look since we got here. Like you're waiting for something to go wrong so you can finally relax."

It was, Taylor thought, an uncomfortably accurate description of most of her life, and she hated that he'd noticed it standing three feet away from a man he genuinely believed was one of the good ones.

"I just wanted some air."

"There's a balcony for that. Nice one, too. I saw it on the way in." He steered her, gently, unmistakably, back toward the main room, back toward the string quartet and the chocolate fountain and two hundred people who had no idea anything in this building was wrong. "Come on. Stay where I can see you for a while, huh? Humor your old man."

*"And there's the wall's other half,"* the gallery said, quiet now. *"Nobody has to believe you're wrong. Somebody just has to love you enough to stop you before you find out."*

Taylor let herself be steered. There wasn't a version of this where she explained it to him, not here, not now, maybe not ever, and the unfairness of that, of watching her own father's protective instincts work perfectly against the exact thing they were supposed to protect him from, sat in her chest like something swallowed wrong.

*He's not wrong to stop you,* McClane said, quieter than she'd heard him yet. *For what it's worth. He doesn't know what he's stopping. Doesn't make the instinct bad.*

*Doesn't make it right either.*

*No,* McClane agreed. *It doesn't. Welcome to the job, kid. Half of it's exactly this.*

She let Danny walk her back into the light and the music and the small talk, and didn't look at the service corridor again, though she could feel, distantly, three cold, sluggish bugs still sitting under a door that had already told her everything it was going to tonight.

*For what it's worth,* McClane said, once Danny had drifted off to refill his own drink, satisfied she was staying put, *you handled that well. Didn't argue. Didn't push. Just let him think he'd won.*

*He did win.*

*Tonight, sure.* Something almost like a smile in the words, tired and a little grim. *Tomorrow's a different question. But tonight, letting him win was the smart play, and you found it without me telling you to. That's worth something.*

She didn't answer, mostly because she wasn't sure yet whether being told she was good at lying to her own father was a compliment or something closer to a warning.



Chapter 6: Squawk
