The rest of the evening went the way evenings like this always went for Taylor, slow and bright and loud in a way that had nothing to do with her, except now there was a second person trapped inside all of it with her, restless in a way she was starting to recognize as its own kind of language.

*You're doing the fake-listening thing again,* McClane observed, somewhere around the councilwoman's third circuit back to the zoning variance.

*It's a skill.*

*It's a good skill. Wasted on her, though. She's not gonna say anything worth hearing.* A pause, and she felt him doing the thing he did, the constant background scan, cataloging exits and angles and the rhythm of two hundred people none of whom knew anything was wrong. *You said the bugs were slow tonight. Cold.*

*They warm up. Give them time.*

*How much time.*

*Numbers aren't really the problem. Not anymore. I multitask that like breathing at this point.* She kept her voice down to something that barely qualified as breath, angled at a fresh ginger ale she had even less intention of drinking than the last one. *They just need to be awake enough to pay attention. Cold ones are slow. Give them time to warm up and they're fine.*

*Then start warming them up. We don't have all night, but we've got enough.*

She started anyway, and that was the problem, because the itch of it, the pull toward doing more, trying harder, finding some clean and clever use for a power she still barely trusted, felt uncomfortably good in a way she wasn't sure she was allowed to enjoy.

Doing it while remaining Danny Hebert's daughter at a charity gala turned out to be harder than doing it alone.

Danny introduced her to a city planner who remembered the ferry terminals before the storm of 1998. Taylor shook his hand while walking a beetle along a heating duct. A woman from the hospital board asked where she went to school. Taylor said Winslow without watching the woman's expression and used three flies to map the kitchen doors behind her. Someone placed a fresh ginger ale in Taylor's hand before she had finished abandoning the first one.

Every conversation demanded her face while the building demanded everything behind it.

McClane began calling turns like a driving instructor.

*Smile. Not that much. Guy on your left's about to ask a question. Pull the one under the radiator back, somebody's moving a cart. Good. Now ask your dad something so you look like you're participating.*

*You said you weren't going to backseat-drive my power.*

*This is backseat-driving your cover. Different department.*

Taylor asked Danny whether the hospital board chair had always talked with both hands. Danny nearly choked on his drink trying not to laugh. The city planner launched into a story about a budget meeting. Nobody noticed the first six bugs reach the service level.

The work began acquiring rhythm. Taylor did not send a continuous stream toward Anders. Continuous movement made a trail. She moved one insect, waited, changed level, sent another along a route with no visible relationship to the first. McClane had her break every pattern after three repetitions even when the pattern appeared to be working.

*Anybody can follow success,* he said. *Professionals follow habits.*

*Are you calling him a professional?*

*I'm calling him alive and in charge. You don't get both for long by being careless.*

It took the better part of an hour, working slow, working careful, never more than she could hold without her hands starting to shake, before she had a real net out. Not just the three cold, sluggish stragglers from before. A dozen, then two, drawn in through gaps under doors and along window seals, arranged in a long, thin loop that traced the edges of the room the way she'd trace it herself if she could walk the perimeter without anyone noticing.

The kitchen supplied most of them. Fruit flies lifted from the bins beneath the prep tables. Roaches emerged from the warmth behind the commercial ovens. Moths woke in the dry-storage room where sacks of flour held summer heat long after summer had ended. Taylor routed them away from food and exposed counters, unwilling to create the health violation everyone would reasonably blame on the only bug controller in the city if her identity ever came out.

The exterior ring remained thin. November had killed or buried most of what lived outside. She used spiders tucked into window corners and beetles sheltering beneath the loading ramp, creatures slow with cold but already positioned where she needed eyes.

For the first time since her power arrived, scarcity made each insect feel valuable.

She built it in layers, the way McClane described building a perimeter around a building he actually cared about: an outer ring along the exterior walls and window ledges, a middle ring threading through the service corridors and staff-only doors, and an inner ring, thin and careful, drifting the edges of the atrium itself, close enough to the crowd to catch a raised voice, far enough back that nobody's champagne glass was in any danger of gaining an uninvited passenger. Three rings, dozens of small, scattered bodies, all of it held together by a single thread of attention she was somehow, impossibly, managing not to drop.

The bugs themselves weren't the hard part. The routing was, a hundred small decisions instead of one big one, where to send a given straggler so it wouldn't cross a bright patch of floor, which gap under which door was actually wide enough to matter, all of it new in a way raw numbers never had been. She'd never had a reason to think this carefully about the path, only the destination.

*You're doing the tactical thing now,* McClane said, something like approval under the observation. *Not just moving bugs. Planning routes. That's new for you, isn't it.*

*Never had anything worth planning a route for before.*

*Well, get used to it. It's a good habit. Wish somebody'd made me learn it slower, instead of the way I actually learned it.*

*That's better,* McClane said, and there was something almost like approval in it, warm underneath the flatness. *That's a real perimeter. You built that in an hour, on your first real try, at a party, without anybody clocking it. That's not nothing, kid.*

*"There's no music,"* said the newer voice, the warm, overeager one Taylor still didn't have a name for. *"This is prose. There's no soundtrack department."*

*"There's always a soundtrack department. You just have to imagine it hard enough."*

Taylor almost smiled, and caught herself before it showed, because a girl standing alone near a dessert table with an unreadable little smile was exactly the kind of thing that got noticed at a party like this, and getting noticed was the one thing McClane had been very clear she couldn't afford tonight.

It was the smile, or something like it, that she was still fighting down when the first bug died.

It wasn't dramatic. It never was, with something this small, just a single thread of awareness winking out along the loop she'd built, gone the way a light went out, no drama to it at all. She almost missed it entirely.

The fly had been clinging beneath a conduit eight feet above the loading corridor. No shoe could have reached it. No closing door had crossed its position. One instant it felt vibration through painted metal; the next, nothing.

*What was that.*

*One of the bugs. Near the loading dock corridor.* She was already reaching for the next one in the loop, sending it wide around the spot where the last one had died, cautious now in a way she hadn't been a second ago. *Could be nothing. A door closing wrong. Somebody's shoe.*

*Could be somebody found it and didn't like finding it.*

The second one died four minutes later, closer to the main hall this time, near enough to the loading dock corridor's mouth that the pattern was hard to argue with. Not swatted. Not stepped on by accident. Something colder than that, something that felt, in the brief half second before the awareness winked out, like being noticed back.

Taylor replaced it from the opposite direction.

That bug survived twenty-two seconds.

Before it died, she felt a narrow object touch the baseboard and move along it at a measured pace. Not a broom. Too small. Too controlled. The object stopped beneath the bug's hiding place, rose, and ended the signal.

Someone was probing every seam where an insect might conceal itself.

Not killing pests. Testing routes.

A third followed less than a minute after, and then a fourth, both from the middle ring this time, both near enough to each other that Taylor could feel the shape of a hand sweeping methodically along a stretch of hallway, patient, thorough, the work of someone who wasn't hunting randomly, but clearing a grid one square at a time. She pulled the rest of that ring back fast, hands going cold around her ginger ale glass, her own heartbeat suddenly loud enough to hear over the quartet, fear doing what four dead bugs alone hadn't quite managed.

*"There it is,"* the gallery said, all lightness gone. *"That's the turn. Somebody just started watching the watcher."*

*Anders,* McClane said, and there was nothing uncertain in it at all, the flat, immovable certainty of a man who'd spent thirty years learning exactly when certainty mattered, and hearing it settled something cold and low in Taylor's stomach.

*You don't know that.*

*I know somebody just killed two bugs with surgical precision, near the exact hallway where our guy stepped out for a private conversation an hour ago, in a pattern too clean to be an accident.* His voice had gone flat, professional, every trace of the earlier warmth folded away. *That's not a coincidence read, kid. That's somebody who's dealt with exactly this kind of surveillance before, and knew exactly what he was looking at the second he found it.*

*So he knows. About the bugs. About me.*

*He knows something's watching him.* A beat, careful, deliberate, like a man choosing each word before he spent it. *He doesn't know it's you. Not yet. Could be anybody in this building, as far as he knows. Could be a rival. Could be a fed. Could be a ghost story he's telling himself because he's paranoid and tonight happened to give him a reason.*

*But he's looking now.*

*He's looking now.*

Taylor let the rest of the loop dissolve, called every remaining bug back the long way, slow, careful, nothing patterned enough to read as anything but random insect drift if anyone happened to be watching for it. Across the room, through the crowd, she caught one clean look at Max Anders, back from wherever he'd gone, glass in hand, laughing at something a city councilman had said.

He wasn't looking at her. He wasn't looking at anyone in particular. But there was something different in the set of his shoulders now, something coiled underneath the easy charm that hadn't been there an hour ago, a man who'd just confirmed something he didn't like and was working very hard not to let it show.

She watched him excuse himself from the councilman a beat too fast, watched his eyes flick once, briefly, toward the loading dock corridor and then away again, deliberate, like a man reminding himself not to look somewhere twice. It was such a small thing. She wouldn't have caught it a week ago. She wasn't sure she liked how easily she caught it now.

*"I don't love this,"* the newer voice said, its eagerness gone. *"I don't love this at all."*

*"Nobody loves this,"* Fury said. *"That's usually when it's about to get good."*

Taylor set the ginger ale down, mostly full, and didn't reach for another one, and somewhere in the back of her mind, distant and steady, she felt McClane doing the same slow, careful math she was, both of them arriving, separately and together, at the exact same uncomfortable number: whatever tonight had been when it started, it wasn't that anymore.



Chapter 7: Holding Pattern



The toast came without warning, which McClane said afterward was exactly the point.

One moment the quartet was halfway through a waltz and waiters were replacing the dinner plates with coffee cups. The next, the musicians stopped on the same beat, the coffee service withdrew, and the lights above the guest tables dimmed while the stage remained bright. The room had been cued without a visible cue.

Taylor caught four people responding before the first guest looked up. The man at the east doors turned his body toward the crowd. The woman near the kitchen shifted closer to the service corridor. Both men on the mezzanine stopped pretending to converse. Max Anders passed his empty glass to a waiter without looking and stepped onto the stage.

*Rehearsed,* McClane said. *Not the speech. The room around it.*

The change kept spreading after Anders reached the stage. A woman at the silent-auction table closed the lid of the cash box and slid it beneath her chair. Two MedHall board members near the front turned their name cards outward, suddenly attentive to where the photographer stood. At the back of the atrium, a young man with a reporter's notebook abandoned a conversation in the middle of a sentence and found a clear line of sight between two floral arrangements.

The guests had not merely become an audience. They had arranged themselves into the version of the audience they expected to appear in tomorrow's photographs.

Danny straightened his tie.

"Do I need to stand somewhere?" Taylor asked.

"No. God, no." He glanced toward the stage, then at the board chair beckoning him closer with two fingers. "Apparently I need to stand somewhere. Stay here?"

Taylor caught his sleeve before he could move. She did not know why until she had already done it.

Danny looked down at her hand, then at her face.

"They can see you fine from here," she said.

It was a weak excuse. He knew it. She knew he knew it. But the board chair had already turned away to make room for a councilwoman, and Danny let the moment pass.

"Fine by me," he said. "I didn't come here to decorate the stage."

He stayed beside her.

*Good instinct,* McClane said.

*I didn't have an instinct. I just didn't want him closer to Anders.*

*That's an instinct. You don't have to like where it came from for it to count.*

Someone dimmed the atrium lights by half, and Max Anders climbed two steps onto the small stage where the string quartet had been working all evening, glass in hand, easy and unhurried in the way of a man who'd given this exact speech, or one close enough to it, more times than he could count. Two hundred conversations folded down into one, all at once, the hush a room full of powerful people made when they'd collectively decided somebody else was worth listening to for a minute, waiters going still along the walls, even the string quartet lowering their bows in practiced unison, and for one long beat the whole vast, glittering atrium turned to face a single small man on a stage no bigger than a dinner table.

*Here we go,* McClane said. *Watch him. Not what he says. Where his eyes go while he's saying it.*

"I won't keep you from the chocolate fountain long," Anders said, and the room laughed, warm, on cue, the laugh a room always gave a rich man for a joke that wasn't actually funny. "I made that promise to the planning committee in writing, and I have been advised that several attorneys are present to enforce it."

The second laugh came easier. Anders waited exactly long enough to let it crest.

"I just wanted to say, before the night gets away from us, how much it means to have all of you here. This building does real work. The kind that doesn't always make the papers. A child gets an inhaler before an emergency-room visit becomes an overnight stay. A woman who has missed three appointments because the bus route changed gets seen at a neighborhood clinic instead. A dockworker who has spent twenty years being told that pain is part of the job gets a physician who asks a second question. None of those moments belongs on a plaque. All of them are why this place exists."

His eyes found each constituency as he named it. Hospital staff for the child. The council table for the bus route. Danny and the union contingent for the dockworker. It felt spontaneous until Taylor noticed how neatly every example placed gratitude at a different table.

"And it does that work because people like you, and people like Danny Hebert back there, believe a city's worth investing in, even the parts of it that don't always believe in themselves."

He raised his glass toward the silent-auction display. "By tomorrow morning, every pledge and outreach commitment represented here will enter independent state review before becoming part of the public trust. Transparency is not an obstacle to good work. It is how good work earns the right to continue."

That drew the serious applause, board members and city officials responding to the word *transparency* as if Anders had personally invented it. Taylor saw his gaze touch the loading-corridor entrance before returning to the room.

Danny, three feet from Taylor, went faintly pink, embarrassed and proud at once, the way he always got when someone praised him in public he genuinely admired, and Taylor watched a dozen heads turn his way, polite, brief, attention he'd have hated if it lasted one second longer than it did.

The photographer got the picture anyway. Danny caught mid-blink, one hand still touching the knot of his tie, Taylor beside him with her face arranged into something she hoped would pass for a daughter tolerating her father's public embarrassment.

Anders had made Danny part of the building's moral architecture in twenty words. Tomorrow, anyone reading about the review would see a union man standing in the room when it was announced. Someone honest enough to make the promise look honest merely by having heard it.

*He just used Dad as a character reference,* Taylor thought.

*Without asking him. That's how guys like this get the best references.*

At the auction table, the woman with the cash box passed a slim blue folder to one of the board members. Taylor had seen identical folders beside the pledge cards earlier. This one carried a white state seal and a red strip across the corner. The board member opened it only far enough to confirm the contents, then tucked it beneath his program.

Onstage, Anders saw the exchange. His smile never changed.

"Now, I know it's been a strange night for security," Anders went on, and something in his voice shifted, light, almost joking, but Taylor felt McClane go very still inside her, the way a man went still right before he stopped breathing loudly on purpose. "Seems we've got some unwelcome guests. Six-legged ones. My events team assures me it's nothing, probably just the cold driving them in through a door somebody forgot to shut. But if anyone spots one of our little uninvited friends, do let a staff member know. We'd hate for anyone to feel, ah, watched, at their own charity gala."

The laugh that followed was smaller this time, uncertain, the kind of laugh a room gave when it wasn't sure the joke had actually been a joke. Somewhere near the bar, a woman in a green dress murmured something to her husband and got a shrug in return, the universal gesture for a rich man's joke that wasn't worth the energy of actually parsing.

Taylor felt the security staff watching the laugh rather than sharing it. One man near the coat check tracked every guest who looked toward the loading corridor. Another watched the people who did not laugh at all. The woman near the kitchen studied hands instead of faces, looking for a phone disappearing into a purse, a napkin being folded too carefully, fingers tightening around a glass. One of the men on the mezzanine watched the reporter at the back. The other watched the room reflected in the dark glass above the stage.

The toast was not only bait. It was a camera with human eyes, six angles, and a staff trained to notice what embarrassment looked like when someone was trying to call it boredom.

McClane walked Taylor through them as Anders let the laughter fade.

*Coat-check guy has the loading corridor. Kitchen woman has hands and phones. Mezzanine left has the press. Right one's using the windows so he can watch without turning his head. East-door guy's watching the people who looked for an exit after the bug line.*

*And the sixth?*

*Hasn't moved. That's the one I don't like.*

Taylor found him at last beside the sound controls, dressed like a technician, one hand resting on a closed equipment case. He was not watching the guests. He was watching the watchers, checking that every other pair of eyes stayed on task.

Anders had built a chain of observation into the room. Taylor sat at the far end of it, unseen for now, feeling each link pull tight.

Danny leaned toward Taylor. "Little pointed for a bug joke."

She turned to him too quickly. "You noticed?"

"Hard not to. Guy spends all night pretending everything's effortless, then uses a charity toast to complain about insects." Danny lifted one shoulder. "Maybe he's got strong feelings about pest control."

The joke did not hide his unease from her. Taylor wanted to tell him that the insects were hers, that Anders had found them, that the men at the doors were counting reactions. The truth assembled itself behind her teeth and stopped there.

"Probably," she said.

Danny looked at her for another second. Before he could ask the question forming behind his eyes, the applause began and forced them both to face the stage.

"Taylor."

He had not raised his voice. That made it worse.

"What?"

"You've spent half the night watching doors. Now the man giving the toast makes a joke about being watched and you look like somebody read your diary out loud."

She could have told him. Three words would have done it. *The bugs are mine.* Five more could have made the danger clear. *The people here know something.*

Then he would look. Not because he was careless, but because he was her father and she had just told him a threat existed in the same room as his daughter. He would look at Anders, the doors, the men pretending to be waiters. Security would see a middle-aged union officer begin assessing exits five seconds after the bait landed.

Taylor made herself glance toward the photographer instead.

"I hate having my picture taken."

Danny followed her gaze. The photographer lowered his camera and moved toward the council table.

"You could've warned me before I dragged you to an event with a hired photographer."

"I did. Repeatedly. I used the phrase 'I'd rather chew glass.'"

"I thought that was about the dress."

"It was a flexible objection."

His mouth twitched. He did not stop being worried, but he accepted the exit she had given him. That, too, hurt.

*Everybody's already forgetting he said it,* McClane observed. *That's the real trick of a room like this. Say something true, wrap it in enough charm, and it evaporates in about four seconds.*

*Not for us.*

*No,* he agreed. *Not for us.*

Anders's eyes moved once, slow, deliberate, across the crowd, not lingering anywhere long enough to be obvious, a practiced sweep that could have passed for nothing more than a host checking his audience's mood.

They passed over Taylor for exactly as long as they passed over everyone else.

*Don't react,* McClane said, fast, low, urgent in a way that cut clean through her own instinct to do exactly the opposite. *Whatever you're feeling right now, bury it. Smile at your dad. Look bored. This is the test. This is him fishing, wide, seeing who flinches.*

Taylor smiled at her dad. It felt like lifting something heavy.

Danny smiled back, uncertain now. Taylor made herself roll her eyes toward the stage, the universal expression of a teenager trapped at an adult function. His shoulders eased. The movement hurt more than holding still had.

Anders's gaze passed them. It did not pause.

At the east doors, one of his men touched his earpiece and relaxed.

*"There it is,"* the gallery murmured. *"The engineered moment. He's not looking for the culprit. He's looking for the flinch."*

*"I'm flinching," Gladly admitted. "Internally. Constantly."*

*"You don't have a body, Gladly."*

*"I flinch on principle."*

Anders finished his toast a moment later, warm, gracious, thanking the room again, stepping down off the stage to scattered applause and the low murmur of two hundred conversations picking back up where they'd left off. He didn't look toward Taylor again. He didn't look toward anyone in particular, which was, McClane pointed out grimly, exactly the problem.

He did not vanish into the board members waiting for him. He worked the room.

Anders stopped first at the nurses' table, where he remembered one woman's husband's name and asked another about a grant application that had stalled in committee. He crossed to the city delegation and took a joke from the deputy mayor with the patient smile of a man granting the joke an audience. He touched the reporter's elbow, said something too quiet for Taylor's remaining insects to catch, and left the young man smiling despite the fact that his notebook had somehow ended up closed.

Every stop looked casual. Every stop lasted less than a minute. Every stop left the person he had spoken to facing away from whichever doorway they had been nearest.

Then he came toward Danny.

*Don't leave,* McClane said.

Taylor had not realized she was shifting her weight until he said it.

*Why not?*

*Because your dad stays if you go, and then you can't hear what Anders asks him. Because walking away from the host coming to thank your table looks weird. Because right now you're not a suspect. You're Danny Hebert's bored kid. Be Danny Hebert's bored kid.*

Anders was intercepted twice on the way. A physician wanted thirty seconds. A board member touched the blue audit folder beneath his program and received the smallest possible shake of Anders's head. Not here. Not yet. Then Anders arrived with both hands empty and his attention apparently devoted to Danny alone.

"Danny. Forgive the ambush."

"You could've picked somebody who photographs better."

"I did. She was standing next to you."

Anders turned the smile toward Taylor. It was polished, harmless, and supported by four separate members of his security staff watching the surrounding crowd.

"Taylor, isn't it? Your father tells me you have inherited his patience for formal events."

"He says a lot of things when he's trying to get me into a dress."

Danny made a sound halfway between a cough and a laugh.

Anders laughed too, and Taylor hated that it sounded genuine. "A sensible position. I have spent twenty years trying to make these evenings shorter and somehow only succeeded in making them more expensive."

Up close, there was nothing visibly predatory about him. No unusual stillness. No cold eyes waiting behind the friendly ones. His tuxedo fit perfectly, but not ostentatiously. His cuff links were plain silver. He gave Danny the courtesy of standing at an angle that included Taylor without forcing her into the conversation. If Taylor had met him yesterday, she might have found him reassuring.

*That's the package,* McClane said. *Remember the package isn't the product.*

Anders looked back to Danny. "I meant what I said. Community labor made the outreach program possible, and your people caught two design failures before the first clinic opened. The review team should hear that from you, if you're willing."

Danny's expression changed. Praise embarrassed him. Work did not.

"They can hear it. They should also hear the wheelchair ramp at the Mason Street clinic still freezes before the sidewalk does."

"Then I hope you tell them before anyone else does." Anders nodded toward the board member carrying the blue folder. "Nine tomorrow morning. Conference suite on six. No speeches, no cameras, and if anyone tries to turn it into either, you have my permission to leave."

The offer was so exactly shaped for Danny that Taylor could feel the craftsmanship in it. Respect the work. Distrust ceremony. Put the grievance on the record. Anders did not flatter people by telling them what he liked about them. He flattered them by proving he knew what they liked about themselves.

"I'll be there," Danny said.

"Good." Anders offered his hand. Danny took it. "Enjoy the rest of the evening. Both of you. And if our tiny trespassers make another appearance, I promise the building can survive the scandal."

He gave Taylor no extra glance when he said it. No pause, no concealed emphasis. The words were a callback to the toast, available to anyone who heard them.

Taylor still felt the hair rise along both arms.

"We'll keep our eyes open," Danny said.

*Dad, no.*

Anders smiled. "That's all any of us can do."

Then he moved on.

Taylor held herself still until he had crossed three conversation clusters and the technician beside the sound controls had looked away.

*Did he know?*

*No,* McClane said after a moment. *He knew your name because your dad's on his program and you came in together. He came over because he just borrowed your dad's reputation in front of a room. Thanking him sells the gesture. The bug line at the end was him keeping the bait in the water, not setting the hook in you.*

*You're sure.*

*I'm sure enough to act on it. That's not the same thing as forever.*

Danny watched Anders go. "He remembers too much."

Taylor looked at him.

"Names, projects, the ramp. People who are good at that make me nervous." Danny picked up his abandoned drink and frowned when he found the ice melted. "Nobody's that interested in everybody."

For one bright second Taylor thought he had seen all of it.

Then he added, "Still, if he gets the ramp fixed, he can be as unsettling as he wants."

The hope went out quietly. Taylor hated herself for feeling relieved.

*He's not looking for you specifically. Not yet.* McClane's voice had gone flat, careful, the tone of a man laying out a threat assessment he didn't like the shape of. *He's looking for a reaction. Anybody's reaction. That toast wasn't a joke, it was bait, dropped in front of two hundred people to see who bit.*

*Did anybody?*

*Not that I could see. Which either means nobody in this room's onto him but us, or it means whoever's actually watching him back is a lot better at holding still than the average party guest.* A pause, and something almost like grudging respect crept into the words. *Which, for what it's worth, you just proved you can do. He fished. You didn't take it. That's not nothing.*

*So he doesn't know it's me.*

*He doesn't know it's you.* McClane's certainty had an edge to it now, careful, provisional, the certainty of a man who knew exactly how fast a read like that could go stale. *He knows something's off. He doesn't know what, or who, or how much they've actually seen. That's a wide net, kid, and wide nets catch a lot of nothing before they catch the one thing they're actually after.*

*That's supposed to make me feel better?*

*It's supposed to make you careful.* No warmth in it now, just weight, blunt in the way of a man who'd stopped softening things the moment softening stopped being useful. *Wide net now becomes a narrow one fast, the second he gets one real data point to work from. Right now you're a needle in two hundred haystacks. Give him a reason to start counting fewer haystacks, and that math gets a lot worse for you, a lot faster than you want it to.*

Taylor looked across the room, once, carefully, and found Max Anders already deep in conversation with a city councilman, laughing at something, easy, warm, a man with nothing on his mind heavier than chocolate fountains and charity math. Behind him, through the tall glass windows overlooking the parking structure, she caught a flash of headlights sweeping across the lot, some latecomer's car pulling in, and for one irrational second her whole body went tight with the certainty that it meant something, before she made herself breathe and let it go. Not everything tonight was a signal. Just most things.

She didn't believe it for a second. Neither, she was fairly sure, did he believe his own performance of it, not entirely, not underneath.

It was a strange thing, watching a man be that good at something so small. Charity math. Chocolate fountains. A whole life's worth of practice spent making a room feel comfortable, and all of it, every warm word and every practiced laugh, sitting on top of whatever he actually was the way a tablecloth sat on top of a table somebody had built to hold considerably more weight than dinner.

*"I don't know how I feel about a villain this good at his own toast,"* the newer voice said. *"I almost expect a beautiful woman to step out for a tango."*

*"That's usually the kind you should worry about most,"* Fury said. *"The ones who never stop performing long enough to slip."*

The quartet started up again, something lighter this time, and the party closed back over the moment the way water closed over a stone, smooth, unbroken, as if nothing at all had happened, as if two hundred people hadn't just watched their gracious host cast a net none of them had noticed was a net at all.

Taylor kept smiling. It was, she was starting to realize, going to be a very long night.



Chapter 8: Traffic Alert



The staff changed first. That was the tell McClane caught before Taylor did, a shift so small she'd have written it off as nothing if he hadn't flagged it the second it happened.

*Two more of them by the east doors,* he said. *Watch how they're standing. Not serving anybody. Not clearing plates. Just standing.*

Taylor found them without turning her head, a skill she was getting better at by the hour, two men in the same black-and-white catering uniform as everyone else, positioned at the east doors with trays they weren't offering to anyone. One of them had a build that no amount of formal wear quite managed to disguise, shoulders too wide, stance too settled, weight on the balls of his feet in a way waiters simply didn't stand.

*Muscle. Somebody just doubled the muscle on that door.*

*Or somebody's short-staffed and grabbed whoever was closest.*

*Kid.* No heat in it, just certainty, the patience McClane seemed to keep in reserve for arguments he'd already had a hundred times with a hundred less careful people. *I've worked enough details in my life to know what a bored waiter looks like versus what a guy pretending to be one looks like. Those two aren't bored. They're watching the room the exact same way we've been watching it. Different training, maybe. Same job tonight.*

One of the real waiters reached them carrying a tray of empty champagne flutes. He was young, sweating lightly beneath a collar starched past comfort, moving with the exhausted speed of someone who had spent five hours being told that rich people should never see him hurry. The wider man murmured something without turning his head.

The waiter stopped.

For a second his expression was blank. Then he looked down at the tray, looked toward the kitchen, and handed it over.

The wider man accepted it without shifting his stance. The waiter removed a folded service cloth from his belt and passed that over too. Last came the small black order pad from his breast pocket. The false waiter tucked it away without opening it, fully equipped now with the props of a job he still had no intention of performing.

"Break rotation changed," the second man said.

The real waiter glanced toward a woman supervising the coffee service. She had heard. She kept arranging cups and did not meet his eyes.

"Nobody told me."

"They just did. Kitchen until somebody comes for you."

There was no threat in the words. That made the obedience worse. The waiter took one look at both men, lowered his gaze, and walked back the way he had come with empty hands.

*That wasn't extra staffing,* Taylor thought.

*No. That was a uniform changing owners without anybody taking it off.*

The false waiter lifted the tray and began turning slowly with the room. From a distance he looked useful. Up close, every glass on it was empty.

It kept happening, small and quiet, over the next twenty minutes, each new piece slotting into a shape neither of them liked. A third man near the coat check, positioning himself where he could see the main entrance and the service corridor both at once. A fourth drifting a slow, unhurried loop through the crowd that took him past every cluster of guests without ever once stopping to actually serve anyone. A fifth stationed at the base of the grand staircase, angled to watch both the mezzanine above and the atrium floor below at the same time. None of them looked at Taylor twice. None of them looked at anyone twice, not obviously, which was, McClane said, exactly the problem.

Taylor stopped counting people and started counting positions.

The atrium had six routes out if she treated every door as equal. The main entrance opened into the coat-check lobby and the covered drive. The east doors crossed a glass vestibule to the garden terrace. The kitchen connected to a service corridor, then forked toward the loading dock and staff parking. A fire door beneath the mezzanine stairs led somewhere her insects had not fully mapped. The stairs themselves reached the second-floor conference suites. The bank of three elevators could reach every public floor and, judging by the buttons Taylor's bugs had felt beneath the plates, two levels the guests had no reason to visit.

Six routes. Seven if she counted the service elevator separately. Eight if the kitchen's dry-goods door reached the alley instead of another storage room.

McClane made her do it again without assuming any door opened where its sign claimed it did.

*Main entrance gets you to a lobby,* he said. *Lobby isn't outside. East doors get you to glass. Glass isn't outside until the next door opens. Kitchen gets you into a corridor we know forks. We don't know where both forks end. Elevators get you into a box somebody else controls. Stairs go up, which isn't out. Count destinations, not promises.*

Taylor revised the map.

Two confirmed exterior routes. One probable. Five ways to move inside the building. No route that carried both her and Danny out without crossing a position Anders's people had quietly occupied.

That did not mean they were trapped. Not yet. It meant someone had decided every departure should have a witness.

The real staff reacted without understanding why. A waiter carrying coffee reached the east doors, received a quiet word, and turned his whole route around. The coat-check attendant surrendered her stool to the third man and moved behind the counter. A MedHall employee tried the mezzanine staircase with a stack of auction sheets and was redirected toward the service lift by the man at its base.

The employee tried to laugh it off. "I only need the board packets."

"They're bringing them down."

"Since when?"

The man smiled at her and touched two fingers to the radio wire disappearing beneath his collar.

"Since just now."

She stood there long enough for the interruption to become visible. Then a board member called her name from across the room, and she seized the excuse to turn away. By the time she reached him, her own smile had returned. She bent close and spoke. The board member glanced at the stairs.

The man at their base was helping an elderly guest find the restrooms. He looked attentive and harmless. The board member nodded before the employee had finished explaining, a tiny surrender offered to avoid making a scene at a charity gala.

*That's how the room helps them,* Taylor thought.

*Yeah. Everybody in here has spent their whole life learning not to be the first person who calls something ugly.*

The replacements did not merely watch the exits. They controlled who was allowed to approach them.

Taylor sent insects to each door. The main entrance still opened whenever a late guest arrived, but only after the coat-check man looked toward the mezzanine and received a nod. The east doors had gained a steel floor pin that had not been there earlier. At the loading corridor, somebody had moved a rolling rack across the fire exit, not blocking it completely, not yet, but close enough to finish the job in seconds.

She tested the routes without moving herself.

A moth above the coat-check desk rode the pressure change when the outer doors opened for a late-arriving couple. Cold air crossed the lobby, but the inner doors remained closed until the new man had compared their names to the guest list. He asked for identification from the husband and not the wife. He looked past both of them at the drive, counting the valet, the sedan, and whoever might still be sitting inside it. Only then did he welcome them into the gala.

At the east vestibule, a spider clinging behind the brass push plate felt the inner door move less than an inch before the floor pin caught. Someone had installed it from the atrium side. It could be lifted in a second by a person who belonged there and would turn an ordinary departure into a visible request for permission for anyone who did not.

The kitchen route was worse. Taylor sent two roaches beneath the swinging door, one along each wall so she could read the corridor from both sides. A security man stood beside the employee time clock, apparently checking a clipboard. Another waited beyond the loading-dock fork with one hand in his jacket pocket. Between them, cooks and servers still moved normally, but every worker leaving the kitchen crossed one man's field of view and approached the other.

The rolling rack at the fire exit held boxed wine. Too heavy for one hurried person to move quietly. Light enough for two of Anders's men to shift once they wanted the door.

*They haven't closed it,* Taylor said.

*They don't need it closed yet. They need to know who tries it.*

*They're building a lock,* she thought.

*Yeah. One piece at a time, slow enough nobody tries the door while it's changing.*

*A room full of people would notice being locked in.*

*A room full of people notices a locked door. Nobody notices six unlocked doors becoming watched doors until somebody needs one.*

Taylor looked at the smiling guests, the quartet, the servers circling with coffee, and understood that the takeover had begun while the party was still happening. The only missing element was anyone admitting it aloud.

She found Danny beside the silent-auction table studying a framed aerial photograph of the waterfront. Someone had marked the current bid at four hundred dollars, which seemed excessive for a picture Danny could have taken from the ferry if he owned a camera and remembered to bring it.

"How much longer?" Taylor asked.

Danny checked his watch. "Auction closes in eighteen minutes. Then we can go."

"We don't have to wait for it."

He turned fully toward her. That was the problem with Danny. He could spend half an evening missing what she was doing because he assumed she wanted the space, then hear one wrong note and give her his complete attention.

"What's wrong?"

Taylor could feel three of Anders's people from where she stood. One at the coat check. One looping behind Danny. One at the stairs, too far away to hear ordinary conversation but looking in their direction whenever his route allowed it.

"It's loud."

"We can leave now."

He meant it immediately. No irritation, no reminder about the invitation or the review meeting or how rarely they did anything together anymore. He reached into his pocket for the coat-check ticket.

Taylor nearly said yes.

McClane did not stop her. That mattered. He laid out the facts instead.

*Main door is still opening. Your dad gives them the ticket, you both put on your coats, you walk out. Might work. Might be the cleanest chance you get.*

*But?*

*But you asked to go three minutes after Anders put the bug line in his toast. If the coat-check guy's reporting sudden departures, you and your dad go on the list. If they decide to delay you, your dad asks why, and then this becomes a confrontation with him standing in the middle of it.*

Danny had the ticket between two fingers. Waiting.

Taylor hated that staying required less visible courage than leaving. She hated more that visible courage was exactly what Anders's people were positioned to notice.

"Eighteen minutes is fine," she said. "I just wanted a number."

Danny did not put the ticket away.

"You sure?"

"Yeah. We leave when the auction closes."

He searched her face, then tucked the ticket back into his pocket. "Eighteen minutes. Even if Anders announces a second chocolate fountain."

"Especially then."

He smiled. Taylor did too. Over his shoulder, the man completing the slow circuit moved past them without altering his pace.

The decision created a deadline. They only needed the room to remain normal for eighteen more minutes.

*How many's that now,* she asked, once the fourth one had drifted past for the second time.

*Counting the two by the east doors? Six that I've clocked. Probably more I haven't.* No alarm in the delivery, just an accounting, careful and precise. *Six is a lot of bodies to burn on watching a party. Whatever he's protecting, it's worth more to him than looking normal tonight.*

*That's supposed to be a comfort?*

*It's supposed to be information. Comfort's not really on the menu right now, kid. Wish it was.*

*"I don't like this chapter,"* Gladly said, and for once there was no lightness at all underneath it. *"I liked the toast chapter better. The toast chapter had jokes."*

*"This one's got jokes too. They're just worse jokes."*

*Is he doing this because of us? Because of what happened with the bugs?*

*Maybe. Maybe not.* McClane's voice had gone careful again, weighing a read he clearly didn't love the shape of. *Could be standard practice for a guy who runs whatever he actually runs, tightens the room a little every night just on principle. Could be him reacting to a hunch he can't name yet. I can't tell you which. What I can tell you is it doesn't matter which, not for what we do next. Either way, this room's getting harder to move through unseen by the minute, and we don't have forever to figure out why.*

Taylor watched the fourth man complete another slow loop, and felt something settle in her chest that wasn't quite fear, not exactly, something colder and more useful than fear, the same feeling she got right before a fight she hadn't chosen but couldn't avoid either. Above them, the chandelier's light caught the crystal in long, slow ripples every time the air handling kicked on, and for one strange, suspended second the whole grand room felt less like a party and more like a stage set built at genuine expense to be torn down later, all of it beautiful, all of it temporary, none of it built to survive whatever the night still had left to throw at it.

*He still doesn't know it's me.*

*No.* Careful again, provisional, a certainty McClane clearly knew had an expiration date on it. *Not from anything I've seen. But knowing something's watching him and not knowing who is a problem he's actively trying to solve right now, in real time, with real bodies on real doors. That's not a man settling back down after a false alarm. That's a man narrowing his search.*

*And when he finishes narrowing it?*

McClane didn't answer right away, and the silence told her more than any answer would have.

*Then we're not playing defense anymore,* he said finally. *Then it's a different kind of problem, and I'd rather we weren't standing here waiting to find out what kind when it happens.*

Across the room, Danny laughed at something the councilwoman said, easy, unaware, a man having a perfectly ordinary evening at a party his daughter had stopped being able to enjoy the moment a stranger's voice had arrived in the middle of it. He'd loosened his tie an inch sometime in the last hour, the closest thing to letting his guard down he ever managed in a room like this, and the sight of it, small and domestic and utterly unaware, made something in Taylor's chest hurt in a way none of the men by the doors had quite managed yet. Taylor watched him for a long moment, and felt the shape of the night rearrange itself around one simple, unbearable fact: whatever came next, he was still standing three feet from the center of it, with no idea at all that there was a center, or a moment, or anything at all worth being afraid of.

*"Anybody want to bet whether she gets those eighteen minutes?"* the newer voice asked.

*"With what money?"* Fury said. *"None of you have wallets."*

*"I contain multitudes and gambling debts."*

Taylor didn't smile this time. She was watching the fourth man complete his loop again, watching him pause, just slightly, just for a second, near the spot where she'd stood twenty minutes ago sending three cold bugs under a service door, and felt, for the first time all night, total certainty that whatever window they'd had to walk away from this quietly had already, somewhere in the last hour, closed behind them without either of them noticing it happen.

*McClane.*

*Yeah, kid.*

*I think we're out of time.*

*Yeah,* he said, and for once there was no dry humor in it at all, just a flat, professional readiness that felt, underneath the fear, almost like being handed something solid to hold onto. *Yeah, I think you might be right.*



Chapter 9: Final Call



The woman approached four minutes into Taylor's promised eighteen.

Danny had gone to confirm that his name was not somehow attached to a five-hundred-dollar harbor photograph. Taylor remained at the edge of the auction display, close enough to call him back, far enough that doing so would interrupt three people. She had been watching the slow hand of the wall clock above the coat check when the woman detached herself from the coffee service and came directly toward her.

She had the same catering uniform as the others, the same trained pleasantness, and Taylor knew, before she'd said a single word, that this wasn't about hors d'oeuvres. The woman carried a clipboard with one blank page clipped to it. She held it angled toward the room as if protecting confidential information that did not exist.

"Miss Hebert?" A clipboard, held loose, more prop than purpose. "There's a call for you. Front desk. They said it was important."

*Nobody knows I'm here except my dad,* Taylor thought, careful, quiet, angled at the woman's badge instead of her face. *And he's twelve feet away.*

*Don't say that out loud. Smile. Ask who's calling.*

"Did they say who?"

"A school administrator, I believe. Something about a scheduling matter." The woman's smile didn't waver, professional, patient, reciting a line she'd clearly been handed rather than one she'd written herself. "If you'd like to follow me, the front desk has a phone you can use privately."

She stepped half a pace aside as she said it, opening a path toward the coat-check lobby. It was a courtesy movement, the sort meant to keep Taylor from feeling directed. Behind her, the man at the main doors turned to accept a claim ticket from an elderly guest and placed himself squarely between Taylor and the outer vestibule.

The phone might be real. The call might even contain a voice prepared to sound like Blackwell. None of that made the path private by accident.

"Which administrator?"

The woman did not hesitate. "Principal Blackwell."

Taylor's first hope, that the story would collapse under one follow-up question, disappeared.

*She got the name right,* Taylor thought.

*Means they did homework. Doesn't make the call real.*

"What scheduling matter?"

"She didn't give me details." The woman's expression added just enough sympathy to imply school trouble without saying it. "She did say it couldn't wait until Monday."

Blackwell knew Taylor's father handled school calls. Every form in Winslow's office listed Danny's number first. The woman had the right name and the wrong procedure, which made the lie more frightening than a careless one would have been.

Someone had not guessed at Taylor. Someone had looked her up.

Or they had looked up everyone.

Taylor remembered the printed guest list at the coat-check desk, the board packets upstairs, the blue audit folder moving through the room. Danny had not simply bought two tickets. MedHall knew his employer, his position at the Dockworkers Association, his home address for the receipt, and the name of the daughter attending with him. From that, Winslow took minutes to find.

*Prepared background,* McClane said. *Not necessarily prepared for you. Guy planning to control a room full of people wants a pressure point for everybody worth controlling. Employers. Spouses. Kids. Schools. Doesn't mean they solved us. Means they had a file and picked the page most likely to make a teenager walk away from her dad.*

That was not comforting. It did put the danger back where it belonged. Taylor had become a candidate, not an answer.

*That's not a school calling.* McClane's voice had gone flat, fast, all business. *Nobody administrative works this late on a Saturday, and even if they did, they'd call your dad's cell, not track down a fifteen-year-old at a party through the venue's front desk. This is a pull. They want you somewhere with fewer people in it.*

*So what do I do.*

*You don't go anywhere alone with a stranger holding a clipboard, that's rule one, doesn't matter what world you're from.* A beat, calculating, fast. *But you also can't just say no. That reads as guilty, or scared, either one gives them exactly the data point they're fishing for. You need a reason that isn't a refusal.*

"Actually," Taylor said, already turning, already reaching, "my dad's right over there, I'll just grab him, he'll want to take it too if it's about school."

Something flickered behind the woman's professional pleasantness, brief, controlled, gone almost before it registered. "Of course. Whatever's easiest."

She did not leave. She waited for Taylor to act on the answer.

*"Nicely done,"* Gladly said, delighted. *"Redirected the whole play without ever saying no. That's, what, improv? That's improv. I'm very proud."*

*"She had help,"* Fury said.

*"She had help fast. That's still hers."*

Taylor crossed the room toward Danny because failing to do so would turn a good excuse into a visible lie. The woman followed for three steps, then stopped beside the auction display where she could watch without appearing to.

Danny saw Taylor coming and excused himself from the harbor-photograph argument.

"Please tell me you didn't bid on it."

"I was finding out who did. Apparently the frame is reclaimed pier timber."

"We have reclaimed pier timber in the garage."

"That's not reclaimed. I keep meaning to throw it away."

Taylor reached him. The woman remained in place, patient, still expecting a father to be collected for the school call.

"Front desk says somebody called from Winslow," Taylor said. "Probably wrong number, but they want you too."

Danny's attention sharpened. "Who from Winslow?"

"They said Blackwell."

"At this hour?"

"That's what I thought."

He looked toward the woman. Taylor felt the danger in the movement before it completed, but Danny did not study her or the doors behind her. He lifted one hand and beckoned her over, perfectly willing to make a school administrator wait on the line while he demanded an explanation in public.

The woman smiled, shook her head, and tapped the empty page on her clipboard. She pantomimed checking, then held up one apologetic finger. A mistake. One moment.

She turned away before Danny could cross to her.

"Efficient operation," Danny muttered.

"Maybe the chocolate fountain handles messages too."

He checked his watch. "Fourteen minutes."

"I'm counting."

Taylor stayed beside him until the woman disappeared through the service door. Then a councilwoman reclaimed Danny with a question about the review, and Taylor moved only far enough to avoid being folded into it.

A bug on the wall watched the woman reach the service corridor, discard the pleasant expression before the door had finished closing, and speak one sentence into the radio beneath her collar.

"She brought up the father."

The answer came from the man beyond the loading-dock fork, too low for the wall insect and nearly lost beneath a cart rattling across the kitchen tile.

"Expected. She asked to leave after the toast. We verify the reaction, then clear her."

The woman touched two fingers to her radio. "She questioned procedure. Father questioned the hour."

"Then the father stays out of it. Try the host."

The woman looked through the frosted glass toward Danny.

Then she walked away.

*There,* McClane said. *That's why you. Asking to leave put you on a list. They don't know what you saw. They don't know what you can do. They know the timing made you worth checking.*

*And if I pass?*

*They clear you and move to the next person who looked wrong after the toast.*

*If I don't?*

*They stop asking you questions they already know the answer to.*

*That's one.* McClane's voice had lost the tactical edge, gone flatter, grimmer. *There'll be another. Guy running a play like that doesn't stop at one attempt just because the first one didn't land.*

*How do you know.*

*Because I've run plays like that. Different context. Same shape. You don't get lucky once and call it a night, not if the thing you're after actually matters to you.*

*How long till the next one, then.*

*Depends how patient he is. Some guys wait an hour, let you relax first. Some guys can't stand not knowing and come back in five minutes.* A pause, weighing it. *Guy who just watched his own security detail lose somebody? My money's on the second kind.*

He wasn't wrong.

The second attempt came eleven minutes later, with three minutes left on Danny's clock.

In the interval, Taylor saw the auction coordinator receive the blue review folder from the board member, lock it inside a wheeled document case, and hand the key to a MedHall attorney. She saw the man at the mezzanine stairs report twice into his collar without appearing to move his mouth. She felt the woman from the false call take a station in the kitchen corridor rather than return to the party.

Nobody else approached Taylor. That was how she knew the second attempt had begun before the man reached her.

He wore a suit, not catering black-and-white this time, actual suit, actual confidence, and steered close enough that Taylor felt the wrongness of someone entering her space with intent rather than accident. He had come from the direction of the mezzanine stairs, which meant somebody up there had a clean line of sight down onto the whole atrium floor. Someone had been watching from above while she'd been busy tracking men at eye level.

The man chose his approach carefully. Danny stood beside the councilwoman near the bar. A pair of hospital administrators crossed between him and Taylor just as the man arrived. Behind Taylor, the dessert table and a velvet auction rope limited how far she could step back without making the encounter look like something she wanted to escape.

"Miss Hebert. Mr. Anders would love a word, if you have a moment. Somewhere a little quieter."

*There it is.* McClane's voice had gone very still, a man doing math he didn't like the answer to. *That's not a clipboard anymore. That's a direct ask, from someone who works for him, using his name like it's supposed to open every door in the building.*

*What do I say?*

*The truth, mostly. You're not supposed to be alone with strange men at parties, and your dad would want to know if the host is asking. Make it about manners, not suspicion. Manners he can't argue with.*

"That's so nice of him," Taylor said, bright, easy, a girl who'd clearly been raised right. "Could you let him know I'll come find him in a bit? I promised my dad I'd stick close for the toast photos."

The man's smile held. Whatever was underneath it didn't quite manage to.

"It won't take more than a minute. Mr. Anders prefers to thank the families of tonight's community partners personally."

It was better than the school call. It used something Anders had already done in public, gave Taylor a harmless reason to feel chosen, and made refusal sound rude rather than frightened.

"Then he won't mind thanking Dad at the same time."

"Mr. Hebert appears occupied."

"He'll forgive us."

Taylor lifted her hand toward Danny.

The man moved half a step, enough to interrupt her line of sight without openly blocking it. "There's no need to disturb him."

The sentence changed the encounter. Not loudly. Not enough for the administrators beside them to hear it as a threat. But the pleasant fiction required Taylor to accept that a stranger knew when she was permitted to involve her father.

McClane did not give her a line. He had already given her the rule. Make the pressure visible without accusing anyone of applying it.

Taylor leaned sideways and spoke past the man's shoulder.

"Dad? Mr. Anders wants me somewhere private."

She pitched it one degree too loud for an ordinary aside. The nearer hospital administrator glanced over. The second followed her attention. Danny stopped speaking in the middle of the councilwoman's answer.

The man in the suit stepped clear at once.

"A brief misunderstanding," he said, raising one hand toward Danny. "Please, don't let us interrupt you."

His retreat was smooth enough to preserve the room's interpretation. Taylor had misunderstood an invitation. The staff member had corrected her. An awkward second passed, then the administrators turned back to their conversation.

Danny did not.

"Taylor?"

"He wanted both of us for another picture," she said. "I told him we're leaving."

The man was still close enough to hear. That was intentional.

Danny checked his watch. "Three minutes. Then coats. No extensions."

"Works for me."

He kept watching until the man made a small apologetic bow and moved away.

*"Toast photos,"* Gib said, appreciative. *"A lie built entirely out of things that actually happened. I respect the craftsmanship."*

*"Taylor's the one containing multitudes right now,"* O'Malley said quietly. *"That's her whole schtick."*

Taylor watched the man thread back through the crowd toward the service corridor, toward, she was fairly sure, wherever Max Anders was standing right now, working out what came next.

*Two.* McClane's voice had no humor left in it at all. *That's two real attempts in under fifteen minutes, kid. He's not fishing anymore. He's actively trying to get you somewhere quiet, and he's running out of polite ways to ask.*

*He still doesn't know it's me. You said so.*

*I said he doesn't know you're the one watching him.* Careful now, deliberate, weighing a distinction that mattered more than it sounded like it should. *He knew your name before the toast. You're on his guest list and he used your dad in the speech. That's not the same as knowing what you did. Two attempts on you specifically, in fifteen minutes, means you've become somebody's best guess, even if you're not their confirmed answer yet. Best guess is close enough to be dangerous.*

Taylor looked across the room, once, and found Danny back in conversation, his attention divided now, one eye returning to her between the councilwoman's sentences. He knew his daughter had been bothered twice. He did not know both approaches belonged to the same search, that the people conducting it watched every exit, or that the gracious host apparently wanted her isolated before Danny's three minutes expired.

*"Three minutes,"* Fury said. *"Plenty of warning, right up until it isn't."*

*You still with me?* McClane asked, once she'd put a solid twenty feet and a cluster of chattering board members between herself and the last place anyone had seen her standing.

*Still here.*

*Good. Keep moving. Don't stop long enough to be findable.*

Taylor set her shoulders, found the nearest cluster of guests thick enough to disappear into, and started walking, unhurried, toward the safest, most crowded corner of a room that no longer felt safe at all. Above her, on the mezzanine, she caught the barest flicker of movement, someone stepping back from the railing just as she looked up, gone before she could be sure she'd seen anything at all.

*You catch that,* she thought.

*Yeah.* Grim, certain. *Guy up top. Different angle, same job. We're not dealing with a handful of guys anymore, kid. We're dealing with a building.*



Chapter 10: Go Around



The third attempt didn't ask.

Taylor felt it before she saw it, a shift in the crowd's texture near the edge of the atrium, two of the catering-uniformed men closing in from opposite angles, unhurried, professional, converging on a spot that happened to be exactly where she was standing. No clipboard this time. No pretense of a phone call.

The wall clock had not yet consumed Danny's final three minutes.

He was making his way toward her from the bar, one hand already inside his jacket for the coat-check ticket, when the first man changed course. He did not move between Taylor and Danny. That would have looked deliberate. He intercepted a server carrying coffee, took two cups, and turned with the tray at precisely the angle that forced Danny to step around a knot of departing board members.

The second man entered from Taylor's right.

Neither looked at her. Both altered the room around her.

*Move.* McClane's voice cracked through her like a slap, all the careful patience from the last two attempts gone. *Now. Toward the crowd, not away from it. Don't run, don't look like you're running, just get somewhere thick with people they can't work around without making a scene.*

She moved. Not fast, not obvious, a girl weaving toward the dessert table the way any bored teenager might, and felt the two men adjust behind her, recalculating, still closing, still patient, something in that patience making her skin crawl. They didn't hurry. That was the worst part. Two men who could have closed the distance in three strides instead paced themselves to match the room's own unhurried rhythm, professionals who'd clearly done this enough times to know that speed was the one thing that would actually get somebody's attention in a crowd like this.

Danny cleared the board members and looked toward the place Taylor had been.

She could call to him. The first syllable would bring his head around. It would also tell both men that she understood the approach and needed rescue from it. After two polite failures, that might be all the confirmation they required.

Taylor kept moving.

*He'll see I'm gone.*

*That's better than him seeing why.* McClane's answer came instantly. *Keep him out of their hands first. Explain it when you're not surrounded.*

The man on her left reached the dessert table first and stopped to examine a row of pastries. The second drifted behind her. Neither blocked her path. Together they left only one comfortable route, a gap between the chocolate fountain and a decorative partition leading toward the service hall.

They were herding her.

Taylor slowed by half a step and let the man behind her believe the lane had worked. Beyond the partition, a security guard waited beside the service-hall door with his face turned toward the stage. He held no tray, no clipboard, no reason to stand there. The comfortable route ended with three men and no witnesses.

Taylor changed direction toward the dance floor.

The man beside the pastries abandoned an untouched tart. A real server emerged from the kitchen with a tray of water glasses just as he turned. The false waiter clipped the tray with his elbow.

Glass struck glass. Water sheeted across the server's sleeve and splashed onto the carpet. The server stopped in shock, then began apologizing to the nearest guests even though nothing about the collision had been his fault. The spill closed Taylor's route back toward Danny and drew a loose semicircle of people around it.

The men had built a temporary wall out of manners.

The man behind her placed two fingers against her elbow.

"Miss Hebert," he said pleasantly. "Careful. Crowded floor."

The grip was gentle enough that nobody watching would have called it a grip. His thumb rested over the nerve inside her arm. When she tried to continue, pressure flashed down to her hand.

*Don't pull,* McClane said. *Pulling starts a contest and tells him you know you're in one. Turn toward him. Make him move his feet.*

Taylor pivoted as if responding to the warning. The man had to release her elbow or twist his own wrist. She smiled, stepped around a couple entering the dance floor, and put six moving bodies between them.

The couple were older and moving carefully through turns they had probably learned before Taylor was born. Taylor matched their rotation for three steps, using the man's shoulder and the woman's raised arm as cover. The grip man circled clockwise. His partner circled the other way. They did not shove through the dancers. They waited for the music to deliver Taylor back to the edge.

It would take less than one verse.

*That was a grab.*

*That was him finding out whether you'd let it become one.* McClane's anger remained controlled, which made it feel larger. *Next one won't ask.*

Her hands wanted to shake. She didn't let them, mostly because McClane's voice in her head had exactly the texture of a man who'd never once let his own hands shake at a moment like this, and some petty, stubborn part of her didn't want to be the first one to break the streak.

*They're not gonna grab you in front of two hundred witnesses. That's not the play. The play is corner you somewhere the crowd thins out, make it look natural, walk you somewhere quieter before anybody notices you're gone.*

*So what do I do? I can't out-walk them forever.*

*No.* A beat, and she felt him doing the thing he did, fast and blunt, cataloging not just the room now but the whole shape of the problem, angles, exits, a crowd that could be used as cover or as a trap depending entirely on who was moving through it. *You need a diversion. Something that pulls attention somewhere else, buys you a clean thirty seconds to actually get lost in this room instead of just delaying the inevitable.*

*I don't have thirty seconds worth of anything.*

*You've got a few dozen bugs already warmed up from earlier, and a room full of people who'll all look the same direction the second something interesting happens.* Something sharpened in his voice, fast, tactical, the exact texture of a man building a plan out of nothing because nothing was what he had to work with. *Chandelier. Big one, over the dance floor. You said you can get things moving without anybody clocking the how. Give me a distraction up there, loud enough to turn every head in the building, and you use that half second to get lost in the crowd.*

*I can't move a chandelier.*

*Forget the chandelier. Chandelier's a maybe. This is a definitely.* Something sharpened further, fast, certain, a plan snapping into its final shape. *You've got a wasp in that batch. Don't waste it making shapes in the dark where nobody can see it clearly. Send it down. Right onto whoever's playing that horn. Nobody looks at a hallway when a live musician screws up a note that hard, in front of everybody, and can't for the life of him figure out why.*

Taylor found the wasp clinging to a warm lighting fixture above the quartet. It was sluggish, one hind leg damaged, its body working harder than it should have had to in the winter air moving down from the glass ceiling.

*It might not make it.*

*Then shorten the route. Wait until the dancers turn you toward the stage. Bring it straight down.*

Taylor let the older couple carry her through another half turn. The grip man appeared between shoulders, four yards away. His partner waited at the edge of the floor. Danny stood beyond both of them, searching now, coat ticket in his hand.

The horn player lifted his instrument.

Taylor sent the wasp.

It took less than the thirty seconds he'd promised her. Three bugs went up into the dark above the quartet, cover, motion, nothing that mattered. The fourth, the wasp, dropped low and straight, landing on the horn player's hand a half second before he needed it, and stung him right between two knuckles just as he drew breath for the next phrase.

The note that came out wasn't a note at all, a blaring, cracked bleat that cut through the whole quartet at once, and the horn player flinched hard enough to nearly drop the instrument, shaking his hand out, red-faced, mouthing something that was very clearly not an apology fit for a charity gala. The rest of the quartet stumbled half a bar trying to cover for him before giving up entirely. The whole room's attention snapped toward the little stage, immediate, total, the exact pull a professional musician visibly losing his composure mid-performance always had in a crowded room, a pull no amount of scrabbling in a chandelier could have matched.

The two men in catering black-and-white hesitated, just barely, just long enough.

Taylor was already moving, already three tables deeper into the crowd, already turning a corner around a cluster of city councilmen who'd all craned their necks toward the little stage like the horn player's ruined evening owed them an explanation. She cut left around a server balancing a full tray, ducked under the arm of a man mid-toast with someone she didn't recognize, wove through a knot of women comparing earrings loudly enough to make actual conversation impossible for anyone standing near them, using the party's own careless, oblivious mass the way McClane had told her to, a river she could disappear into as long as she kept moving with its current instead of against it.

She did not run toward Danny. Both men expected that. One recovered from the distraction and drove directly toward him, eyes cutting across the faces between them. The other went to the main doors, betting that a frightened girl with a waiting father would choose the exit over cover.

Taylor turned away from both.

She slipped behind the silent-auction display, crossed through the photographer's abandoned line of sight, then reversed direction along the inner edge of the dance floor. The move bought her a view of Danny through three separate insects. He reached the coat check alone, set his ticket on the counter, and looked back over the room.

Taylor saw the instant concern replaced confusion on his face.

*I'm making him think I left him.*

*You're making sure he doesn't watch two grown men put hands on you. Keep moving.*

Behind her, distantly, she heard one of the two men say something sharp into a lapel she was fairly sure hid a radio, a short, clipped transmission that had nothing polite left in it at all.

*"There it is,"* the gallery said. *"That's the engine: never beat the men chasing her when she can make the whole room look somewhere else."*

*"The horn player flubs the note,"* someone said, pleased. *"And yes, I was part of that chase."*

*Nice work, kid.* Real warmth in it now, unmistakable, a man who'd built a hundred plans out of nothing over thirty years and recognized one landing clean when he saw it. *That's exactly the kind of thing I meant. You didn't have the toolkit for that an hour ago. Now you do.*

Taylor felt something loosen in her chest that had nothing to do with the two men she'd just lost in the crowd, something closer to pride, sharp and unfamiliar and a little bit frightening in how good it felt. She'd built things before, science fair volcanoes and a robotics club project nobody else had wanted a part of, and none of it had ever felt like this, like a piece of herself clicking into a shape she hadn't known was missing until it filled in.

*"Is this the moment?"* Gladly asked.

*"No line yet,"* Fury said. *"Rules are rules."*

Taylor, half-hidden behind a pillar now, catching her breath, felt the shape of the last thirty seconds settle into something she hadn't expected to feel this early into a night like this one. Not fear. Not even really relief. Something closer to the electric satisfaction of a plan working exactly the way it was supposed to.

*You good?* McClane asked, quieter now.

*Yeah.* She almost smiled, and let herself, just this once, just for a second, safely out of sight behind a pillar nobody was looking at. *Yeah, I think I actually am.*

*Good. Because I gotta be honest, kid, watching those two idiots stand there looking up at nothing for a full three seconds while you walked right past them...* Something in his voice cracked, unexpectedly, into real delight, the first unguarded laugh she'd heard out of him all night. *Yippie Kay Yay, Mother Fluffer.*

There was a beat of total, ringing silence from the gallery.

*"THAT'S the moment!"* Gladly shrieked. *"That's it, that's it, pay up, pay up, everybody pay up!"*

*"...Goddammit,"* Fury said.



Chapter 11: No-Fly Zone



Whatever quiet satisfaction the ruined horn note had bought her lasted exactly as long as it took Max Anders to stop smiling at the mayor's wife.

Taylor caught it from across the room, a flicker, brief and total, the practiced warmth dropping off his face for less than a second while his eyes swept the crowd once, hard, methodical, before the smile reassembled itself like nothing had happened at all.

*He knows.* McClane's voice had gone very quiet. *Not you. Not yet. But he knows something just moved through his room that shouldn't have, and he knows two of his own people just lost whoever they were chasing.*

*How do you know that's what happened.*

*Because that's the face of a man whose plan just failed and who's already three steps into the next one.* No hesitation in it, no doubt, the flat certainty of someone who'd worn that exact expression himself, more than once, in rooms a lot less comfortable than this one. *Watch. He's not gonna panic. He's gonna adjust.*

Anders adjusted.

The first person to test the change was an elderly donor in a tuxedo who reached the main doors with his coat already over one arm. The coat-check man stepped in front of him smiling, listened to a complaint about an early flight, and produced an event coordinator who promised the valet line was temporarily backed up. The donor accepted another drink while he waited.

A couple tried the east doors next. One of the false waiters apologized for a spill in the vestibule and guided them toward the silent auction instead. Neither noticed the steel pin already seated in the floor.

The third attempt came from a kitchen employee carrying two trash bags toward the loading dock. He returned thirty seconds later without the bags, face pale, accompanied by a man Taylor had counted twice among Anders's crew.

Nobody shouted. Nobody drew a weapon. Three exits had been refused in three different polite ways, and the party continued.

Then Danny tested the main doors.

He did not have his coat over his arm. He had the claim ticket and the expression he used when a union meeting had exhausted every graceful way to end. Taylor felt him place the ticket on the counter through a fly clinging beneath its edge.

"Two coats," he said. "Hebert."

The attendant who had surrendered her stool reached automatically for the numbered rack. The man occupying her place put one hand over the ticket first.

"It may be a few minutes, sir. We had to move several racks while the entry carpet was cleaned."

"Then show me where they went."

"I'm afraid guests aren't permitted in the storage area."

"I'm not asking to tour it. I'm asking for my coat."
