The event coordinator appeared before his voice rose. She apologized, used Danny's name without being given it, and promised both coats would be brought directly to him. Danny listened with his jaw set, then looked over his shoulder for Taylor.

The search crossed the crowd once, returned to the auction display, and sharpened.

"My daughter was right there."

"I'm sure she hasn't gone far," the coordinator said.

Danny took the claim ticket back from beneath the man's hand. "Find the coats. I'll find my daughter."

He left the counter before they could offer another drink.

*The deadline ran out,* Taylor thought.

*Yeah.*

*He's looking for me.*

*And the two guys looking for you are watching him do it. You walk straight to him now, they get both of you in one place.*

Danny started with the dessert table. Taylor stayed where she was, separated from him by thirty feet of guests and a search that had learned to use him as a compass.

*He's testing the lock,* McClane said. *Making sure his people can turn folks around without anybody realizing they're being contained.*

*What happens when someone doesn't turn around?*

*Then the polite part ends for that person. Everybody else keeps drinking.*

It happened slow enough that no single moment felt alarming, which was, McClane pointed out, exactly the point. Two more men in dark suits appeared near the atrium's east exit, unhurried, unremarkable, their presence reading as security only if you already knew to look for it. A third drifted toward the main doors. A fourth, then a fifth, spreading out along the room's perimeter in a pattern that had nothing to do with serving drinks or clearing plates. Somewhere beyond the tall glass windows, Taylor caught the slow arc of a flashlight beam sweeping the parking structure, then another, two men working the lot in a pattern too deliberate to be anything but a perimeter check, front doors and back doors and every door in between, the whole building being quietly, methodically wrapped in a net nobody outside it would ever notice tightening.

Taylor watched a waiter get quietly redirected away from the east exit by one of the new men, a brief exchange, a polite nod, the waiter changing course without ever seeming to register he'd been told to. It was, she thought, a small, ugly kind of elegance, the way none of it looked like anything at all unless you already knew exactly what you were watching for.

*"That's a lot of very serious men for a charity gala,"* Gib observed, dry, none of his earlier lightness left in the voice at all.

*"He's not looking for one girl anymore,"* Fury said. *"He's locking the room. Nobody gets to leave until he's satisfied, whatever satisfied looks like to a guy like this."*

Taylor felt it too, before McClane had finished saying it, a shift in the air itself, subtle and total, a room that had quietly, without announcement, become a place nobody was getting out of easily.

*Is he actually going to lock two hundred people in a building?*

*Not obviously. Not all at once.* McClane's voice had the careful, weighing quality of a man building a threat assessment out of a dozen small, ugly pieces. *Guy like this doesn't need a lockdown. He just needs enough eyes on enough doors that leaving looks suspicious, and staying looks normal. Works the same either way. Either the person he's looking for gets nervous and bolts, which flags them immediately, or they stay, which means he's got all night to keep narrowing the field.*

*And if I try to leave?*

Taylor was already moving before he'd finished the question, one step, then another, angled toward the bar, toward Danny, toward some half-formed idea about a headache and an early night and a coat check that suddenly felt like the only sane plan left in the building.

*Stop.* Not loud. Flat, immediate, a word that had clearly stopped better people than her cold in worse rooms than this one. *Right now, this second, you're the one guest who suddenly needs fresh air the second security tightened up. You'd be handing him exactly the read he's fishing for. That door was open five minutes ago. Walking to it now, on your own two feet, in front of everybody, gets you nothing.*

*So what do I do instead.*

*Same play as the horn note. Bigger.* Something sharpened in him, fast, already three steps ahead of the sentence. *Last time you needed thirty seconds and one man's attention. This time you need the whole room looking everywhere at once, so hard that nobody's actually looking at you specifically. You don't walk out the front door. You disappear in the middle of it and let two hundred people do your covering for you.*

*Bigger how.*

*You tell me. You've got a lot more than four bugs to work with now.*

She did. Taylor let the thought settle, cold and clear, and reached for every warmed-up straggler still drifting the edges of the room, a dozen small commotions instead of one, scattered wide, timed close enough together that no single one of them would read as anything but bad luck. A moth against a candle two tables over. Something skittering across a white tablecloth near the bar. A soft, startled shriek from someone by the dessert table who'd felt a wing brush the back of her neck. A man near the mezzanine stairs swatting at his own collar. Another near the coat check stamping his foot at nothing. Nothing dramatic on its own. All of it at once, everywhere, impossible to track.

The room didn't panic. It just turned, all at once, in a dozen directions that weren't Taylor's, a hundred small distractions blooming across the crowd like the room itself had developed a nervous tic. In the half-second of scattered attention that bought her, Taylor didn't just slip sideways into the crowd. Some quiet accounting she'd been keeping without meaning to all night already knew exactly where to go: a linen closet near the kitchen doors, a laundry chute she'd never have found on her own, but her bugs had been living in the walls around it for the better part of an hour, drawn to the dark and the fabric and the warm, undisturbed dust. She reached the closet and started folding herself over the chute's metal lip before the last commotion had finished blooming. It was a route that didn't exist on any floor plan a man in a dark suit would think to watch. If she could get inside it, she would not be harder to spot. She would be gone.

One of the men saw the closet door move.

Taylor felt him through a roach beneath the buffet as he abandoned the false disturbance and cut toward the kitchen. She was inside the closet but not yet inside the chute, one leg over the metal lip, dress caught on a protruding screw.

*He's coming,* McClane said.

She pulled. The fabric held.

Footsteps struck the service tile outside.

Taylor reached back, found the snag by touch, and tore the hem rather than trying to save it. The closet handle turned as she folded both arms over her head and let herself drop.

Light cut across the chute above her. A man's hand caught empty air where her ankle had been.

Then the metal shaft turned, gravity took her around the bend, and the hand disappeared.

*"Bigger, messier, and this time she vanishes instead of getting lucky,"* the gallery said. *"That's growth."*

She landed in a bin of tablecloths at the bottom of the chute, breath knocked half out of her, buried in warm cotton and the smell of bleach, safe, hidden, and utterly alone for the first time in an hour.

She didn't look for Danny again, not yet, not until she'd put real distance between herself and the spot the men in dark suits had last seen her standing. Somewhere above her, faint under a dozen small overlapping commotions, she thought she heard Max Anders' voice go flat for just a second before smoothing back over, a man doing math he very much did not like the answer to.

*It'll be a nice night out,* Taylor thought, into a pile of somebody's used napkins, breathless and half-laughing despite herself, in a cadence that wasn't quite hers, borrowed off a man who'd apparently said some version of this to himself in a much worse duct, a much worse night, a long time before tonight. *Get out of the house, they said. Meet some new people, they said.* She spit out a mouthful of somebody's linen. *God, I hate this job.*

*"Now we're locked in a building with a guy who might kill people,"* Gladly said.

*"That's the job,"* Fury answered. *"Funny right up until it isn't."*

She didn't need her own eyes for the rest of it. A straggler still drifting near the loading dock corridor caught a sixth man taking up position there, the same corridor she'd sent three cold bugs under, hours ago now, in a night that had felt, at the time, like it might still turn out to be nothing.

*McClane.*

*Yeah, kid.*

*He's not going to stop until he finds someone. Is he.*

*No.* Careful, honest, no comfort offered because there wasn't any real comfort to offer. *He's not. Which means the only question left is whether he finds you before you find whatever it is he's actually hiding.*

She found Danny at last through a bug perched near the bar, not her own eyes, laughing at something, easy and unaware, safe for now on the other side of a door she'd just slipped through. Somewhere behind that borrowed sense of him, Taylor heard, for the first time all night, the quiet click of a door being locked, real or imagined, she genuinely couldn't tell anymore, and found she no longer cared which.



Chapter 12: Regroup



They gave it ten minutes before either of them said anything worth saying, ten minutes of Taylor sitting still in a basement laundry room that smelled like bleach and warm cotton, half-buried in a bin of used tablecloths at the bottom of the chute, letting her heartbeat come down out of somewhere near her throat before she trusted herself to climb out and decide what came next.

She did not spend those minutes alone.

Footsteps entered the laundry room less than a minute after she landed. Taylor buried herself beneath the tablecloths, drew the nearest insects into stillness, and listened as two men opened cabinets, checked behind the pressing table, and shone a flashlight into the open mouth of the chute.

One beam swept across the cloth over her face. Light turned the weave bright enough for her to see individual fibers.

"Nothing," one man said.

"She came through here."

"Then she kept moving."

The first man kicked the bin. Taylor's shoulder struck its metal side. She locked her jaw before the sound could escape.

They searched for another three minutes. McClane spoke only once.

*Breathe when they move. Hold when they stop.*

She matched her lungs to their footsteps until the door closed behind them.

*Okay.* McClane's voice had leveled out, the tactical edge from earlier folded back down into something steadier, more patient. *We can't just keep dodging guys in suits all night. Eventually one of them gets lucky, or he stops sending polite ones.*

*So what, we run? Get my dad and just leave?*

*Could. Wouldn't blame you for wanting to.* No judgment in it, just an honest weighing of the option. *But you leave now, with nothing but a hunch and some dead bugs, and he stays exactly what he's been all night. Respected. Untouchable. Free to do this again to somebody else's kid at the next gala he throws, and next time neither of us will be in the room to notice.*

*That's not fair.*

*No,* McClane agreed. *It's not. Doesn't make it less true.*

*"She's not a cop. She's a scared kid,"* Gladly said.

*"She's also the one there,"* Fury answered.

The tablecloth bin made a strange kind of confessional, dark and warm and smelling of bleach, and Taylor let herself sit in it a full minute longer than she needed to, just breathing, just being a girl in a basement instead of whatever she'd been upstairs for the last hour. It didn't last. It never seemed to, lately.

She couldn't remain buried in a laundry bin all night, but leaving the basement would throw away the one position the search had already cleared. Taylor climbed out, brushed lint from her torn dress, and moved the rolling carts until they formed a narrow pocket between the wall and the pressing table. From there her bugs could still reach the dance floor while her body stayed out of the room entirely. *So what do we do instead.*

*We stop playing defense.* Something settled into his voice, a plan starting to take real shape instead of just reacting to the last five minutes. *Right now all we've got is a hallway glance and a guy who's suddenly very interested in finding one anonymous teenager. That's not nothing, but it's not enough to do anything with either. Not enough to tell anybody. Not enough to make a case, if this city's even got anybody left worth making a case to.*

*You think it doesn't.*

*I think a city that lets a guy like this throw a charity gala and call it civic pride has bigger problems than one dirty host.* Flat, matter-of-fact, no heat in it, just the accumulated weariness of thirty years watching institutions move too slow to matter. *So we build something ourselves. Real. Concrete. Something that survives past tonight even if we don't get out of this room clean.*

*"That's what I recruited him for,"* O'Malley said, and the gallery went still around the quiet sentence.

*Did you just call me a protagonist?*

*What? No. Didn't say anything.* A beat, and she felt McClane go carefully, deliberately still, the same stillness he did right before a room stopped making sense to him either. *You hear something?*

*I don't know. Somebody said "protagonist." It wasn't you. It wasn't me either.*

*"Quiet,"* Fury warned the gallery. *"Bleedthrough, not a breach. Let it pass."*

Taylor shook her head, hard, once, the way she'd shake water out of her ear, and the strange, borrowed sense of being watched by something that wasn't McClane and wasn't one of Anders's men either receded back into the general noise of the party, gone as suddenly as it had arrived.

*Build it how? I can send bugs. That's it. That's my whole toolkit.*

*That's not nothing, kid. That's actually a lot, if you point it right.* A pause, and she could feel him laying the shape of it out, piece by piece, the same fast, blunt cataloging he did with rooms, now turned on a problem instead of a space. *You can go places a camera can't. You can listen where a microphone would get found. You want evidence on a guy like this, you don't need a smoking gun, you need patterns. Who he talks to. Who he avoids. What rooms he clears before he'll say anything real out loud.* Something in his voice eased, just slightly, an idea landing that he clearly liked better than the last twenty minutes of them both standing out in the open. *And you don't have to be the one standing there watching it happen. Stay somewhere quiet, somewhere nobody's got a reason to look for a nervous teenager, and let the bugs do the actual work. You're not the one who needs to be brave tonight. They are.*

*What am I actually looking for.*

*Whatever doesn't fit.* He said it like it was obvious, and maybe, to a man with thirty years of practice, it was. *A room full of people is a room full of stories that are all supposed to make sense together. Rich people at a charity gala, staff working an event, a host being a host. You're looking for the piece that's telling a different story than everybody else. Might be a door that gets checked too often. Might be a guy who doesn't quite fit his own uniform. You'll know it when you see it, same as you knew that hallway glance meant something before you could've told me why.*

*And if we find something?*

*Then we've got leverage instead of a hunch. Leverage is a different animal. Leverage you can use.* Something almost like anticipation crept into the flatness, the energy of a man who'd spent thirty years building cases out of exactly this kind of patient, unglamorous work. *We're not trying to beat him tonight. We're trying to make sure tonight isn't the only night this ever mattered.*

Taylor watched the room for a long moment. Danny remained near the bar because the event coordinator had asked him to wait there while staff checked the restrooms for her. His drink sat untouched. Every thirty seconds his attention moved from the kitchen doors to the main entrance to the stairs, searching. The men in dark suits remained spread along the walls like furniture nobody had asked for, and Max Anders stood somewhere in the crowd wearing a smile that had stopped, hours ago now, meaning anything at all. It was strange how quickly a room could stop looking like a party and start looking like a battlefield with better lighting, and stranger still how easily she'd made that switch herself, somewhere in the last two hours, without ever quite noticing the exact moment it happened. Two hundred people still moved through it, still laughed, still balanced canapés on little napkins, entirely unaware that the building around them had quietly become something closer to a chessboard, every one of them a piece whose position mattered to somebody, whether they knew it or not.

Taylor found a laundry inventory clipboard and turned the last page over. She drew the atrium first, then the kitchen, service corridor, loading dock, main doors, east doors, and the staircase. McClane corrected proportions when her memory compressed distances. Taylor added the chute and the basement rooms her bugs could reach.

*You draw every room you know,* he said. *Then you mark every room you don't. Unknown space is where plans go to die.*

The blank rectangle behind the locked service door became the largest question on the page.

*"Gib, you got anything useful, or are you just gonna keep making Whitman jokes,"* Fury said, dry.

*"I contain layers,"* Gib said. *"Some of them are useful. This isn't one of the useful ones."*

*"Shocking."*

*Okay,* Taylor said, and felt something steady settle into her chest, not confidence exactly, but the shape of it, close enough to work with. *Where do we start.*

*Same place every good case starts.* McClane's voice had gone almost warm, the satisfaction of a man handing a rookie their first real assignment. *We watch. Careful, quiet, patient. And we let him think he's still the only one in this room who knows what's actually going on.*



Chapter 13: Radar Contact



The laundry room made a decent office, once she stopped thinking of it as somewhere she'd fallen and started thinking of it as somewhere nobody would think to look for her twice.

Taylor settled into the gap between a rolling cart of folded napkins and the wall, knees pulled up, and let the real work start. Not the dramatic kind. Not diversions, not stings, not anything McClane would have recognized from thirty years of doorway standoffs. Just patience, and a few dozen bugs spread thin across a building that had, over the course of one very long night, stopped feeling unfamiliar to her at all.

Patience did not come as easily to Danny.

Through insects above the atrium, Taylor watched him wait the five minutes the event coordinator requested. At six, he crossed to the women's restroom and asked a nurse leaving it whether a tall teenage girl in a green dress was inside. At eight, he returned to the coat check and demanded both coats again. At ten, he found the coordinator and made her stop walking while he spoke.

"She's not in either restroom."

"We have staff checking the second floor, Mr. Hebert. Teenagers do wander at these events."

"Taylor doesn't."

The coordinator softened her face. "I understand you're worried."

"Then stop understanding and find her."

Two men near the main doors watched the exchange without appearing to. The coordinator touched Danny's forearm and guided him back toward the bar, away from the exits and the service corridor. She promised an announcement if Taylor had not returned in another ten minutes.

*I should tell him where I am.*

*How?* McClane asked. *No phone. No clean messenger. Any bug trick you use gets reported straight to Anders, and a note means putting your body back where they're hunting it.*

*He's scared.*

*Yeah. And right now scared has him standing under lights with two hundred witnesses. We can hate the cost without pretending the alternative is safer.*

Taylor marked Danny on the back of the laundry clipboard anyway. Not because she could forget where he stood. Because every plan from here forward had to include the route between them.

*Slow,* McClane said, settling into something closer to a coach's voice than a cop's. *You're not looking for one big thing. You're looking for a shape. Patterns don't announce themselves. They just show up, over and over, until you notice you've seen them before.*

*So what am I actually watching for.*

*Who he talks to without smiling first. That's rule one. Everybody at this party gets the smile. Not everybody gets the version underneath it.*

She worked outward in rings, the way she'd learned to build a perimeter, starting close and widening slowly: the atrium first, then the service corridors, then the kitchen and the loading dock and, eventually, careful and patient, a scattering of bugs finding their way up the grand staircase to the mezzanine level itself, into a lounge where the party's more important guests had been quietly siphoned off for a second, smaller gathering all night. It took the better part of forty minutes, spread thin and careful across the whole ground floor and half the second, before the shape started to show itself. A man near the coat check, the same build as the two who'd tried the clipboard trick, getting a private nod from Anders that lasted exactly as long as it needed to and not one second longer. A woman near the kitchen doors, sharp-eyed, unsmiling, who Anders crossed the room twice to speak with briefly, low, out of anyone's earshot but a bug's. A locked door off the service corridor that three different men had checked, separately, in the last half hour, unhurried each time, the check of men confirming a perimeter rather than looking for a bathroom.

*He's got at least six people here who aren't caterers and aren't guests,* Taylor thought, cataloging it the way McClane had taught her to without quite noticing she'd started doing it his way. *Maybe more. All of them checking in with him separately. All of them acting normal everywhere else.*

*That's not security for a charity gala.* McClane's voice had gone flat again, certain, the weight of a man watching a threat assessment solidify into something he could actually use. *That's a crew. Organized. Disciplined. The kind of discipline that doesn't come from a rent-a-cop agency, it comes from people who've worked together before, under worse conditions than a chocolate fountain.*

*Worse how.*

*I don't know yet. That's the next question.*

There were smaller things too, details that wouldn't have meant anything on their own, that only added up to a shape once she had enough of them stacked together. The way certain guests, three of them, four, got a slightly warmer welcome from Anders than their apparent importance seemed to justify, men and women who didn't run companies or sit on hospital boards, as far as she could tell, but who Anders greeted like colleagues rather than donors. The way the bartender, of all people, kept glancing toward the locked door every few minutes, quick and involuntary, like a man checking a clock he wasn't supposed to be watching. None of it proved anything. All of it fit.

The mezzanine lounge, once she got a bug close enough to actually listen, turned out to be its own small ecosystem entirely, a dozen well-dressed men and women who never quite mingled with the party below, conversations pitched low, body language stiff not from formalwear but from the effort of looking off duty. She counted faces twice, cross-referenced against the ones circulating downstairs, and came up with at least four who never appeared in both places at once, a rotation, careful and deliberate, someone always watching from above even while the party below carried on as if nothing up there existed at all.

*You're building a case,* McClane said, something like approval threaded through the observation. *Real one. Not evidence a court would take, not yet, but the kind of thing an investigator builds a case out of before the evidence ever shows up. You're doing this right.*

*I don't feel like I'm doing anything. I'm just sitting in a laundry room.*

*That's most of the job, actually. Nobody tells you that part in the movies.*

The locked door off the service corridor was the one that mattered most, and Taylor spent ten careful minutes working a single bug under the gap at its base, patient, slow, past a draft that smelled like cold concrete and old paper. Voices, muffled, two of them. Anders's, unmistakable now after a night of hearing it work a room. A second, rougher, unfamiliar, calling him something that wasn't his name and wasn't quite a title either.

The insect could not see, but sight was only one way to construct a room.

Its feet found sealed concrete instead of carpet. The temperature was lower than the corridor. Air carried machine oil, cut foam, metal, and a sharp chemical odor McClane recognized before Taylor did.

*Explosives,* he said.

*You can smell explosives through a fly?*

*I can remember what a room full of demolition gear smelled like, and you're giving me enough pieces to hate the resemblance.*

Taylor moved the fly beneath a shelving unit. Large cases stood against the far wall, their weight bowing the lowest shelf. Coiled wire hung above them. A man crossed the floor carrying something rectangular in both hands and set it into foam with careful precision.

Behind the cases stood two fireproof document cabinets on low steel dollies. Their manufacturer plates had been covered with black tape. A portable machine sat open on top of one, cables arranged beside a row of empty drive cases. Taylor did not know what it copied. She knew it had been positioned to move.

*Explosives and office equipment,* McClane said. *That's two different jobs sharing one locked room.*

This was not a private office. It was equipment storage.

Prepared equipment storage.

*Boss.*

*That's it. That's what he called him.* Taylor kept her breathing even, careful, the same discipline she'd been using all night to keep from talking to herself out loud in a room full of people. *Not Mr. Anders. Not sir. Boss.*

The rougher voice said something else after that, too low and too fast for the single bug to catch cleanly, and then a second voice, higher, tighter, clearly reporting rather than conversing, the cadence of someone giving an update to a man who didn't like being kept waiting for one. Taylor caught fragments only. *Handled. Contained.* A word that might have been *reporter*, might have been something else entirely, gone before she could be sure.

"Archive credential arrives through the dock," the higher voice said. "Transfer team can begin as soon as the room clears."

"It will arrive," Anders said. "No transfer until the observer is identified."

"Exterior teams are set," the higher voice said more clearly. "Door crews can close in ninety seconds."

"Not until we identify the surveillance," Anders answered.

"We can proceed without it."

The silence that followed lasted long enough to feel punitive.

"No," Anders said. "We cannot. An unknown observer turns a controlled operation into evidence. Find the observer. Then we proceed."

Taylor pressed both hands against the laundry-room floor.

The party had not become dangerous because she and McClane noticed Anders. Whatever waited inside those cases had been placed before Danny's borrowed sedan reached the valet stand.

They had not created the plan.

They had become the one piece Kaiser could not account for inside it.

*"There it is,"* the gallery said. *"A respectable charity host does not get called Boss beside demolition gear."*

*Good.* McClane's voice had gone very quiet, very careful, a man handling something he didn't want to spook. *That's exactly the kind of thing I meant. Not a confession. Not a smoking gun. Just one word, in the wrong room, from the wrong guy, that doesn't fit the story he's telling everybody else tonight.*

*It's not enough, though. Is it.*

*No.* Honest, the way he'd been honest all night, no comfort dressed up as reassurance. *One word's a data point. You need more than one before it's a pattern, and more than a pattern before it's something you can actually use. But it's a start, kid. A real one. First one you built yourself, start to finish, without me telling you where to look.*

Taylor let the bug ease back from the gap under the door, slow, careful, and called the rest of her scattered net home in the same unhurried rhythm, gathering pieces of a night that had started as a party and was quietly, steadily, becoming something else entirely.

She sat with it for a moment, alone in the dark with a dozen small, disconnected facts and the growing certainty that they were all going to add up to something she wasn't going to like. Somewhere above her, muffled through a ceiling and a chute and however many feet of building, the string quartet started up again, bright and oblivious, playing for a room that had no idea it was sitting on top of any of this.

*"I love this chapter,"* Gladly said.

*"You say that every chapter,"* Fury answered.



Chapter 14: Wind Shear



The music stopped for the second time that night, and this time nobody had to tell Taylor it wasn't an accident.

Max Anders climbed back onto the little stage, and whatever composure the horn player had clawed back over the last hour, he lost it again just watching his host approach, flinching like a man who'd been personally involved in something he'd rather not have Anders think too hard about.

*Here we go again,* McClane said, already tight, already reading the room a half-second ahead of the words. *Watch his hands this time, not just his face.*

"Folks, I'm so sorry to interrupt again." Anders's voice carried the same easy warmth as before, but there was something underneath it now, thinner, stretched further than it had been an hour ago. "It seems we've had a small security matter come up, nothing to worry about, but I'm going to ask everyone to stay in the main hall for just a little while longer. My team's going to do a quick walkthrough, purely routine, and then we'll get right back to the evening."

*"That's not routine,"* Gib said, flat, all the earlier dryness gone out of him. *"That's a sweep. He's about to have his people walk this room guest by guest."*

*He can't do that.* Taylor's breath had gone thin, careful, even from the safety of the laundry room, hearing it happen one floor up through nothing but a single straggler bug's borrowed sense of the room. *Two hundred people. He can't just search two hundred people at his own charity gala.*

*He's not searching two hundred people.* McClane's voice had the flat, fast clarity of a man watching a plan take a shape he recognized. *He's watching two hundred people get searched, and cataloging who looks nervous about it. That's not security theater, that's a net closing slow enough that nobody screams about it until it's already closed.*

*What do I do.*

*Nothing, yet. You're not in that room. That's the one good thing to come out of you being down here.* A beat, weighing it. *But your dad is.*

Taylor's stomach dropped, cold and total, the laundry room's bleach smell suddenly sharper than it had been a second ago. *Danny.*

*He's not a target. Not specifically. But he's about to get patted down by a guy pretending to be a rent-a-cop, same as everybody else in that room, and he doesn't know what kind of wrong he's standing in. People working from the wrong picture ask dangerous questions at dangerous moments.*

Taylor was moving before she'd finished deciding to, up out of the gap between the cart and the wall, toward the chute, toward the stairs, toward a room she'd worked so hard to disappear from an hour ago.

*Slow down.* Not a command this time, something closer to a hand on her shoulder. *You go up there panicking, you undo everything the last hour bought you. Think. What do you actually need to happen.*

*I need my dad to be fine.*

*He will be, if you let the sweep do what it's built to do, which is find nothing on a union rep who showed up with an invitation and a clean conscience.* McClane's voice had gone steady, deliberate, walking her back from the edge of something neither of them wanted to name outright. *What you need is eyes in that room without your body in it. You've still got bugs up there. Use them. Watch him get searched from a safe distance instead of walking back into the one room where showing up flustered actually gets you noticed.*

Taylor stopped at the base of the chute, one hand on cold metal, and made herself breathe, slow, the way she'd been making herself breathe all night. Her whole body still wanted to climb, every instinct she had screaming at her to be in the same room as her father right now, close enough to actually do something if something went wrong instead of watching it happen through a bug's flat, distant sense of the world.

*I know,* McClane said, quiet, like he'd heard the want in it even without her saying anything. *I know exactly what that feels like. Doesn't make it the wrong call to stay put.*

*Okay.* She closed her eyes, reached, found the handful of stragglers still drifting near the bar, and settled them, carefully, into a loose ring around where Danny stood, close enough to warn her, not close enough to be noticed by anyone looking for exactly that kind of thing. *Okay. I'm watching.*

The sweep took eleven minutes, unhurried, thorough, six men in dark suits working the room in loose, overlapping patterns that never quite looked like a search unless you already knew what a search looked like. Taylor watched it happen across a dozen borrowed vantage points at once, a mosaic of small moments stitched together into one long, unbearable eleven minutes: a woman in a red dress laughing off a search of her clutch, an elderly man patting his own pockets helpfully before he was even asked, the horn player himself still visibly rattled from earlier, going stiff and pale the second one of the suited men approached his little stage. Two hundred people, one at a time, each of them a small, separate held breath she couldn't do anything about except watch. They started at the edges and worked inward, polite, apologetic, reciting a practiced script about insurance requirements and unfortunate necessity. The performance would have satisfied Taylor completely an hour ago. Now, through borrowed bug-eyes, it read as rehearsed as everything else tonight.

They were not looking for a weapon. Taylor understood that after the fourth handbag.

Weapons received a glance. Phones received attention. Each man checked recent calls, camera screens, unsent messages, and the warmth of the device before returning it. At the silent-auction table, one of them opened a donor's text conversation and scrolled far enough to earn an offended protest. He apologized, blamed a signal-interference concern, and watched the protester rather than the phone.

They were looking for communication leaving the building.

One guest refused.

He was a defense attorney Taylor recognized from television, silver-haired and loud enough that people made space for his voice by habit. When the suited man asked for his phone, he held it against his chest and demanded to know under whose authority a private security contractor intended to search privileged communications.

Conversation died around him in widening rings.

Anders stepped down from the stage before the confrontation could become a scene. "Gerald, you're absolutely right," he said, warm and apologetic. "This has been handled poorly. Nobody is entitled to your private messages."

Relief moved through the nearby guests. The attorney lowered the phone.

Anders held out his hand. "Let me keep it secure until the walkthrough is finished. Personally. Then there can be no question of anyone accessing it."

The attorney surrendered the device to the host he trusted.

Anders passed it behind his back without looking. The suited man carried it away.

*He just won the argument and got the phone,* Taylor thought.

*That's why he's the guy on stage and the others are carrying guns under their jackets.*

The search expanded into the service levels while the guest sweep continued. A pair of men checked the kitchen, dry storage, and staff lockers. Another descended the basement stairs.

Taylor felt him through insects in the cinderblock wall.

*He's coming back to laundry.*

*Then we make laundry look searched,* McClane said.

She left the bin overturned from the first visit. She scattered tablecloths toward the opposite door, opened a supply cabinet, and sent roaches moving down the corridor beyond it. Then she climbed onto the pressing table, pushed a ceiling tile aside, and folded herself into the narrow space above it.

The man entered thirty seconds later.

He followed the false trail through the laundry room, saw the open cabinet and disturbed cloth, and continued toward the mechanical corridor. Taylor lay across two steel supports above his head, dust against her face, one hand clamped over her mouth while the ceiling tile bowed beneath her hip.

He stopped in the doorway.

Turned.

Looked up.

A spider crossed the light fixture. Taylor sent it down on a thread past his face.

He swore, batted it away, and left.

Taylor remained above the ceiling until her arms began to tremble.

Danny stood where the coordinator had repeatedly returned him, no longer patient and not remotely confused. He submitted to the light, professional pat-down because refusing would cost time he wanted spent finding Taylor. He even made a joke about hoping they'd at least buy him dinner first, an automatic line deployed without humor, and the man's professional smile didn't crack, not even a little, which told Taylor more about him than anything he'd actually done with his hands.

"Phone," the man said.

Danny handed it over.

The screen showed three missed calls, all from the same number. Their home phone. Taylor had not made them.

Danny frowned. "That's odd."

The suited man's thumb stopped above the call log. "Expecting someone?"

"My daughter came with me. Nobody should be home."

Taylor felt the trap open beneath a sentence Danny had no reason not to say.

"Where is your daughter now?"

Danny looked around the room. His gaze found the coordinator, the main doors, and the kitchen in a sequence Taylor had already watched him repeat six times.

"Bathroom, probably." He took the phone back before the man offered it. "She's fifteen. Keeping me informed isn't high on the list."

The joke sounded better than the first one because Danny wanted it believed. He knew Taylor was not in either bathroom. He had been asking staff to find her for nearly an hour. The lie came without preparation, offered to a stranger who had become too interested in where his daughter was.

The suited man touched his earpiece as he moved away.

*They made those calls,* Taylor thought.

*Yeah. Wanted to see who noticed, who looked for somebody missing, who gave them a relationship they could trace.*

*And Dad just gave them me.*

*No. They already suspected. Your dad just told them he doesn't trust the question. He lied for you without knowing why. Keep that distinction. It matters.*

Outside, through the tall windows, Taylor caught the slow, deliberate sweep of a flashlight crossing the parking structure again, and this time a second beam joined it, the two of them converging near the loading dock before the transmission crackled once, brief and low, and both lights winked out at the same moment, satisfied with whatever they'd found. Or hadn't.

*He's fine,* McClane said, once the man moved on to the next guest. *Told you.*

*This time.*

*This time,* McClane agreed, no false comfort in it. *Which means we're out of this room's worth of patience. Whatever we're building, kid, we're building it faster now. Not because I want to rush you. Because he's not gonna give us a slow night twice.*

*"There it is,"* the gallery said, all lightness gone. *"The clock was always running. Now everybody in the room knows it."*



Chapter 15: Center of Gravity



Taylor almost missed him entirely, one more suit in a room full of them, if it hadn't been for the notebook.

She half-recognized him once she looked twice. Marcus Thorne, city desk, the byline under half the breathless crime stories the Brockton Bay Bulletin ran on a slow news week.

Nobody carried an actual paper notebook to a charity gala anymore, not unless they wanted people to notice they were working, and this man wanted exactly that, weaving through the crowd with the hungry energy of somebody circling a story he hadn't quite landed yet. She'd clocked him twice already tonight without registering it, once near the bar, once hovering at the edge of Anders's toast, always close enough to overhear, never quite close enough to be part of the conversation.

*Reporter,* McClane said, flat, the second she thought to ask. *Local paper, probably. The notebook's a tell. Everybody else in that room's got a phone in their pocket. He wants people to see him writing something down.*

*Is that bad?*

*Depends what he's writing.*

*He's been talking to caterers all night. Kitchen staff. That woman from the coat check.* Taylor watched him work the edges of the room with patience that almost looked professional if you didn't know better. *Building something. I don't know what.*

*Guys like that build one of two things. A puff piece about a rich man's generosity, or something with teeth. Given how much shoe leather he's burning on the help instead of the guests, I'd bet on teeth.*

For ten minutes, Taylor considered making him useful.

Thorne had the things she lacked: an adult face, a recognizable name, and an institution that answered when he called. He had spent the night asking questions. Questions were closer to belief than anything Taylor had received from security or the hospital administrator.

She followed him through her swarm while he interviewed a dishwasher near the kitchen doors. He began kindly, asked how long the man had worked for MedHall, laughed at a complaint about overtime, then circled toward security. Had the extra personnel arrived tonight? Did Max Anders usually inspect the loading dock himself? Had anyone been told not to use the east exit?

The dishwasher answered until he realized the answers formed a story. Then he looked toward the nearest man in a dark suit and remembered he needed to return to work.

Thorne wrote down the retreat too.

*He sees it,* Taylor thought.

*He sees something,* McClane corrected.

*If I tell him about the locked door and the word “Boss,” he can call the police. He can put it in the paper.*

*Maybe. Or he can walk straight to Anders and ask for comment because that's what reporters do before they print an accusation.*

*You don't trust anyone.*

*I trust people to do the job they think they're doing. His job is getting a story with his name on it. Your safety comes somewhere after that, and he doesn't know you well enough to feel bad about the order.*

Taylor watched Thorne close his notebook when a donor approached and reopen it the instant the donor left.

She decided not to contact him.

The decision should have protected her.

It was bad.

Taylor watched, borrowed and distant through a straggler bug drifting the edge of the crowd, as the reporter angled himself toward Anders with the boldness of a man who'd decided tonight was the night he stopped being polite about getting his story. He caught Anders between conversations, low, urgent, a hand half-extended like he was offering something instead of asking for it.

*I need to get closer. I can't hear anything from here.*

*Don't.* McClane's voice cracked through sharp, immediate. *You get one bug close enough to actually hear that conversation, and if Anders's people are running any kind of counter-surveillance sweep right now, which after tonight I'd bet money they are, you just handed them exactly what they've been hunting for all night.*

*Then how do I know what he's telling him?*

*You watch his face. And you watch Anders's.*

Taylor sent the bug anyway.

Not directly. She moved it along the underside of the bar, crossed behind a row of bottles, and settled beneath a folded cocktail napkin six feet from the conversation. Close enough for fragments. Far enough that finding it would not point toward her body.

McClane felt the choice and did not waste time arguing after it had been made.

"...girl near the loading dock," Thorne said. "Tall, dark hair, green dress. Asked security about Anders meeting someone. Then disappeared right when your people started circulating."

He spoke to Anders's chief of events rather than Anders himself, but Anders stood close enough to hear while appearing absorbed in another conversation.

"And you believe this concerns Mr. Anders?" the events man asked.

"I believe it concerns unusual activity at his gala. I believe the Hebert girl saw something. Her father runs the Dockworkers Association, which makes the loading dock angle play beautifully if there's a labor story underneath it." Thorne tapped his notebook against his palm. "I give you the courtesy of a warning before I ask her on record. You give me ten minutes with Max after the speeches."

The events man did not look surprised by Taylor's name. "On what subject?"

"The state review. Community-outreach contracting. Whatever has your temporary waiters guarding fire doors while the real ones hide in the kitchen." Thorne smiled as though the line were charming. "I'm offering Max a chance to get in front of it."

"And if there is no story?"

"Then ten minutes costs him nothing."

"If there is?"

Thorne rolled the notebook once through his fingers. "Then he gets to decide which version of his answer I print first."

He was not demanding truth. He was selling placement.

It was not a source traded for a byline. Taylor had never spoken to him. It was a child traded for access before she could become a source at all.

The events man looked toward Anders.

Anders did not turn. He lifted his champagne glass and touched one finger to its stem.

The events man smiled. "I'm sure Mr. Anders will make time."

Thorne relaxed.

Thorne added something about the girl repeatedly circling the service side of the building. His gesture drew the route in the air: loading dock, kitchen, mezzanine, then back toward the atrium.

"She approached building security after Mr. Anders left the loading corridor," Thorne said. "Security sent her away. Then your staff tried to pull her out of the room twice, and she disappeared. Her father has spent the last hour asking where she went."

The events man's professional smile became very still.

Thorne mistook recognition for interest. "That's the spine of it. Respected labor leader brings daughter to transparency gala. Daughter sees something at the loading dock. Host's private security isolates her. If it's nothing, Max says so on record and I kill the implication."

*He saw the whole chain,* Taylor thought.

*Yeah,* McClane said. *Just never asked which person in it needed protecting.*

Anders's eyes moved once, sharp and fast, toward the bar.

Toward Danny.

*No.* Taylor's whole borrowed sense of the room went cold at once. *He gave them my dress. My hair. My dad's name.*

*Yeah,* McClane said. Anger stripped every trace of humor from the word. *He gave them enough.*

Thorne kept talking, oblivious, delighted with himself, a man who thought he'd traded a rumor for an exclusive and had no idea what he had handed over. Anders nodded once, slow and thoughtful, then stepped out of his neighboring conversation as though he had only just noticed the reporter waiting.

"Marcus," Anders said warmly. "I'm told you've been doing excellent work tonight."

Thorne shook Anders's hand, pumped it twice like a man closing a deal, and Taylor felt something ugly and helpless twist in her chest. He walked away pleased with himself, already composing the story, never knowing what he had done to a fifteen-year-old girl he'd never met and a father whose only mistake had been trusting the wrong host.

*"You traded a child for access,"* the gallery said, the movie-review distance gone. *"You never even asked what it would cost her."*

*He gave him me.*

*He gave him you,* McClane said. No comfort, because none remained to offer. *Anders just stopped looking at two hundred people and started looking at one family.*

Anders waited until Thorne had been absorbed by the crowd. Then he handed his untouched glass to the events man.

"The father?"

"At the bar. He has been asking staff to locate her."

"The girl?"

"No confirmed sighting since the dance-floor diversion. Laundry and mechanical levels were searched."

"Not well enough."

The events man accepted the judgment without defending his people. "Do you want the room closed?"

Anders looked toward the auction coordinator wheeling the state-review folder away from the display. The credential had not arrived. The guests still believed inconvenience was the worst thing happening to them. Every second of delay protected the operation's appearance and gave the unknown observer another second to transmit what she knew.

"Quietly," Anders said. "Move the father where we can keep him visible. The girl is alive until I say otherwise."

The events man touched his earpiece.

Three men answered by peeling away from their posts along the walls without any visible signal, the whole machinery of the building reorienting itself around a single point the way a compass needle swings north.

Anders turned. He wasn't looking toward the service corridor, the loading dock, or any of the doors his people had spent the last hour watching.

He was looking, slow and direct and entirely unhurried, toward Danny Hebert. Danny stood alone at the bar with a drink he'd barely touched, pretending to listen to the coordinator's newest assurance while his eyes kept searching for Taylor. He knew his daughter was missing. He did not know that fact had just made him the single most important person in the room.

*McClane.*

*Yeah, kid.*

*He knows.*

*Yeah.* Flat, honest, no version of this that came out easier however he said it. *He knows.*

Taylor stayed very still in the narrow pocket behind the laundry cart and watched, through a bug she could barely bring herself to keep steady, as Max Anders crossed the room toward her father with the same warm, unhurried walk he'd used all night, like nothing in the world had changed, like he wasn't about to have a conversation that could end with Danny Hebert understanding, for the first time, exactly what kind of building he'd brought his daughter into.

*What do I do.* It came out smaller than she meant it to, none of the careful composure she'd been holding onto for the last two hours left in it at all. *McClane. What do I do.*

*You breathe.* Steady, deliberate, a man building a floor under her one word at a time because there wasn't anything else left to build with yet. *You breathe, and you let me think, and you trust that I've been in worse rooms than this one and walked more people out of them than you'd believe. We're not out of moves. We're just out of time to be careful about which ones we make.*



Chapter 16: Blades



Max Anders never made it all the way to Danny.

Taylor watched it happen through three separate bugs, three separate angles, and even with all of them stitched together the moment still didn't quite make sense to her at first: Anders slowing, then stopping, then turning, unhurried, toward the small stage where the quartet had gone quiet again, like a man who'd just remembered a more urgent appointment.

*He's not going for your dad.* McClane's voice had gone tight, fast, recalculating in real time. *He's stepping back. Why's he stepping back.*

*"Because he doesn't need to do it himself,"* Gib said, and for the first time all night there was no dryness in it at all, nothing but flat, cold recognition. *"Watch the doors."*

The men in dark suits moved.

Not all at once, not obviously, but in a pattern that made Taylor's stomach drop even before she understood what she was looking at: six men, then eight, then more still filtering in from the service corridors and the mezzanine stairs, converging on the main hall from a dozen different points at once, and as they moved, something about the way they moved changed too, the careful, unremarkable stillness they'd worn all night sloughing off like a coat nobody needed anymore. It was, she thought distantly, the exact opposite of everything she'd watched Anders do all evening, no warmth, no performance, just cold, practiced coordination, a machine built for a very different kind of party revealing itself all at once.

The nearest one's hands weren't hands anymore.

*What is that.* Taylor's voice had gone thin, disbelieving, watching metal ripple and reshape itself across a man's fingers, a spine of jagged silver blooming where knuckles should have been. *McClane, what am I looking at.*

*I don't know.* Flat, honest, the first time all night she'd heard him admit it outright. *That's not anything I've got a file for. That's not anything I've got a category for. But whatever it is, it's not a rent-a-cop anymore, and I need you to trust me when I tell you that changes everything about the next five minutes.*

*"Hookwolf,"* the gallery said, hushed. *"And Cricket by the east doors. From her stance, she's already clocked every exit in the room better than Taylor ever could."*

Another of the suited men had gone very still near the coat check, a stillness that had nothing relaxed in it at all, head cocked at an angle that suggested he was listening to something none of the ordinary guests could hear, a long, curved blade unfolding from somewhere at his hip like it had always been there, waiting.

A third, closer to the bar, went perfectly white all at once, skin and hair and even the fabric of his suit bleaching out to the same flat, colorless pallor, and Taylor felt McClane's attention snag hard on that one specifically, some old cop instinct clocking a threat display even without a name to put on it.

*That one's not even trying to look human anymore.* Something in his voice had gone very careful, very controlled, a man doing math on a threat board that kept adding new columns faster than he could total them. *Whatever the rules are in your world, I don't think he's planning on following polite society's version of them tonight.*

Nearby, two enormous women who hadn't been part of the earlier count of suits at all simply stood up from a table, guests apparently, until they weren't, height stretching impossibly as evening gowns tore at the seams, and the low murmur of confused party chatter finally, belatedly, started curdling into something closer to actual panic.

Taylor's swarm, without her quite telling it to, pulled back from the room in a single flinching ripple, old animal instinct recognizing a predator's silhouette faster than her conscious mind could catch up to it.

Somewhere in the crowd a woman screamed, short and sharp and immediately swallowed, and that seemed to be the signal the rest of the room had been waiting for without knowing it, two hundred people remembering all at once that a charity gala wasn't supposed to look like this, glasses hitting the floor, chairs scraping back, a slow, ugly current of bodies starting to push toward exits that Taylor already knew, with cold certainty, were no longer actually open.

The chandelier over the dance floor swayed, just slightly, as the current of fleeing bodies crashed against the tables beneath it, crystal chiming against crystal in a bright, discordant music that had nothing to do with the quartet, and for one wild, useless second Taylor found herself doing the math again, tonnage and cable tension, wondering whether tonight was when the calculation finally mattered.

*Danny,* she thought, and the word came out smaller and more frightened than anything she'd let herself feel all night. *Where's my dad. I can't see him. I can't find him in this.*

*He's fine. He's exactly where he was.* McClane's voice, impossibly, had gone steadier instead of less, an anchor thrown deliberately into the panic. *A stampede's actually good for him right now, kid. Nobody's got time to focus on a single union rep when two hundred rich people are trying to get through the same door at once. Ugly as that sounds, it's cover. Use it.*

*They're parahumans.* It wasn't a question. Saying it out loud, even silently, made it realer, worse, a fact she couldn't unknow now that she'd named it. *He's not just some guy with a security detail. He's got capes working for him.*

*Capes.* McClane turned the word over like he was checking it for a blade of its own. *That the word for it in your world?*

*That's the word.*

*Then yeah. He's got capes. Question is how many, and I'm not liking the math I'm doing so far.*

The quartet's stage lights cut out.

It happened all at once, no flicker, no warning, the whole small platform going dark except for the pale gleam of the horn player's abandoned instrument, and in the sudden half-dark that followed, Taylor felt, more than saw, the room's mood shift, two hundred guests going from confused to afraid in the space of a single held breath.

"Ladies and gentlemen." Max Anders's voice, still warm, still perfectly pitched to carry, and underneath it now, unmistakable, something colder than anything he'd let show all night. "I'm so sorry for the disruption. It seems we do have an actual security concern after all, and I'm going to need everyone to remain exactly where they are while my people handle it."

The main doors locked with two heavy impacts.

At the east exit, a man drove the waiting steel pin through its floor socket. Another rolled the silent-auction display across the kitchen corridor. The route remained technically open, but only as a shoulder-wide gap beneath Alabaster's colorless stare.

The men who had spent the evening carrying trays opened their jackets and produced compact weapons from harnesses beneath them. The transformation was less dramatic than Hookwolf's blades and more frightening for being ordinary. Safety levers moved. Stocks unfolded. Muzzles selected sections of the crowd.

"Phones on the floor," the man near the sound board ordered. His voice came through the atrium speakers, stripped flat by an electronic filter. "Hands visible. Move toward the center aisle and sit down. Anyone approaching an exit will be treated as a threat to the other guests."

Nobody obeyed immediately.

Then Hookwolf put one bladed arm through a banquet table.

Wood, china, and white linen collapsed beneath the strike. The sound ended the argument the room had not yet begun having. Guests lowered themselves among broken glasses and overturned chairs. A few kept hold of their phones until armed men began collecting them into champagne buckets.

Anders stepped down from the stage with both palms raised. One of the gunmen seized his shoulder and pushed him into the first row of hostages.

The movement looked rough. Taylor, watching through a fly on the man's sleeve, felt the grip loosen before it landed. Anders went to one knee without losing balance and turned immediately to help the councilwoman beside him sit.

Host and hostage in the same motion.

*He's inside the crowd now,* McClane said. *Every camera sees a victim. Every gunman sees the boss.*

Danny did not sit.

An older man had fallen near the bar when the doors locked. Danny pushed through two rows of chairs, crouched beside him, and pressed a napkin against the cut above his eye.

"Sir," one of the gunmen said.

"He's bleeding."

"Sit down."

Danny looked at the weapon, then at the injured man. "Help me move him and I will."

For one suspended second, Taylor watched her father negotiate with a gun the same way he negotiated with shipping managers: as though the other side remained a person who could be embarrassed into behaving correctly.

Anders raised his voice from the floor. "Let him help. Panic will do more damage than cooperation."

The gunman looked toward the sound-board operator. The operator looked at Anders. Anders's hand rested flat against the floor, two fingers bent beneath the palm.

"Move him," the operator said.

Danny did, unaware that the man granting permission sat among the hostages wearing Max Anders's face.

Taylor sent a moth to the back of Danny's collar. She could not speak through it. She could not make him understand. But the small living point gave her his position inside the crowd, steady and exact.

It was all she could give him without giving herself away.

*"There it is,"* the gallery said, quiet, grim. *"No more pretending. He's not performing host anymore. That's the mask coming off, right there, in real time."*

*Taylor.* McClane's voice cut through the rising noise in her own head, sharp, urgent, an anchor. *I need you to hear me. You are not fighting whatever that metal one is. Not tonight. Not with what you've got. This isn't the fight, this is the moment before the fight, and the entire job right now is making sure there's a later for you to actually have it in.*

*Danny's still in there.*

*I know.* No comfort in it, none available to give. *And getting to him means getting through people who can probably kill you in about four seconds if you do this wrong. So we're not doing this wrong. We're doing this smart, or we're not doing it at all.*

Across the darkened hall, the one Gib had called Cricket turned, slow, deliberate, her head tracking something Taylor couldn't see, and for one long, terrible second, Taylor was certain the woman was looking directly at the exact spot where a straggler bug sat perched on a pillar, watching.

*She can't actually see the bug,* McClane said, fast, though his own certainty had a thin edge to it now. *Can she?*

*I don't know. I don't know what her power does. I don't know what any of their powers do.*

*Then we don't test it. Pull back. All of it. Right now.*

Taylor called her whole scattered net home in a single convulsive pulse, no patience left for careful, no time left for subtle, bugs abandoning windowsills and curtain rods and the undersides of tables in a ragged, unmistakable wave that any trained eye in that room would read instantly for exactly what it was.

Cricket's head snapped toward the movement.

*"Oh no,"* Gladly said, small and scared in a way none of them had heard from him all night. *"Oh no, she saw that. She definitely saw that."*

*Move.* McClane's voice had lost every trace of calm it had been holding together with. *Now. Out of laundry and away from the chute. If she reads which direction that swarm came from, you do not stay at the end of the line.*

Taylor was already on her feet. She took the clipboard map, shoved it beneath the rolling cart, and left the pocket between the pressing table and the wall for the first time since the search.

The basement corridor offered two routes. Left reached the chute landing, then the stairs Anders's men had already searched twice. Right ran through mechanical storage and climbed by a narrow staff stair to the service level behind the kitchen.

Taylor went right.

She sent the returning swarm past her rather than drawing it home, a ragged stream continuing toward the loading dock before splitting in four directions. Cricket might read motion. Taylor gave her four false answers and kept none near her own body.

Behind her, the laundry-room door opened hard enough to strike the wall.

Not Cricket. One of the suited men, reporting into his collar as he saw the abandoned hiding place.

"Lower service. She was here."

Taylor reached the mechanical-room door. Locked.

*Map showed another way,* McClane said.

She reversed, cut through dry storage, and climbed over a stack of folded banquet tables to reach the second door. Voices entered the corridor behind her. A flashlight swept across the cinderblock wall and caught the edge of her torn green hem as she pulled it clear.

"There!"

Taylor shoved through the door, crossed a boiler room hot enough to steal her breath, and found the narrow staff stair. Kitchen workers were already coming down it, fleeing the armed takeover above. She flattened herself against the rail while a dishwasher and two line cooks squeezed past, then climbed against their flow.

*You're going up,* McClane said.

*Laundry is burned. The loading dock is watched. The other basement stair puts me under their search team.*

*Good. Keep going.*

At the top, the service corridor had become a collision of catering staff trying to escape and armed men driving them back toward the kitchen. Taylor entered between those currents with her head down and her torn skirt gathered in one hand. She was no longer hiding in a room Cricket could locate by following insects. She was moving through a floor full of people dressed differently, running for different reasons, all of them equally inconvenient to track.

The last image she kept from the atrium before dispersing the observation net was Hookwolf's silver spine catching the light from an emergency exit sign, patient and unhurried, like a predator that already knew it had all the time in the world.



Chapter 17: Near Miss



Cricket was faster than the service-level crowd could account for.

Taylor felt it before she saw it, the wrongness of a straight line closing where a curved one should have been. She looked up from the stream of cooks, servers, and displaced guests just in time to watch the woman cut through a service alcove that should have taken her four times as long to reach, moving like the panic in the corridor was a current she'd already learned to read and use.

Cricket had not followed Taylor's false swarm routes. She had followed the human bottleneck. Every usable stair from the basement fed into one of three corridors, and Cricket had chosen the one with bodies pouring through it.

*She didn't need the bugs,* Taylor thought.

*No. She needed the building. Same thing we've been using all night, except she knows how to hunt in it.*

*She's cutting you off.* McClane's voice had gone flat, immediate, no room in it for anything but the next three seconds. *She's not chasing the crowd. She's chasing the shape of where the crowd's thinnest, which means she's chasing you specifically now, whether she's got a face to put on it or not.*

*I can't get to the chute. Not this way.*

*Then we don't go this way.*

Taylor cut left instead, hard, into the narrow gap between a collapsed dessert table and the wall, and felt Cricket adjust a half second later, already anticipating the correction before Taylor had fully committed to it, and that, more than the blade at her hip or the coiled economy of her movement, was the thing that finally made the fear in Taylor's chest go sharp and clear instead of formless.

Taylor vaulted a fallen chair. Cricket stepped around the place she would land before Taylor's feet reached the floor.

She ducked behind three fleeing guests. Cricket did not push through them. She moved parallel on the other side, watching shoulders and hips, and arrived at the end of the human screen at the same moment Taylor did.

Taylor threw a handful of silverware from an abandoned table. Not to hit her. To create six moving objects with six paths.

Cricket's blades flickered. A spoon split against the wall. Two forks rang off steel. She let the rest pass without looking and continued forward.

*She's sorting threats,* McClane said. *Anything aimed at her goes in one box. Anything that isn't gets ignored.*

*Then I can't distract her.*

*Not with anything she can classify.*

Taylor sent a wasp at the back of Cricket's neck.

Cricket moved before it came within a foot. One blade cut the insect from the air. Her eyes never left Taylor.

The route to the chute vanished behind her.

Taylor backed into the service alcove. A tray rack blocked one side. A decorative wall closed the other. Cricket slowed because speed no longer mattered.

"You made him nervous," she said.

Her speaking voice was quieter than Taylor expected.

"Good."

The answer came out before Taylor decided to give it. McClane's satisfaction flashed through her fear.

Cricket smiled without warmth. "He'll want to know how."

She shifted her weight.

Taylor felt the attack begin before the blade moved, not through precognition, only because McClane had taught her where to watch.

*She's not just fast. She's already three moves ahead. That's not just training.*

*No,* McClane agreed, grim. *That's a power built for exactly this. Reading a fight before it happens. Which means straight lines are a loser's game against her. We need something that doesn't have a straight line to read.*

*Like what.*

*Like everything you've got.*

Taylor didn't have time to be careful. She reached, hard, for every straggler still drifting loose across the ground floor, dozens of them now, warmed and awake and desperate for direction, and threw the whole ragged mass of them not at Cricket's eyes or her hands but at the air around her, a churning, formless cloud of motion and sound with no single point in it worth tracking, no blow to anticipate, no attack to read, just noise, close and total and impossible to parse into anything a combat instinct could sort cleanly.

Cricket's head snapped sideways, hands coming up on pure reflex, blades catching the light in two bright arcs that cut nothing but empty air, and for the first time all night Taylor watched a parahuman flinch.

*"You cannot out-fight a combat reader,"* the gallery said. *"You can out-weird one."*

*Go. Now. While she's still recalibrating.*

Taylor went, cutting past the alcove and into the service corridor beyond it, lungs burning, the cloud of bugs already scattering behind her, their job done, their small, brief usefulness spent. She didn't look back to see whether Cricket recovered fast enough to follow. She didn't have the seconds to spare for finding out.

*"That deserved slow motion,"* Gladly said.

*That,* McClane said, somehow breathless despite having no lungs of his own, *was the single best piece of improvisation I've seen out of a rookie in thirty years on this job, and I include myself in the pool of rookies I'm comparing you to.*

*I got lucky.*

*You got smart. Different thing. Luck doesn't know to throw noise at a power built to read clean signals.* Something almost like pride crept into the flatness. *You didn't fight her. You broke the rules of the fight before she could start one. That's not luck, kid. That's the whole trick. That's the trick I spent thirty years learning and you just found in about four seconds flat.*

The corridor spat her out near the kitchen, steam and shouting and a line cook already halfway out a service door with a fire extinguisher clutched to his chest like a life preserver, and Taylor let herself be carried along in his wake for a dozen steps, one more panicked body in a building full of them, before she peeled off toward the linen closet and the chute beyond it.

She didn't make it three steps before she heard the sound.

Small, sharp, entirely human, coming from a supply alcove tucked behind the kitchen's walk-in freezer: a woman's voice, thin with panic, and underneath it, the rattle of a door handle that wouldn't turn.

*Keep moving,* McClane said, and then, a half beat later, quieter, *No. Wait. What's that.*

*Somebody's stuck.*

Taylor was already changing course before she'd finished the thought, some old, stubborn part of her refusing to let the sentence finish any other way. She found the alcove door exactly where the sound had placed it, a service closet with an old push-button lock that had, sometime in the chaos, jammed itself solid, and behind it, muffled and increasingly frantic, a woman who sounded younger than Danny, older than Taylor, and entirely alone.

"Hello? Is someone out there? The door's stuck, I can't..." A sharp, indrawn breath. "Please, I heard people running, is there a fire, what's happening..."

*You don't have time for this,* McClane said, and there was real conflict in it, not coldness, a man weighing a cost he clearly hated weighing. *Cricket's still out there. Every second you spend on this door is a second she gets to close the gap.*

*I know.*

*I'm not telling you not to. I'm telling you to know what it costs before you pay it.*

Taylor already knew. The old privacy latch had an emergency pinhole beneath the handle. She pulled a bobby pin from her hair, straightened it against the metal strike plate, and sent two tiny insects through the gap around the latch. They could not move the mechanism for her. They could tell her where the inner cam sat and whether the wire passed above or below it.

"I'm here," she called through the door, keeping her own voice as steady as she could make it. "The lock's jammed. Give me a second. Push the door toward me when I say."

"Oh, thank god, thank god, please hurry..."

It took eleven seconds, the longest eleven seconds of the whole night. Taylor missed the cam once, bent the pin, turned it, and felt through the insects when its tip finally settled into the release notch.

"Now."

The woman pushed. Taylor pressed the pin. The latch gave with a soft click, and the door swung open on a woman in a catering uniform, mascara streaked, hands shaking. She stumbled into Taylor, caught herself against the wall, and stared toward the shouting in the kitchen.

"Service stairs," Taylor said, pointing back the way she'd come. "Down one level, then follow the mechanical corridor away from the laundry room. Don't use the loading dock."

The woman seized Taylor's wrist. "Come with me."

For one second the offer was almost enough.

Then Taylor felt Cricket enter the far end of the corridor, slower now, testing every branch.

"I can't. Go."

The woman ran. She looked back once before the stair door closed, long enough to see Taylor turn toward the threat instead of following her away from it.

*"She's not just surviving anymore,"* Gladly said.

*"No,"* Fury agreed. *"She's not."*

Taylor didn't have time to feel what she was feeling about that. She was already moving toward the chute, not to hide in the burned laundry room again but because the shaft offered the fastest route past the search teams closing on both service stairs. She carried the eleven seconds with her like a small, warm weight she hadn't earned the right to put down yet.



Chapter 18: Traffic Control



The mezzanine lounge was still trapped when Taylor felt for it.

A dozen guests, maybe more, the same well-dressed inner circle she'd cataloged from a laundry room an hour ago, backed against the railing with nowhere obvious to go, the main staircase down to the atrium choked solid with the general evacuation and the service stairs on the far side blocked by something Taylor couldn't immediately place, just a straggler's confused, jumbled sense of a hallway gone suddenly impassable.

*That's not panic blocking that hallway,* McClane said, sharp, already reading it. *That's deliberate. Somebody wants that floor sealed.*

*Why would he seal in his own people?*

*Maybe he's not sealing them in. Maybe he's sealing everyone else out, so his own crew can work that floor without two hundred witnesses wandering through it.* Grim, certain. *Either way, that's a dozen civilians about to be very alone with whatever's about to happen up there.*

*"Not our problem,"* Fury said, flat. *"You've got your own exit to make. Cricket's still out there somewhere."*

*"You say that,"* Gib said, dry, *"like you actually believe it."*

Fury didn't answer that one.

Taylor was already reaching before she'd finished deciding to, threading a thin scouting line of bugs down the blocked service corridor, patient, careful, until she found the actual obstruction: not a person, not a barricade, just an overturned linen cart and a fallen coat rack, knocked sideways in the general chaos and wedged tight enough across the narrow hallway to stop a human body cold.

*They can move that.*

*If they know where to push.*

Taylor ran insects over the obstruction. The linen cart's two forward casters had swiveled sideways. One brake was locked. The coat rack lay across its top with a torn garment bag wound around both frames, binding them together.

She gathered the swarm on the locked caster first, packed dark bodies around the small brake pedal, then drew a line up the cart's side to the twisted garment bag.

The barefoot woman reached the blockage and stopped.

"The bugs are on the wheel," someone behind her said.

"I can see that."

She crouched, found the brake beneath the swarm, and kicked it free. The cart shifted an inch. Two men pushed from behind while she pulled the garment bag loose. The coat rack rolled, slid off the cart, and struck the wall. The three of them hauled the cart backward until a shoulder-wide gap opened beside it.

Taylor moved the bugs through the opening.

The barefoot woman understood. She went first.

*"She didn't move it,"* the gallery said. *"She showed twelve frightened people how."*

*Now what,* McClane asked, already moving past the win to the next problem. *You cleared a path. They don't know it's clear. You gonna walk up there and give a safety briefing?*

*No.* Taylor thought fast, turning the shape of the problem over the way he'd taught her to. *But I can make the path obvious. Same way a runway's obvious, if you know what you're looking for.*

She worked quickly, sending a thin, visible trail of bugs skittering along the baseboard of the now-clear corridor, deliberate this time, not hiding, a moving line pointed unmistakably toward the service stairs beyond it. It wasn't subtle. It wasn't supposed to be. Twelve terrified people weren't going to follow a hunch. They needed something that looked like an answer.

*Lights on a runway,* McClane said, and there was real warmth under the observation now, something almost like wonder. *You're building yourself an actual flight path. That's not just clever, kid. That's got style.*

Someone on the mezzanine screamed at the sight of it, predictably, and then, a beat later, someone else, sharper-eyed or simply more desperate, said the word "look" instead, and pointed, and the small, frightened cluster of guests began, hesitant and disbelieving, to follow a trail of insects toward a door they wouldn't have found on their own in time.

The first man refused to step over them. "Those things are being controlled."

"Obviously," said the woman behind him. She had removed her heels and held one in each hand. "Which means somebody knows where they're going. Do you?"

He did not.

She stepped onto the service corridor.

The others followed because a person moving with confidence was easier to trust than a miracle made of beetles. Taylor adjusted the trail around broken glass, down the narrow hall, and through the door she had cleared. At every turn she gathered the swarm into an arrow before spreading it along the next wall.

Halfway to the stairs, the overhead lights went out.

Red emergency strobes continued pulsing along the walls, bright enough to show fragments and dark enough to ruin depth. The group stopped. Someone began praying.

Taylor gathered the swarm into a thick arrow on the pale wall beneath the next strobe. Red light flashed over it. The arrow vanished into gray, then returned with the next pulse, pointing toward the turn.

At the corner she dissolved it and formed another beneath the following light.
