The barefoot woman took the front again.

"Keep a hand on the person ahead of you," she said. "If the bugs turn, we turn."

Twelve strangers became a chain and moved through the dark.

*"They're following the bugs,"* Gladly said.

*"They're following the woman who understood them,"* Fury corrected.

Taylor held the trail steady, patient, careful, feeling strain born not from numbers but from the sustained concentration of holding one clean line of intention through a building full of chaos. She did not let it waver until the last mezzanine guest had cleared the service stairs and vanished from her borrowed sense entirely, safe, or at least safer, headed toward an exit that hadn't existed thirty seconds ago.

She didn't have time to feel good about it. Somewhere behind her, close enough now that she felt it as much as sensed it, something heavy hit the floor of the main hall hard enough to rattle a table three rooms away.

*That's not Cricket.*

*No.* McClane's voice had gone very flat, very careful. *That's the metal one. Hookwolf. And he's not being subtle about it anymore.*

Taylor caught the shape of him through a straggler near the atrium, moving through the wreckage of an abandoned dessert table with the unhurried, deliberate weight of something that had stopped pretending to be in a hurry, silver spines catching the low emergency lighting in long, patient ripples.

*He's not chasing one person. He's just... breaking things. Why's he just breaking things.*

*Because it works.* Grim, honest. *Because a guy that size walking slow through a room full of overturned furniture doesn't need to run. Everybody in his path clears it for him. That's its own kind of hunting.*

He was heading, unmistakably, toward the service corridor Taylor herself needed. Not toward her specifically, not yet, just toward the same geometry of the building she needed to use, and the two facts were about to become the same problem within a matter of seconds.

*Options,* she said, fast, no time left for anything but the shortest version of the question.

*You don't fight that. Full stop, no negotiation on that point.* McClane's mind was already three steps ahead, she could feel it, the same fast, blunt cataloging he did with everything, now scaled up to match the size of the actual threat. *But he's slow, methodical, working through a room like a man clearing rubble. That means predictable. That means you don't need to beat him. You just need him looking at something else for four seconds.*

*Like what.*

*Like every catering cart still standing in that kitchen.*

Taylor understood the shape before he'd finished explaining. The kitchen doors stood behind Hookwolf. Beyond them, three loaded service carts waited on the shallow ramp to the corridor, brakes set, abandoned when the takeover began.

No insect could release those brakes or put several hundred pounds of cart into motion.

Taylor could.

She sent the remaining bugs high through the steam, not toward Hookwolf but around his head, a ragged veil that made him raise one bladed arm and turn toward the motion. Taylor used the instant to slip through the opposite kitchen door.

The first cart's brake lever resisted beneath her shoe. She stamped twice. It released with a metal crack.

Taylor shoved.

The cart barely moved.

She put her shoulder against its warm steel side, shoes sliding on wet tile, and drove with both legs. The wheels crossed the ramp's lip. Gravity took the weight away from her.

The loaded cart rolled into the corridor and crashed directly into Hookwolf's path with a sound like the whole kitchen falling down at once.

The first cart struck sideways and spilled racks of glasses beneath him. Hookwolf's next step crushed them into glittering powder.

Taylor stamped the brake on a second cart loaded with coffee urns. A line cook sheltering behind the prep counter saw what she was doing, crossed the kitchen in three strides, and shoved with both hands. Taylor added her shoulder for the first yard. The cart gathered speed down the service ramp, hit the first, and turned it into a wall of steel trays, spilled coffee, and steam.

Hookwolf tore through the collision rather than avoiding it. Metal spines shredded both carts. Steam rolled across the corridor and hid him for two seconds.

Two seconds were more than Taylor needed now.

Hookwolf drove forward through the wreckage, both bladed arms occupied shredding steel out of his path. Taylor went the other direction. She slipped behind the steam, crossed the gap between his turning radius and the wall, and reached the linen-closet side of the corridor before he finished tearing the second cart apart. She passed closer to a monster than she'd ever been in her life and moved too fast, too focused, to feel the full weight of that until she was beyond him.

*"Twice in two chapters,"* the gallery said. *"She never fought either cape. She changed what they had to look at."*

*"That's not a fluke anymore,"* Fury said, and for once there was no dryness in the observation at all, just plain, weighty fact. *"That's a whole toolkit."*

*You did good up there,* McClane said, quiet, urgent underneath it, a man saying it now because he wasn't sure how much longer there'd be breath to spare for saying it at all. *Real good. I mean that.*

*I got lucky twice.*

*You got smart twice, in two completely different ways, against two completely different problems, inside of maybe ninety seconds.* Something almost fierce crept into the words. *Luck doesn't repeat like that, kid. Skill does.*

Taylor didn't have breath left to argue with him. She made it to the linen closet with her lungs burning and her hands shaking for reasons that had stopped being about cold bugs an hour ago, and didn't let herself stop, didn't let herself think, threw herself into the chute already falling before she'd fully committed to it, dark and cotton and gravity taking her the rest of the way down.



Chapter 19: Ground Stop



Taylor hit the bottom of the chute on one hip, rolled through a mound of damp tablecloths, and struck the concrete wall with her shoulder hard enough to make her right hand go numb.

For several seconds she stayed exactly where gravity had left her. The dark laundry room tipped gently around her. Her lungs kept trying to breathe at the speed she'd been running, though the chase was above her now and there was nothing in front of her except an overturned hamper, a wheeled pressing table, and the pale rectangle of the service door.

*Inventory,* McClane said.

*What?*

*You. Inventory. Head, neck, hands, feet. Anything broken, anything wet that shouldn't be, anything you can't feel.*

Taylor worked through it because his voice gave her something smaller than the whole building to solve. Head sore but clear. Neck moved. Shoulder hurt. Hip hurt worse. Both knees worked. Her right hand prickled back to life one finger at a time.

*Nothing broken.*

*Good. Now the building.*

The difference between Taylor's eyes and her swarm had never felt wider. Her eyes saw a basement room under one buzzing fluorescent tube. Her bugs saw MedHall coming apart by sections.

The atrium doors had not opened when the crowd rushed them. The two men stationed there had thrown steel pins into the floor sockets and turned the polished brass handles into decoration. Three guests had gone down in the first crush. Danny and two other men were dragging them clear while people behind them shouted questions nobody answered.

The east exit was shut. The loading entrance was shut. The parking bridge doors on the mezzanine were shut. Fire doors had dropped across two service corridors, dividing the catering staff into small frightened groups with no view of the main room.

Hookwolf stood at the foot of the grand staircase with metal layered over both arms, not fully transformed and not needing to be. Cricket paced the upper landing. Alabaster watched the service doors. The two giant women held opposite ends of the atrium floor. Armed men moved between them collecting handbags and phones into silver serving trays that had been meant for champagne.

The whole building had changed jobs.

An hour ago the doors had been there to welcome people. The corridors had moved food and staff without disturbing the party. The mezzanine had displayed the guests important enough to be seen above everyone else. Now the doors contained, the corridors divided, and the mezzanine gave Cricket a firing position over the hostages below.

*That's a takeover,* McClane said. *Organized, rehearsed, and already underway before the first blade came out. Nobody locks this many doors by improvising.*

*You said he was reacting to us.*

*He is. Doesn't mean he built the gun tonight. Means we gave him a reason to take it out of the drawer.*

Taylor found Anders through a beetle under the stage.

He was on the atrium floor with everybody else now. Jacket off. Sleeves damp with spilled wine. One hand rested on the shoulder of the councilwoman who had spent half the evening discussing zoning. He spoke quietly to her while one of his own armed men passed six feet away without looking at him.

To anyone watching from the room, Max Anders was another hostage doing his best to keep people calm.

From beneath the stage, Taylor watched two fingers of his free hand tap once against his trouser seam.

Cricket changed direction on the mezzanine.

*He gave her an order.*

*Yeah.* McClane's disgust came through cold and clean. *And nobody saw it because everybody's watching the guns. He gets to be mastermind and brave civic leader in the same picture.*

Danny pushed through the hostages with a folded tablecloth pressed against an older man's bleeding scalp. He was pale. His tie was gone. He kept looking toward the service hall where he had last seen Taylor.

*Dad.*

*Alive. Helping somebody. Keep the categories straight. Alive is the category that matters right now.*

Taylor forced herself to pull her attention outward.

The basement had fewer bugs than the warm atrium, but enough. Silverfish in the walls. Flies sleeping near a floor drain. Two spiders behind the electrical panels. She spread them through the service level and began assembling space from touch and vibration.

Laundry occupied the southeast corner. Kitchen storage sat north of it. A mechanical room took up most of the western wall. Between them lay a security office she had missed earlier because its door sealed nearly flush and its windows faced the loading ramp outside.

There were people inside.

Two, both on the floor. One moving. One not.

Taylor was on her feet before McClane asked.

*Careful,* he said. *People on the floor means somebody put them there.*

The corridor beyond the laundry room was empty. She opened the service door an inch, then another. The alarm strobes had begun flashing without the horns, red light pulsing silently along white cinderblock. Somewhere in the building, someone had disabled the sound while leaving the visual circuit active. Every third second the corridor became red, then gray, then red again.

She sent bugs ahead at ankle height. No footsteps. No breath. No radio static.

Taylor slipped out.

The security office door had been kicked inward. One MedHall guard lay facedown beside the desk with his wrists cinched behind him using a plastic tie. The second had been shoved beneath the bank of camera monitors. Blood had dried from his temple into the collar of his white shirt.

The first man lifted his head when she entered.

"Kid, get out of here."

"Doors are locked."

"Then hide."

"Already tried that."

She found trauma shears clipped to the unconscious guard's belt. Her hands shook badly enough that the first attempt only pinched the plastic tie. McClane talked her through changing the angle, bracing the guard's wrists against the floor, and cutting away from skin.

The tie snapped.

The guard rolled onto his side and stared at her dress, her bare forearms, and the laundry lint still caught in her hair.

"How did you get down here?"

"Laundry chute. Is he breathing?"

That got him moving. He crawled to his partner, checked an airway, then pressed two fingers to the man's neck.

"Yeah. Shallow. Tom? Tom, wake up."

Tom did not.

Taylor looked at the monitors. Every screen showed the gala as it had looked before the takeover: couples dancing, waiters moving between tables, the quartet playing beneath the chandelier. The same woman in a blue dress crossed the same camera every forty-one seconds.

"They're looped," she said.

The guard followed her eyes. "That's impossible. System's closed."

"It isn't tonight. Where's the outside line?"

He reached for the desk phone. No dial tone. He tried the red handset mounted below the monitors. Nothing. Then the panic switch under the desk.

The red indicator stayed dark.

"Hard line to our monitoring company," he said. "Separate conduit."

*Was separate,* McClane corrected.

The guard opened the cabinet below the desk. Three cables hung inside with their copper cut cleanly through.

His face changed. Not fear exactly. The collapse of the last explanation that had allowed this to remain a security incident instead of an occupation.

"Who are they?"

Taylor watched Anders comfort his own hostages through the beetle under the stage.

"Empire Eighty-Eight."

The guard looked at her sharply. "How do you know that?"

Metal struck metal somewhere down the service corridor.

McClane heard it through her before she had sorted it from the building noise.

*Company. Two, maybe three. Coming from the mechanical room side.*

Taylor killed the office light. The pulsing corridor strobe painted the open doorway red every third second.

The guard reached for the empty holster on his belt.

"They took it," he whispered.

*Good,* McClane said. *Means he won't try something brave and get all three of you killed.*

"Can you move your partner?" Taylor asked.

"Not fast."

There was nowhere in the office to hide them. The desk left six inches underneath. The cabinet held wires. The exterior windows faced a loading ramp now crossed by flashlight beams.

Taylor felt along the ceiling with two moths and found a return-air grille above the monitor bank. Too small for a person. Large enough for something else.

"When they come in, fall down again. Hands behind you. Don't look at me."

The guard stared. "What are you going to do?"

"Make them look up."

She gathered every basement insect she could reach into the duct. Not many. A few dozen flies, moths, beetles, spiders carried unwillingly on silk. She packed them behind the grille and held them there while the footsteps came closer.

Two men appeared in the corridor, dark suits open now, compact submachine guns visible beneath them. One carried a radio near his mouth.

"Basement east clear. Checking security."

The guard lowered himself beside Tom and put his hands behind his back.

Taylor pressed flat against the wall beside the open door.

The first man stepped through. He saw the guards. Saw the dead monitors. Began turning toward the blind corner.

Taylor released the duct.

The swarm dropped onto both men at once. Not an attack. A black, fluttering sheet falling directly through the red strobe, wings and legs in faces, inside collars, across weapon sights. The first man shouted and brought his gun up toward the ceiling. The second backed into the corridor, swatting with his radio hand.

Taylor went low between them.

She hooked one foot behind the second man's ankle and drove her shoulder into the back of his knee. He fell hard. His radio skipped across the tile. She caught it, rolled, and kept moving while the first man's gun coughed three suppressed shots into the return-air duct.

Plaster dust burst from the ceiling.

The guard surged out of the office after her. He stamped once on the fallen man's wrist until the submachine gun came loose, kicked it through the office door, then dragged the man inside by his collar.

"Go!" he shouted.

Taylor went.

She ran toward the mechanical room because the loading ramp was watched and the laundry room was where they would expect her to return. A bullet struck the wall behind her with a dry crack. Another punched through a metal storage cabinet and sent a stack of baking sheets crashing across the floor.

The radio in her hand came alive.

"Security team, report."

Taylor hit the mechanical-room door, found it locked, and nearly broke her fingers trying the handle twice.

*Hinges open toward you,* McClane said. *Utility cart behind you. Ram the latch side.*

She seized the narrow cart stacked with folded aprons, swung it around, and drove its steel corner beside the lock. Once. The door flexed. Twice. The frame split enough to show darkness. On the third impact the latch tore out and the door banged inward.

Taylor hauled the cart through after her and wedged it under the handle.

The mechanical room rose two stories around her, all pipes, valves, and square ventilation trunks. Pumps throbbed beneath the floor. A steel stair climbed to a catwalk and disappeared over an enormous boiler. Heat pressed against her wet skin.

The radio spoke again.

"Security team. Report."

Then a different voice, clipped and impatient: "Cricket is moving to basement east. Nobody fires again unless they have the target. Mr. Anders wants her breathing."

Taylor stopped halfway to the stairs.

*Her,* she thought.

*Yeah,* McClane said. *We're done pretending they don't know.*

The door shook behind her. The cart's wheels squealed across the concrete.

Taylor climbed.

At the top of the catwalk she found a maintenance station bolted to the wall: building diagrams under scratched plastic, valve instructions, emergency numbers, and a gray phone with no dial.

No dial because it called only one place.

She lifted it.

It rang.

Once.

Twice.

"MedHall elevator emergency. State the nature of your emergency."

Taylor nearly laughed. Instead she pressed herself behind a ventilation trunk as the mechanical-room door jumped in its frame below.

"Hostage situation," she whispered. "MedHall gala. Empire Eighty-Eight capes inside. At least two hundred civilians. The security lines are cut and the cameras are looped."

"Ma'am, can you repeat your location?"

"MedHall. Downtown Brockton Bay. Send the PRT, but tell them not to come through the front doors. The host is running it. Max Anders is running it."

The door below burst inward. The cart went over with a crash.

"Ma'am? Who is speaking?"

Taylor saw the first gun barrel clear the doorway.

"Tell them the hostage is the one giving orders."

She dropped the phone and ran along the catwalk.

The first shots struck the railing behind her. Sparks jumped into the dark. Taylor ducked beneath a pipe, hit the far stair too fast, and half slid down it with one hand on the rail. Her swarm found a red wheel valve beside the boiler before her eyes did.

*Steam?* she asked.

*Probably. Also probably hot enough to take skin off. Open it after you pass, not before.*

She sent beetles into the wheel's spokes and added her own hand as she went by. The valve resisted, then turned with a shriek.

White vapor punched across the catwalk behind her.

The gunmen swore and stopped firing. One tried to push through, got close enough to feel the heat, and recoiled. Taylor reached the lower door on the far side and pulled it shut behind her.

Only then did she realize the radio was still in her hand.

Voices crowded it now.

"Police units on Grant."

"Two PRT transports behind them."

"No approach yet. Perimeter only."

A pause.

Then the same clipped voice as before.

"Boss says start the clock. Move the hostages away from the glass. Find the girl before the heroes decide to be stupid."

Taylor stood in another dark service corridor, bruised, wet, clutching an enemy radio while sirens rose beyond walls she could not see through.

*You got the call out,* McClane said.

*Part of it.*

*Part's enough to get them here.*

*And if they don't believe it?*

Outside, a loudspeaker came alive, its first words reduced by concrete to a low mechanical murmur.

Inside, every armed man in Taylor's borrowed map began moving at once.

*Then we make them believe it,* McClane said.



Chapter 20: Approach Control



The first voice from outside arrived through the radio Taylor had stolen.

"MedHall command, this is Armsmaster of the Brockton Bay Protectorate. We have established a perimeter. State your demands and release any injured civilians requiring medical attention."

The transmission was calm, amplified, and clean enough that Taylor could hear the faint electronic edge added by a helmet microphone. It was followed by silence throughout the building. Guards stopped in corridors. Cricket paused on the mezzanine. Even the hostages seemed to feel that someone outside had finally named what was happening, though the radio words could not have reached them.

Max Anders lowered his head among the captives and tapped two fingers against his knee.

The man nearest the atrium's sound board lifted his radio.

"Protectorate, your people will remain beyond the outer drive. Any attempt to enter the building will be treated as an attack on the civilians inside. We will provide demands when we are ready."

His voice had been flattened through a modulator. The words came out sexless and mechanical.

*There,* McClane said. *That's the front man. Everybody outside talks to him. Everybody inside takes orders from the hostage sitting on the floor.*

Taylor crouched behind a stack of banquet chairs in a storage room off the western service hall. The radio rested against one ear. Through her bugs, MedHall had become a diagram drawn in people.

One hundred and sixty-three hostages sat or knelt in the atrium. Twenty-one more were held in the mezzanine lounge after the service-stair group Taylor had freed ran into a locked fire door below. Fourteen catering workers waited in a kitchen cold room with the refrigeration turned off. Seven people, including the two security guards, hid in the basement laundry.

Danny sat near the stage with the injured man's head in his lap.

Taylor counted again.

The numbers did not change.

*You keep recounting, you're gonna get the same answer,* McClane said.

*I might have missed someone.*

*Then count routes, not people. People stay alive if the routes work.*

It was the kind of advice that sounded heartless until she understood it. McClane was not telling her to care less. He was telling her where caring could become useful.

Taylor unfolded a linen inventory sheet on the floor and began drawing MedHall from memory. Atrium at center. Grand stairs northeast. Kitchen and loading dock west. Laundry beneath the southeast service corridor. Mechanical room below the stage. She marked locked doors with Xs and groups of hostages with circles.

*Three exits from the atrium,* McClane said. *Main doors, east doors, loading corridor. Main's covered from the mezzanine. East is covered by the two big ones. Loading corridor puts everybody past the kitchen and through a door narrow enough to turn a crowd into a pile.*

*There are fire stairs from the mezzanine.*

*Locked below. We learned that one.*

*Parking bridge.*

*Cricket owns it.*

Taylor drew another X.

On the radio, Armsmaster answered the modulated voice. "We need proof of life and a head count."

"You will receive both."

"We also received a partial emergency call identifying Empire Eighty-Eight capes inside the building."

Every guard channel went silent.

Taylor stopped drawing.

The modulated voice replied, "Then you understand the cost of testing us."

Not a denial.

Not confirmation either.

*He just took your warning and made it part of his threat,* McClane said. *That's good. Means he's fast. Also means the people outside still don't know who's wearing which hat.*

A second voice entered the channel, lower than Armsmaster's and female.

"Inside caller, if you can hear this frequency, key your radio twice. Do not speak."

Taylor stared at the transmit button.

*Trap?* she asked.

*Maybe. Could be the bad guys checking whether the stolen set's still live. Could be somebody outside figured out their radio traffic is getting repeated.*

"Inside caller," the woman said again. "Two clicks only."

Taylor pressed twice.

Across the building, six radios answered with bursts of static as men shifted their hands toward transmit buttons and stopped. The sound was too small to identify a set.

"Received," the woman said. "My name is Miss Militia. Keep this channel. Do not answer again until I address you as Tower."

*I like her,* McClane said.

*You heard two sentences.*

*First one didn't get you killed. Second gave you a code word. That's a strong opening.*

The gallery had gone quiet. No wagers. No reviewer's voice. Taylor was grateful for it.

She folded the map into quarters and put it inside her dress at the waist. Then she moved.

The storage room opened onto a staff corridor running behind the atrium wall. Decorative panels separated it from the main room. During the gala, servers had passed through concealed doors with trays. Now armed men used the same route to move behind the hostages without crossing their sightlines.

Taylor crawled beneath a row of garment racks and sent bugs ahead.

At the far end of the corridor, two men rolled something heavy toward the atrium. Not weapons. Black equipment cases, each the size of a footlocker, drawn from the locked room her reconnaissance had found earlier.

They opened the first case behind the stage.

Inside were shaped charges, wire, and three compact control units.

McClane went still.

*Those aren't for the doors.*

*How do you know?*

*Too much charge. Wrong placement. They're putting one against the building columns.*

Taylor watched a man lift a gray block from the foam lining and carry it toward the west support beneath the mezzanine.

*Why would they bring down their own building?*

*They might not intend to. Explosives do two jobs in a hostage situation. They can blow something up, or they can make the people outside believe you will. Right now he needs everybody staring at the detonator while he does whatever tonight was built to do.*

*You said he was reacting to us.*

*The lockdown was. The hardware wasn't.*

The conclusion changed the shape of the night again. Kaiser had not merely kept dangerous people near him at a gala. He had prepared MedHall to be seized. Taylor and McClane had tripped the plan early, or forced him to change its purpose, but the cases had been waiting behind the locked door long before the first bug crossed the loading corridor.

There had always been a siege coming.

They had only made it start before Kaiser was ready.

One of the men with the equipment cases turned toward Taylor's corridor.

She flattened herself beneath the garment bags. Polished shoes approached, stopped close enough that she could see water beaded along the soles from the atrium floor.

The man keyed his radio. "Found movement behind west service."

Taylor held every bug still.

"Human?"

"Not sure. Door moved."

The muzzle of his weapon pushed between two hanging coats.

Taylor sent three cockroaches across the opposite wall.

The man's eyes tracked them. His gun followed.

She came out behind him, caught the handle of the equipment case, and shoved it into the backs of his knees. He fell across the lid. Taylor ran before he finished turning, through the concealed service door and into the atrium itself.

For one terrible second there was nowhere to hide.

Hostages covered the floor in ordered rows. Fenja stood near the east doors. Alabaster faced the stage. Armed men watched every aisle. The room Taylor had spent all night learning now offered no empty space at all.

Then Danny stood up.

"He needs a doctor," he shouted, pointing at the unconscious man beside him. "You want proof of life for the people outside, start by keeping him alive."

Every head near the stage turned toward him.

Taylor crossed behind the last row of hostages, bent nearly double, and slipped beneath a banquet table whose cloth still reached the floor.

*He didn't know he was covering you,* McClane said.

*I know.*

*Still counts.*

Anders rose among the captives with both hands visible. "He's right. This man needs help. Whatever point you're trying to make, a dead donor won't improve it."

The armed men watched their supposed hostage speak. One looked toward the sound-board operator for instruction.

Anders touched his thumb to the second joint of his forefinger.

The operator nodded. "Bring the injured man forward. The union representative comes with him."

Taylor's stomach dropped.

Danny helped lift the injured man. Another hostage took the legs. They carried him toward the stage under three guns.

Anders stepped aside to make room, gracious even now.

As Danny passed him, Anders said something too low for human hearing.

A fly clinging beneath his collar caught it.

"Your daughter has made this evening much more complicated than it needed to be."

Danny missed a step.

Taylor stopped breathing.

Anders smiled for the room and took the injured man's hand as cameras near the stage came alive for the promised proof-of-life feed.

"Tower," Miss Militia said through the radio hidden against Taylor's ribs. "If you can hear me, we need your threat map."

Taylor watched her father realize that the respected man beside him knew exactly who she was.

*We need a new route,* she thought.

McClane's answer came hard and immediate.

*No. We need to take his building away from him.*



Chapter 21: Wrong Runway



Taylor stayed beneath the banquet table until Danny and the injured man disappeared behind the stage.

She tracked them with the moth in Danny's collar. Two gunmen escorted him into a treatment room attached to MedHall's first-floor clinic. Anders remained in the atrium, still among the hostages, one hand resting on the councilwoman's shoulder while the sound-board operator negotiated with Armsmaster in a machine voice.

"Our conditions are simple," the operator said. "No aircraft within two miles. No parahuman personnel inside the outer drive. At midnight, three vehicles with full fuel tanks will be delivered to the loading entrance. Drivers will exit and walk away."

"You have placed explosives against structural supports," Armsmaster replied. "No vehicle approaches until our engineers receive the trigger design and placement."

Every head inside the atrium turned toward the columns.

The charges had been hidden behind decorative panels. Armsmaster had just told the hostages they existed.

Panic moved through the room without anyone standing. People leaned away from the columns, pressed closer together, whispered names into a silence where no phones remained to call them.

The movement reached the guards before it became a rush. One man stepped into the center aisle and raised his weapon. Another dragged a banquet chair against the main doors, not because the chair could stop anyone, but because scraping metal across marble made every head turn toward him.

"Stay seated."

A woman near the north column began to cry. Her husband put both arms around her and drew her down until his forehead rested against the back of her neck. Two rows away, a city planner Taylor recognized from one of Danny's union meetings stared at the decorative panel hiding the charge and silently measured the distance to the nearest door. He saw Fenja there. He looked at the main entrance and saw Menja. He stopped measuring.

Near the stage, Anders raised his voice just enough to carry.

"Everyone remain calm. The Protectorate knows the building. They will not provoke a detonation."

It was exactly what a respected civic leader should have said. It also told his people that the newly exposed charges could remain where they were.

Taylor felt the effect move through the hostages. Shoulders lowered. Whispering thinned. The room accepted comfort from the man who had put explosives beside them.

In the clinic behind the stage, Danny and the second hostage lowered the injured donor onto an examination table. The room had been designed for demonstrations rather than emergency care. Its cabinets held sealed dressings, sample packages, and diagnostic equipment no one had expected to use. The escorting gunman swept a tray of brochures to the floor with his forearm.

"Keep him alive."

"I drive a forklift," Danny said. "If you've got a doctor, this is the point where you bring one in."

The gunman pointed through the glass partition at a gray-haired woman kneeling in the first hostage row. "She said she was an anesthesiologist. You get four minutes."

He left Danny with the other hostage and locked the treatment-room door from outside.

Taylor's moth stayed beneath Danny's collar. His pulse beat hard enough to move its feet.

*He wanted proof the caller was real,* McClane said. *Armsmaster just gave it to him over an open channel.*

The sound-board operator looked toward Anders.

Anders bowed his head as if comforting the councilwoman. His fingers tapped once against her shoulder.

"One vehicle," the operator said. "Armored. No tracking equipment. You have forty-five minutes."

Taylor slipped from beneath the table while every guard watched the frightened hostages. She returned through the concealed service door and moved toward the western stairs.

"Tower," Miss Militia said through the radio. "Confirm you are mobile."

Taylor keyed once.

"Confirm the caller's claim that Anders is directing the hostage takers."

Taylor keyed twice.

No.

The answer made her stop.

*You said he is.*

*He is. She asked whether you can confirm it. Right now you've got hand signals and a bug nobody else can hear through. That's knowing, not proving.*

Taylor hated the distinction because McClane was right.

Miss Militia waited, then said, "Understood. We have Anders visible on the hostage feed. He appears restrained and under guard. Armsmaster assesses the emergency caller may have mistaken his efforts to control panic for command activity."

*There it is,* McClane said. *Wrong picture, taken by a camera the bad guy pointed for them.*

The hostage feed was not merely a picture. It was a composition.

The camera showed Anders seated beneath the MedHall banner with a frightened councilwoman tucked against one side of him. Alabaster stood behind them with a weapon taken from a security guard, his white hair hidden beneath a black knit cap and his face angled away. The injured donor's empty place remained visible in the foreground. Every element said captive, guard, casualty.

It did not show the sound-board operator watching Anders's hands. It did not show the charge ten feet left of the frame. It did not show the clinic door where Danny was being held apart from everyone else.

Taylor sent a beetle over the camera housing. A red tally light glowed on the front. Two cables ran from the back, one toward the building's media cabinet and another down through a hole drilled roughly into the stage.

The second cable had not belonged to MedHall.

*Prepared feed,* she thought.

*Prepared story,* McClane corrected. *The picture's just how he's selling it.*

Outside the east windows, moths clustered beneath the floodlights gave Taylor fragments of the response Anders was selling it to. Police cars sealed the road at both ends. An ambulance crew had unfolded two empty stretchers beside a brick planter and now waited without patients. PRT troopers formed teams behind armored vans while officers pulled on helmets, checked seals, checked one another, and continued not entering.

Armsmaster stood within a loose circle of equipment cases beneath a portable canopy. A technician held a transparent building plan against a lit board. From Taylor's insects it was only a bright rectangle and moving shadows. Miss Militia stood apart from the others, facing MedHall instead of the plan.

The distance put them beyond Taylor's reliable detail. That was almost worse. There were dozens of trained people outside, all moving with purpose, and the man inside still controlled what they believed their purpose was.

"Tower, we need actionable observations. Number and identity of capes. Weapon positions. Explosive locations. Do not speculate on command."

Taylor keyed once.

She wanted to throw the radio through the wall.

Instead she pressed her thumbnail against the edge of its casing until the pain narrowed her attention.

*She's not dismissing you,* McClane said. *She's asking for something she can carry into an argument.*

*I gave them Anders.*

*You gave them your conclusion. Give her the work.*

Taylor looked through the moth in Danny's collar. The anesthesiologist had been brought into the clinic and was cleaning the donor's wound while the guard watched through the glass. Danny stood at the counter opening dressings before she asked for them. He kept glancing at the door, then at the ceiling, as if he knew Taylor might be in either direction.

He could not see the fly under the upper cabinet. He had no way to know she was watching him.

He still positioned himself between the doctor and the gunman.

Instead she returned to the storage room, unfolded her paper map, and compared it to the building through her swarm.

Hookwolf remained at the main stairs. Cricket patrolled the mezzanine. Alabaster covered the service corridor. Fenja and Menja had reduced their size enough to fit beneath the atrium ceiling and now guarded the east and main doors. Eleven armed personnel remained visible. At least four more moved outside her reliable range near the loading dock and parking bridge.

The charges sat against six supports, not three. Armsmaster had identified only the ones visible through the hostage camera.

Taylor clicked once and waited.

"Tower. Speak for ten seconds after the next public transmission. Begin with cape count."

Armsmaster's voice filled the enemy channel. "We have received your vehicle request. Before delivery, release ten hostages and provide a view of the injured."

Taylor pressed transmit while his words still echoed from radios throughout MedHall.

"Five capes. Hookwolf at main stairs, Cricket mezzanine, Alabaster west service, Fenja east doors, Menja main. Fifteen armed minimum. Six charges, west and north supports not visible on camera. Anders commands by hand signals."

Nine seconds.

She released the button.

The transmission felt much longer from inside it. On the third second Alabaster turned his head. On the fifth, Cricket stopped in the middle of a step. At seven, the sound-board operator lifted his free hand and began counting positions from the direction of the interference. At nine, a guard at the western doors looked directly toward the wall behind which Taylor crouched.

The sound-board operator looked down at his radio.

Every armed person in the building heard Armsmaster. Whether any had heard the whisper underneath depended on volume, distance, and attention.

Cricket turned toward the west service corridor.

She had heard.

*Move,* McClane said.

Taylor snatched up the inventory sheet and the pencil. Leaving either behind would tell them exactly where she had been and what she knew. The pencil rolled against her palm as she reached the door. She nearly dropped it, caught it against her skirt, and lost two seconds doing so.

The first guard entered the far end of the corridor before she reached the kitchen.

"West storage clear?"

"Checking now."

"She can't have crossed the atrium."

Cricket's answer arrived as three measured taps against the mezzanine rail, transmitted through the building more clearly to Taylor's insects than any voice. Men split at the next junction. One pair came through the service corridor behind her. Another descended toward receiving. A third moved to seal the parking bridge.

They were not chasing a teenage girl anymore. They were clearing sectors.

Cricket did not chase her body. She chased the channel. Guards began checking storage rooms from both ends of the service floor, one pair opening doors while another watched the corridor intersections. Taylor moved through the kitchen, passed beneath a prep counter, and climbed into the dumbwaiter shaft her bugs had found an hour earlier.

The kitchen was not empty. The fourteen catering workers had been moved out of the cold room and seated along the far wall with their hands locked behind their heads. A guard stood near the ranges, covering them and the two service doors at once.

Taylor came through the pantry on his blind side.

A dishwasher saw her first. His eyes widened. Taylor put one finger against her lips.

The guard noticed the change in his face and turned.

Taylor pulled open the nearest warming cabinet. A tower of clean sheet pans slid out, struck the tile one after another, and filled the kitchen with metal crashes. She went low while the guard brought his weapon around. The catering workers folded toward the floor.

"Stay down!"

He shouted it at everyone. Taylor crawled behind the prep island, sent flies into his eyes, and felt him stagger into the hanging rack. Ladles, strainers, and whisks swung above him. She reached the dumbwaiter, opened its narrow door, and got one knee inside.

The dishwasher stood.

For one second Taylor thought he meant to follow. Instead he caught the warming-cabinet door and shoved the remaining pans into the aisle behind her.

"There!" he shouted, pointing toward the opposite pantry.

The guard fired at the empty doorway.

Taylor pulled herself into the shaft before he could discover the lie.

The car sat one floor above. The shaft below dropped to receiving. Taylor braced her shoes against one wall and her back against the other, suspended in darkness while a guard opened the kitchen door.

"Radio traffic came from this section."

"Could be reflected signal."

"Cricket says it wasn't."

The man checked beneath the counters. Taylor felt his flashlight through roaches on the floor. Another opened the dumbwaiter door below her.

Light climbed the shaft and stopped inches beneath her shoes.

Taylor held herself above it until her legs shook.

The shaft had been built for trays, not people. Grease filmed the guide rail beside her cheek. The wall pressed cold through the back of her dress. She had one shoe wedged against a seam in the metal lining and the other braced on the narrow lip of the service opening. Every breath shifted her weight. Every shift made the cable above her tick against its pulley.

Below, the flashlight beam moved away from her feet and returned.

"Nothing."

"Check the car."

"It's upstairs."

"Then she went down the shaft."

The lower guard leaned inside. Taylor felt his hand close around the guide rail below her.

The radio at her waist clicked against the wall.

Both men went quiet.

The lower man raised his light. Its edge found the sole of Taylor's shoe.

"Got her."

*Up,* McClane said.

The dumbwaiter car above her was held by a steel cable. Taylor reached for the insects inside its mechanism, packed them into the door interlock, and forced the latch to release. The car dropped six inches before the brake caught.

The noise came from above.

The guards looked up.

Taylor pushed away from both walls. She slid down the shaft, struck the lower man's shoulders feet-first, and drove both of them through the open receiving door. He hit the floor beneath her. His flashlight spun away.

Her right ankle folded on impact. Pain flashed white along her shin. She rolled with it because McClane shouted the instruction before she knew she needed it, and the lower guard's hand closed on empty air where her shoulder had been.

The second guard lunged through the kitchen opening. Taylor rolled behind a stack of flour sacks as two suppressed shots punched into the dumbwaiter wall.

Flour burst into the air.

One round split a sack at shoulder height. The other struck the steel frame and threw a spark into the cloud. Taylor flinched from the flash and waited for fire that did not come. Her bugs became useless inside the flour. Wings clogged. Antennae lost the air. For three steps she was reduced to her own eyes in a white room.

The cloud hid the whole receiving room. Taylor sent insects low across the floor, found the gunman's legs, and moved opposite him. He fired toward a rolling cart she shoved with one hand. The bullets chased the cart. Taylor went through the loading-prep door behind him.

Her bad ankle struck the threshold. She bit down on the sound, grabbed the crash bar, and pulled the door shut behind her. A bullet punched through above her hand. Another hit the closer mechanism and showered her hair with plaster and metal filings.

The door no longer latched.

She seized a linen cart parked in the passage and drove it sideways beneath the handle. The guard hit the other side. The cart rolled six inches. Taylor put her back against it until a second crash bar opened farther down the hall and boots entered from ahead.

*Left,* McClane said.

*That's a closet.*

*Then find out what kind.*

Taylor went left. The narrow door admitted her into a janitorial room crowded with chemical drums, replacement bulbs, and a floor sink. No second exit. She closed herself inside and sent bugs into the wall.

Pipes rose behind the sink. Above them, an access panel opened into the cavity beneath the main staircase.

The screws had been painted over.

Taylor put the trauma shears under one edge and levered until the first screw tore through plaster. The corridor door opened behind her as she pulled the panel far enough to fit one shoulder through.

A hand caught the back of her dress.

Taylor twisted, leaving fabric in the guard's fist, and crawled headfirst among the pipes. Her injured ankle struck the frame. He grabbed for it. She kicked blindly and felt her heel connect with fingers.

Then she was through.

The wall cavity narrowed around her ribs. Dust filled her mouth. She dragged herself beneath the stairs while the guard tore at the opening behind her, reached a maintenance hatch on the far side, and fell through it into the western fire passage.

She did not stop until two fire doors separated them.

"Tower," Miss Militia said. "Threat map received. Break contact if pursued."

Taylor keyed once while running.

"Armsmaster is preparing a roof reconnaissance. No entry authorization. Repeat, no entry authorization."

Through a spider sheltering beneath the roof access, Taylor felt a vibration above.

Metal touched gravel.

Weight settled beside the skylight.

Someone was already on the roof.

*No entry authorization,* Taylor thought.

*Doesn't mean no entry,* McClane said. *Means somebody wants a closer look and thinks calling it reconnaissance changes what happens if they're spotted.*

Cricket left the mezzanine and climbed the north stairs.

Hookwolf moved away from the atrium.

Anders tapped two fingers against the floor among the hostages.

The sound-board operator raised his radio. "Roof team in position. Hold until both targets enter the stair enclosure."

Taylor pressed transmit.

"Miss Militia, they know. Abort the roof. They know."

The words went out openly. Every radio heard them.

For the first time that night, Max Anders looked toward no camera, no hostage, and no member of his crew.

He looked directly at the nearest ceiling speaker, as if Taylor's voice had given him a face to meet.

Then the roof access exploded.



Chapter 22: Hard Landing



The roof access door came down three flights of stairs.

Taylor felt its path through insects scattered across the north side of MedHall: the charge lifting steel out of its frame, the door striking the first landing edge, turning once, and crushing the railing beneath it. The blast blew roof gravel through the opening in a black wave. Smoke followed.

Two PRT troopers had entered the stair enclosure before it detonated.

The first went over the railing with the door. His safety line caught after twelve feet and slammed him into the concrete wall. The second fell backward onto the roof, one leg trapped beneath twisted framing.

Hookwolf entered the stairwell from below.

Cricket stepped onto the roof.

*Ambush,* McClane said. *One team split into two problems, and each problem gets a cape.*

The radio dissolved into overlapping voices.

"Roof Two is down!"

"North access compromised. We have an interior blast."

"All units hold positions."

"Roof One, report."

Miss Militia cut through them. "Tower, status of hostile movement?"

Taylor keyed twice, then realized yes or no meant nothing.

She transmitted openly. "Cricket on roof. Hookwolf in north stairs. Two PRT down, one hanging inside, one pinned outside."

The sound-board operator answered before Miss Militia could. "Your inside source has now compromised the safety of your personnel twice. Silence her channel, or the next consequence will be taken from the hostages."

*He means your dad,* McClane said.

Taylor was already moving toward the north service stairs.

"Tower, do not approach," Miss Militia said. "Recovery team is staging."

Taylor counted the people staging outside. Counted Hookwolf's progress upward. Counted the rise and fall of the hanging trooper's chest through a fly caught in his harness.

*They won't reach him first.*

*No,* McClane said.

"Tower, acknowledge."

Taylor turned the radio off.

*That's gonna hurt the relationship.*

*You turned yours off too.*

*Yeah. Usually right before doing something everybody told me not to do. I'm not criticizing. I'm recognizing the move.*

Taylor put weight on her right foot and nearly fell.

The ankle had swollen beneath the strap of her shoe. She crouched in the fire passage, removed both shoes, and pushed the right one beneath a radiator where no pursuing guard would notice it. A length of torn table linen became a compression wrap. McClane talked her through crossing it over the joint, pulling tight beneath the heel, and knotting it above the swelling.

*Can you walk?*

Taylor stood. Pain climbed her leg, but the joint held.

*Yes.*

*That's not what I asked.*

*It's the answer we have time for.*

She kept the left shoe on for four steps before the uneven height made the injured ankle worse. That one went beneath a linen cart. She ran barefoot toward the stairs, the marble cold and gritty beneath her feet.

The north corridor had been cleared of staff. Emergency lighting painted the floor in alternating red and shadow. Taylor ran with one hand against the wall and her swarm thirty feet ahead.

The blast had shaken the entire northern face of MedHall. Ceiling dust hung beneath the emergency lights. A sprinkler pipe had split near the stair enclosure and sprayed one wall in a silver fan. Water carried plaster, roof gravel, and a thread of blood down the corridor toward Taylor.

In the atrium, the hostages had thrown themselves flat when the explosion hit. Guards hauled them back into rows while the sound-board operator announced that the Protectorate had attacked the building. Anders helped the councilwoman sit up. He brushed white dust from her shoulder with the tenderness of a man cameras were meant to trust.

In the clinic, Danny braced both hands over the injured donor as a light fixture swung above the table. The anesthesiologist shouted for the guard to unlock the door. He did not.

Taylor felt all of it and kept moving.

Hookwolf reached the landing beneath the dangling trooper. The man hung unconscious, helmet gone, rifle suspended from its sling. Hookwolf extended one bladed arm toward the safety line.

Taylor sent every insect in the stairwell into the smoke detector above him.

The local horn sounded.

Hookwolf looked up. Not startled. Irritated.

The stairwell fire doors released from their magnetic catches. Three floors of weighted steel swung closed at once. The door above Hookwolf struck his extended arm and wedged against the blades. The door below shut cleanly, dividing him from the rest of the stairs.

It would not hold him. It did not need to.

Taylor entered on the ground floor, climbed one flight, and found the safety line anchored around the central rail. The trooper hung above her in drifting dust.

His line creaked each time his body turned. One glove dragged against the wall, leaving a black mark in the concrete dust. A laminated identification tab had torn loose from his vest and hung against his throat.

ORTEGA, L.

Taylor sent flies across his face. Air moved through his nose. Blood ran from his hairline, crossed one closed eyelid, and dropped into the open stairwell.

*Can you lower him?*

*Not with that knot.*

*Don't untie it. Rail's already bent. Use the buckle on the harness to shorten the drop, then cut the anchor when you've got his weight.*

Taylor climbed onto the damaged railing. The open stairwell fell beneath her farther than she allowed herself to measure. She reached the trooper's shoulder, caught the rear handle of his armor, and pulled him close enough to hook one arm through the harness.

He outweighed her by at least eighty pounds.

Her wrapped ankle trembled on the rail. She tried to take his weight and the first pull moved him less than an inch. The harness handle cut into her palm. The safety line drew taut above them and rotated Ortega away from the landing.

*Use the turn,* McClane said. *Don't fight it. Swing him.*

Taylor let the line carry him out, then pulled when he came back. His shoulder struck the landing edge. She caught the armor before he rebounded. On the second swing his chest reached concrete. On the third she got his upper body across the lip.

The railing shifted beneath her foot.

One anchor bolt tore free with a sharp metallic crack.

Taylor dropped flat across the landing and held Ortega by the harness while the rail leaned into the open shaft. Her injured foot slipped between two bars. For a second it carried both of them.

She screamed once, through closed teeth, and hauled until Ortega's weight came off her ankle.

The fire door above screamed. Hookwolf's arm widened inside it. Steel peeled around him.

The alarm horn continued above the tearing metal, absurdly small and orderly. Smoke detector. Fire door release. Please use the nearest marked exit. A system built to move civilians through an emergency kept announcing rules to a man who had turned himself into a demolition tool.

Taylor found the harness adjuster and hauled. The line shortened by inches. The trooper rose until his boots touched the landing edge.

"Wake up," she whispered. "Please wake up."

He did not.

She got one boot onto concrete, then the other. His weight shifted from the open shaft to Taylor all at once and drove her to both knees.

The fire door tore free.

*Cut and drag,* McClane said.

Taylor drew the trauma shears she had kept from the security office. The safety line resisted the first bite. She sawed twice, three times, while Hookwolf stepped through the ruined frame above.

The cord parted.

Taylor dragged the trooper through the door below and pulled its manual latch. Hookwolf struck the other side before she cleared the landing. The whole frame bowed inward.

The impact drove the latch tongue halfway out of its socket. Taylor planted both bare feet against the stair edge and pulled Ortega by the shoulder straps. His armor caught against the door closer. Hookwolf hit the frame again. Concrete dust jumped from every seam.

Taylor sent beetles into the closer arm and packed the hinge until its joint refused to fold. The door could no longer open far enough for Hookwolf's largest blades. A narrow spike punched through beside Taylor's face and swept sideways, searching.

She flattened herself over Ortega. The blade passed above her back, cut through his rifle sling, and carved a groove across the landing wall.

The rifle fell between them.

Taylor caught it before it slid through the gap beneath the door.

The trooper's rifle caught on the threshold. Taylor freed it, considered leaving it, then took it because leaving a weapon for Hookwolf felt worse.

She hauled the man down one stair at a time, his armor striking each edge. Her bugs spread through adjoining corridors looking for another route. The second-floor door opened into the mezzanine lounge, where armed guards now held the guests Taylor had tried to evacuate. The ground-floor door opened too close to the atrium.

Basement was the only choice.

Behind them, Hookwolf tore through the latched door.

He did not descend at once. Metal struck the ruined frame in short, controlled blows. Taylor understood why when her insects found him reducing the width of his transformed body to fit the stairwell without trapping himself. Even his violence had procedure.

That bought them one landing.

The trooper coughed.

"Easy," Taylor said. "You're inside MedHall."

His eyes opened without focusing. "Team?"

"One on the roof. Cricket has them."

He tried to rise. His left leg folded.

"Can't."

"Have to."

His voice belonged to someone much farther away than his body. He reached for the stair rail and closed his hand on air. Taylor caught his wrist.

"Your name is Ortega. You're in the north stairs. Your safety line caught you. I need you to look at me."

His eyes found her, lost her, then returned.

"Civilian?"

"Yes."

"You're bleeding."

Taylor looked down. The sole of her left foot had opened somewhere between the kitchen and the stairs. She had not felt it separately from the ankle.

"So are you. Up."

Taylor put his arm over her shoulders. Together they reached the basement landing as metal struck concrete above.

The recovered trooper found the radio control on his vest. "Roof Two to command. Inside, mobile with assistance. Roof One pinned. Hookwolf north stairs."

Static answered first. Then three people began speaking over one another.

"Roof Two, authenticate."

"Medical status?"

"Do not use the north stairs."

Ortega squeezed his eyes shut. "Challenge seven. Response Hawthorne. Possible concussion, left knee unstable. I have Tower with me."

Miss Militia answered. "Identity of assistance?"

The trooper looked at Taylor.

She shook her head.

"Tower," he said.

Silence held the channel for one beat.

"Understood," Miss Militia said. "Follow Tower's directions."

*Relationship might survive,* McClane said.

Taylor guided the trooper into the mechanical corridor and hid him with the MedHall guards in the laundry room. One guard took the rifle. The other began cutting away the trooper's pant leg.

The laundry had ceased being a hiding place and become a casualty station. A catering worker lay beneath two tablecloths with an oxygen mask held over her face. One of MedHall's guards sat against a dryer with his dislocated shoulder strapped to his chest. Towels had been sorted into clean stacks, bloodied stacks, and strips ready for bandages.

Ortega stopped at the sight of them.

"How many?"

"Eight here," the older guard said. "More upstairs."

"Fourteen catering in the kitchen," Taylor added. "Twenty-one in the mezzanine lounge. Main group in the atrium."

Ortega looked at her bare, bleeding feet, the torn dress, the radio at her waist, and finally the insect-drawn map unfolded across a laundry hamper.

"You made this?"

"Most of it."

He sat because his knee stopped giving him a choice.

"Roof One," the trooper said again.

Taylor reached through the building.

Cricket had not killed the second member. She had removed his radio, bound his hands, and dragged him to the skylight above the atrium where the outside cameras could see him kneeling.

The sound-board operator spoke over the public channel. "You attempted entry. One of your personnel is now our prisoner. Five civilians will be moved to the demolition points. Any further approach will be answered at one of those points."

Armed men entered the hostage rows and selected people.

They did not choose randomly. Taylor saw the pattern as soon as the first guard pointed. Each person had already made trouble, held influence, or mattered to someone who had.

The first was the defense attorney who had objected to the phone search.

The second was the councilwoman.

The third was Marcus Thorne.

The fourth was the injured man's wife.

The fifth was Danny.

The guard from the clinic unlocked the treatment room and crooked two fingers at him.

"You're going back out."

Danny looked at the donor on the table. The anesthesiologist had closed the scalp wound with adhesive strips, but his skin had gone gray beneath the blood.

"He needs an ambulance."

"He needed you to follow directions."

The guard caught Danny above the elbow. Danny did not pull away. He took one step, stopped beside the anesthesiologist, and placed the unopened dressing in her hand before allowing himself to be moved.

In the atrium, the five were made to stand in a line while everyone else remained on the floor. The injured man's wife looked toward the clinic door and asked whether her husband was alive. No one answered. Thorne tried to move between her and the nearest weapon. A guard struck him behind the knee and forced him down.

Danny found Anders watching from the front row.

"You," Danny said.

Anders let confusion cross his face for the cameras.

The guard shoved Danny toward the west support.

They took him toward the west support beneath the mezzanine, the charge Armsmaster had not seen on camera until Taylor reported it.

Anders remained seated with the hostages. His face showed horror at exactly the level the cameras required.

His fingers did not move.

They did not need to. The order had already been given.

Taylor turned her radio back on.

"Miss Militia."

"Tower."

"My father is one of the five."

The admission left the laundry room silent.

Miss Militia answered without asking Taylor's age, name, or why a civilian was doing any of this.

"Then we get all five away from those charges. Tell me what you need."

Taylor looked at the paper map, at the wounded trooper, at the stolen rifle, and at the trauma shears that had nearly failed on one safety line.

"I need the building plans," she said. "The real ones. Electrical, ventilation, alarms, every service route. And I need you to stop listening to Anders."

Outside, Armsmaster began to object.

Miss Militia cut across him on the channel.

"Plans transmitting now."



Chapter 23: Clearance



The building plans arrived on the wounded trooper's forearm.

His name was Luis Ortega. Taylor learned that while a MedHall guard wrapped his knee and the small display built into his armor filled with floor diagrams, utility layers, and emergency-system routes transmitted from the PRT command vehicle outside.

The display was made to be read through a helmet. Ortega had to hold his forearm six inches from his face and close one eye to keep the lines still. Taylor knelt beside him on the laundry floor while a catering supervisor steadied his elbow. Each time the radio spoke, the plan compressed to make room for a text box, then expanded again with a different floor centered.

"Command says these came from city inspection records," Ortega said. "Last full filing was eighteen months ago."

"The building changed since then."

"Buildings aren't supposed to do that secretly."

The older MedHall guard gave a short laugh with no humor in it. "This one does a lot of things it isn't supposed to."

The official plans disagreed with Taylor's map in six places.

A storage room existed where the plans showed open office space. The west service corridor ended twelve feet earlier on paper than it did inside the wall. Two ventilation trunks had been rerouted around the locked equipment room. The elevator machine space was larger than its listed dimensions. A stair behind the clinic had been removed from the latest plan but remained physically present.

Taylor marked each disagreement on her paper map. Ortega added a symbol beside it on the digital plan and sent the correction outside. Six small acts of disobedience became six tactical routes as soon as someone admitted they existed.

The hidden stair bothered her most. It connected the clinic, the first-floor service level, and the basement without touching any public hall. Anders could leave the atrium as a hostage, vanish behind the clinic, and reach the loading dock while cameras continued showing an old loop of him seated beneath the MedHall banner.

*That's his exit,* McClane said. *Vehicles at the dock, charges on the building, everybody outside watching the front. Whatever else he's doing, that's how the hostage becomes the survivor.*

Taylor drew a square around the stair.

*Renovations without permits,* McClane said. *His building, his secret hallways, his charges. Guy's been designing his own crime scene for years.*

Taylor overlaid the plans with her swarm and found the route Kaiser's crew had used all night: equipment room to hidden corridor, hidden corridor to sound booth, sound booth to every major support without crossing a public camera.

The charges were connected by radio, not wire. Each receiver sat inside a protective case beside the explosive block. The sound booth held the master transmitter.

Ortega enlarged the only receiver Taylor could describe precisely. Her insects supplied dimensions by walking its edges. He built the shape with a gloved fingertip: antenna, battery compartment, firing circuit, arming switch.

"Commercial blasting receiver with the markings removed," he said. "Modified case. Could be command detonation, timer, dead-man trigger, or any combination."

"Can you tell from the light?"

"Green means whoever assembled it wanted green to mean something. That's all I can promise."

Taylor had expected the uniform to come with answers. Ortega gave her limits instead, without pretending they were more useful than they were. It made her trust him more.

"Can we jam it?" Taylor asked.

Ortega shook his head. "Command might. Without frequencies, they'd have to flood the spectrum. That kills our radios too, and these could be set to trigger on signal loss."

"Can you disarm one?"

"If I can reach it and if it's conventional."

His injured leg answered whether he could reach the west support.

The MedHall guard finished wrapping it and tested Ortega's foot for circulation. Ortega tried to stand anyway. His knee bent sideways before he got halfway up. The catering supervisor and Taylor caught him beneath the arms.

"That's a no," the guard said.

"I noticed."

Ortega lowered himself onto a folded tablecloth. Frustration moved across his face once and was gone. He pointed at the plan instead.

"If you can get within sight of the receiver, describe every change when it arms. Do not pull the battery unless I tell you. Do not cut a wire because you've seen people cut wires on television."

*I feel unfairly targeted,* McClane said.

*Have you cut wires because you saw it on television?*

*No. I've cut them because there wasn't time to find the guy who knew better.*

Taylor studied the receiver through three insects. A toggle switch. Indicator light. Battery leads. Antenna enclosed in plastic. She could enter through the gap around the switch, but entering was not the same as understanding.

The plastic case vibrated faintly. Not a timer. A transformer or relay. Her insects could feel each pulse without knowing what the pulse meant. She withdrew them before a moving contact crushed the first ant.

*Don't touch what you can't identify,* McClane said. *We need the five people moved first. Bomb gets less useful once nobody's standing beside it.*

The west support sat beneath the mezzanine, twenty feet from the concealed service door Taylor had used earlier. Two gunmen guarded the five hostages. Fenja watched from the east side of the atrium. The sound-board operator could see the whole position.

Taylor and Ortega built the attempt around those four facts.

The concealed door could take the hostages out if it stayed unnoticed. The sound booth had to lose sight of the support. Fenja had to look east or up. The two gunmen had to be disabled together, because either one could shoot a hostage before Taylor crossed twenty feet.

There was no clean way to make all four things happen.

"Then don't make them happen together," Ortega said. "Stack them. First get proof. Then break sight. Then hit the guards. Leave the cape until movement starts."

Taylor looked at the atrium through her swarm. "Fenja can cross the room in seconds."

"Then you need fewer seconds."

*I like him too,* McClane said.

Taylor positioned moths beneath the sound-board camera. Beetles waited inside the concealed panel's floor track. Flies filled the upper atrium near the chandelier, not enough to attack Fenja, only enough to provide something large and sudden when Taylor needed her eyes elsewhere.

Anders rose from the hostage rows.

He asked permission to check on the injured man's wife. The operator granted it after a pause calibrated for the cameras. Anders crossed toward the west support with his hands visible.

*He's going to your dad,* McClane said.

Taylor picked up the stolen radio.

"I need the outside channel held open," she told Miss Militia. "No one speaks until I say."

"What are you attempting?"

"Proof."

Taylor left the laundry room through the mechanical corridor. Ortega caught her wrist before she passed.

"Take this."

He handed her a flat black device with a folding grip.

"Stun charge. Twist the cap, press the center, three-second delay. It will put everyone in a small room on the floor. That includes you."

"How small?"

"Smaller than the atrium."

She put it inside the torn hem of her dress.

"Three seconds means three seconds," Ortega said. "Not three after you hear it begin charging. The cap starts the circuit. The center commits it."

"What does it do?"

"Light, sound, pressure. Your inner ear will hate you. Keep your mouth open and don't be standing."

"You were carrying this on the roof?"

"We thought we were doing reconnaissance."

The shame in the answer did not belong to him. Taylor left before she said so, because neither of them had time to make the distinction feel better.

The hidden stair behind the clinic brought her to the first-floor service level. She crawled the last twenty feet through a ventilation return too narrow to turn around in, pushing the radio ahead of her. Dust coated her palms and entered her mouth every time she breathed.

Her wrapped ankle would not bend enough to follow cleanly. She had to pull the injured leg after her, toe scraping the sheet metal, while the cut on her other foot left a dark smear along the duct. The return narrowed around a bundle of cables halfway to the grille. Taylor exhaled, pushed the radio through first, and compressed one shoulder until she slid past.

Below her, a gunman shifted his stance.

Taylor stopped with the cable bundle pressed across her ribs. She could not retreat. She could not fill her lungs. Dust tickled the back of her throat.

The gunman looked up at the grille.

A fly landed on the charge receiver. Taylor sent it walking across the indicator light.

The gunman glanced down instead and nudged the case with his shoe. The light remained green. He returned his attention to the room.

Taylor moved again.

Below the grille, Anders stopped beside Danny.

"I owe you an apology," he said.

Danny looked at the charge fixed to the column, then at the gunman six feet away. "For the party?"

"For involving Taylor. That was not part of tonight's intention."

Danny's face changed. "You know where she is?"

"No. You should be proud of that. My people are very good. She has made them look remarkably ordinary."

Taylor placed the radio against the grille and held the transmit button with two beetles wedged beneath its edge.

Outside, Miss Militia listened.

"What people?" Danny asked.

Anders glanced toward the nearest gunman. The man looked away, granting privacy to the supposed hostage.

"Tell your daughter to come forward. She has a phone or radio. You will tell her that nobody needs to die tonight."

"You've got bombs on the walls."

"Precautions."

"You brought enough precautions to bury a room full of people."

Anders lowered his voice. "I brought enough to ensure that the people outside followed instructions. Your daughter is the only person making that arrangement unstable."

"Funny. From here it looks unstable all by itself."

"And if I don't?"

"You will. Parents discover clarity when consequences stop being theoretical."

Danny studied him for a long moment. Taylor recognized the stillness. It was the same stillness he carried into union meetings when management offered terms insulting enough to remove uncertainty.

"You're the one doing this."

Anders smiled faintly. "Your daughter reached that conclusion much faster."

"She's smarter than me."

"That intelligence is why she is in danger. Help me keep it from becoming the reason other people die."

Danny looked at the other four people beside the column. The defense attorney's wrists had been tied with white plastic. Thorne had blood on one trouser knee. The injured man's wife still watched the clinic door as if looking away would change whether her husband came through it.

"You picked these people."

"My associates did."

"Your people. Your bombs. Your building. But when something goes wrong, that's a fifteen-year-old girl's fault."

Anders's expression cooled.

"She is old enough to understand consequences."

"Then you should be embarrassed she understands them better than you."

Danny looked up toward the atrium cameras.

"You want me to call her? Fine."

His voice rose.

"Taylor, if you can hear me, stay hidden. Don't trust Max Anders. He's running this."

The nearest gunman struck him across the stomach with the stock of his weapon.

Danny folded but did not fall. The injured man's wife caught one arm. The defense attorney caught the other.

The contact drew the five into a tighter cluster. Taylor saw it through insects on their clothes and understood what Danny had done. He had taken the blow, but he had also put every hostage within reach of the concealed door and forced the guards to look at him.

Her father had built her an opening without knowing her plan.

Anders's smile vanished.

"Detonate the west charge if she does not surrender in sixty seconds," he told the gunman.

Miss Militia's voice came through Taylor's earpiece, quiet and controlled. "Proof received."

Taylor released the radio button.

Anders looked up toward the ventilation grille.

"There," he said.

The gunman raised his weapon.

Taylor twisted the stun charge, pressed its center, and dropped it through the grille.

Three seconds was long enough to crawl backward twice.

The device struck the carpet on its edge, bounced once, and rolled beneath the gunman's heel.

One.

Taylor backed over her elbows.

Two.

Anders looked down.

Three.

White light filled the duct. The sound struck like a hand across her whole body. Taylor lost her grip and slid headfirst into the turn behind her. For several seconds she could not hear McClane, the radio, or herself.

Pressure punched through her open mouth and closed it. Her chin struck the metal. The duct walls flashed white, then black, then returned in doubled outlines. She tasted blood where her teeth caught her tongue.

The right side of her body forgot which direction was down.

Her bugs kept reporting.

Both gunmen were down. Anders had turned away before detonation but remained on one knee, blinded and disoriented. The five hostages lay against the column in a tangle of limbs.

Taylor kicked the grille out and dropped.

Her knees failed on landing. The injured ankle collapsed completely, throwing her onto one hip. She crawled to Danny, grabbed his jacket, and pulled.

"Taylor?"

"Move. Service door. Everybody move."

The defense attorney's wrists were still bound. Taylor pulled Ortega's trauma shears from the dress and cut the plastic while Danny rolled onto one knee. Thorne tried to stand before his balance returned and went shoulder-first into the column. The injured man's wife had lost one shoe and kept turning toward the clinic.

"My husband."

"He's alive," Taylor said. "Doctor's with him. We cannot reach that door from here."

"I'm not leaving him."

The nearest gunman groaned and reached for his weapon.

Danny kicked it beneath a banquet table. "You stay, they put you back on the bomb. Move now, come back with people who can help him."

She looked at him, then at Taylor.

Taylor pointed toward the opening panel.

The woman moved.

The defense attorney recovered first. He helped the injured man's wife stand. Thorne staggered in the wrong direction until Danny caught his collar and turned him around.

Anders reached for Taylor's ankle.

His fingers closed over the linen wrap and found the swelling beneath it. Pain went through her leg hard enough to blur the atrium.

She stepped on his hand.

Not hard enough to break anything. Hard enough to make him release her.

"You," he said, blinking through the stun.

"Me."

McClane returned as a high, distorted voice beneath the ringing in her ears.

*Door, kid. Celebrate later.*

Taylor could not stand without Danny's arm beneath hers. She hated needing it. She used it anyway.

Taylor pushed the extraction toward the concealed panel. Fenja saw the movement from across the atrium and began growing. The sound-board operator reached for the transmitter.

Taylor released the moths above the chandelier.

They fell as a single dark sheet between Fenja and the west support. Fenja flinched and raised one hand across her face. The distraction lasted less than a second. It was the second Ortega had told Taylor she needed to remove somewhere else.

Danny pushed Thorne through the panel. The defense attorney pulled the injured man's wife after him. The councilwoman froze at the threshold, staring at the solid wall where no door had been visible a moment earlier.

"Through," Taylor shouted.

The councilwoman went through.

The west charge's indicator changed from green to red.

The receiver's vibration stopped.

Taylor remembered Ortega's warning. A stopped relay might mean the circuit was armed and waiting. It might mean the radio command had already closed it. It might mean the light had changed colors because a man at a workbench had thought red looked threatening.

She did not pull the battery.

Taylor drove her swarm into the receiver.

Ants packed the switch housing and filled the narrow space beneath the toggle. Beetles forced themselves around the battery leads. Their bodies bridged exposed contacts. The first layer died as current passed through it. Taylor pushed the next layer over them, then another, building a wet, conductive mass where the clean separation of the circuit had been.

The indicator flickered. Red, dark, red again.

The smell reached her a second later, burned insulation mixed with something smaller and organic.

The operator pressed the trigger.

Nothing happened.

Everyone near the column stopped.

For one suspended beat, even Fenja looked at the charge.

He pressed again.

The receiver smoked.

The protective case popped at one seam. Taylor threw herself through the panel and dragged it closed, expecting the support to detonate behind her.

Nothing followed except the sound-board operator shouting for a manual team.

Taylor got Danny through the panel as Fenja crossed half the atrium in three strides. Her enlarged hand struck the wall after the door closed and cracked plaster across the corridor.

The impact blew the concealed panel inward. Danny caught it on his shoulder. Taylor and the defense attorney shoved from the other side while Fenja's fingers curled around its edge.

The panel was decorative wood over a steel service door. Fenja peeled the wood away in strips, found the metal beneath, and pulled hard enough to bow it toward the atrium.

"It opens toward us," Thorne said.

"Not anymore," Danny answered.
