He seized the rolling rack Taylor had hidden behind earlier and drove it beneath the handle. The defense attorney locked its wheels. Fenja pulled again. The rack rose onto two casters, struck the ceiling, and wedged between wall and door.

"Run," Taylor said.

They ran.

The hidden stair brought them down toward the clinic. Behind them, Fenja tore the concealed door out of the wall. Ahead, Alabaster entered from the service hall.

He raised the security guard's weapon.

Taylor sent the last insects clinging to it into the trigger guard. Alabaster fired before he intended. The round struck the ceiling above Danny and showered the stair landing with tile.

The five hostages dropped. Taylor stayed upright only because Danny held her.

Taylor released the fire door between them. It struck Alabaster, knocked him backward, and shut. He began pounding against it immediately.

The latch had not caught. Alabaster's second impact opened it three inches.

Taylor pulled a red fire extinguisher from its bracket and shoved the cylinder through the crash-bar frame. The door closed against it at an angle. Alabaster forced one arm through the gap. The defense attorney kicked the extinguisher deeper until the metal handle trapped his wrist.

"That won't hold him," Thorne said.

"It doesn't have to," Taylor and Danny said together.

They looked at each other.

*Okay,* McClane said. *That was a little weird.*

"Basement," Taylor told the others. "Follow the bugs."

She laid a moving trail along the baseboard and sent the five down the hidden stair. Danny remained beside her.

"You first," he said.

"Dad."

"Not discussing it. Move."

Fenja's hand broke through the wall behind them.

Taylor tried to take the first stair alone. Her right leg folded.

Danny caught her around the waist and took her weight before she hit the steps.

"You first doesn't mean by yourself," he said.

Taylor went first because arguing would kill them both.

They descended together, Taylor hopping on her uninjured foot while Danny controlled the fall of each step. Behind them, plaster broke in slabs. Fenja's fingers entered the stairwell first, then her wrist, then an eye framed by the hole she was widening.

The defense attorney reached the basement landing and held the door. Thorne and the councilwoman pulled the injured man's wife through. Danny half carried Taylor across the threshold.

Fenja's hand closed around the upper rail.

Taylor sent beetles into the stairwell release. The basement fire door slammed. Fenja's fingers remained on the other side.

They reached the laundry room with Alabaster behind one fire door and Fenja widening the corridor above by force. Ortega and the MedHall guards pulled the five inside. The defense attorney took one look at the wounded trooper, the stolen rifle, and Taylor in her torn green dress, then decided questions could wait.

Danny did not.

"Bugs?"

Taylor looked at the trail still moving along the floor.

"Yeah."

Danny's gaze followed it to the map, then lifted to the insects stationed along the laundry ceiling. Taylor felt each one as he found it. Moths above the wounded. Flies at the door. Roaches disappearing beneath the baseboard. The room had been full of her for hours.

"All of them?"

"Most within a few blocks. I can feel through them. Not see exactly. It's complicated."

The building shook as Fenja struck the fire door above.

Danny absorbed the answer in the middle of the noise. His hand was still beneath Taylor's arm. He did not let go.

He closed his eyes once. Opened them. "Voice in your head too, or was Anders being colorful?"

*I like him,* McClane said.

"Yeah," Taylor admitted.

"John McClane," she added, because if she stopped after admitting the voice, she might never manage the rest. "He's a police officer. From somewhere else. He's been helping me."

Danny looked at her for several seconds.

"Does he control you?"

*Good first question,* McClane said.

"No. Never unless I let him, and he hasn't. He talks. I decide."

"Can he hear me?"

"Yes."

Danny looked somewhere just to the left of Taylor's face, as if politeness required giving an invisible man a place to stand.

"Then thank you," he said. "We can deal with everything else when my daughter isn't bleeding on a laundry-room floor."

McClane did not answer immediately.

When he did, his voice had lost every joke.

*Tell him you're welcome.*

"He says you're welcome."

"We survive first," Danny said. "You explain later."

It was more trust than she had expected to receive in a lifetime, delivered in eight words while something enormous tore apart the corridor above them.

On the radio, Armsmaster ordered the perimeter units to prepare for a forced breach. Miss Militia argued that the remaining five charges were still live. The sound-board operator announced that negotiations were over.

Taylor looked at the alarm diagram on Ortega's display.

Six evacuation zones. Magnetic fire doors. Sprinkler mains. Dedicated emergency signal to the fire department. A system designed to take control of MedHall away from every person inside it when smoke appeared.

*He owns the doors,* she thought.

*Then make the building open them,* McClane said.



Chapter 24: Final Approach



The fire system gave them six zones, five live charges, three usable exits, and one chance to make all of those numbers change at once.

The sixth structural charge lay blackened beneath the west support. Its receiver had fused around a mass of dead insects. The separate device at the roof access had already spent itself on the failed PRT entry. Five green indicators remained inside the building, and Kaiser still held the transmitter that could turn any one of them red.

Taylor spread the plans across the laundry-room floor. Danny knelt beside her. Ortega sat against a washer with his injured leg extended. The two MedHall guards watched the corridor. Around them, the people Taylor had pulled from the west support tried to become useful because the alternative was listening to the building come apart overhead.

The laundry machines transmitted each impact through their steel sides. Fenja struck the basement fire door above them. Alabaster hammered from the clinic passage. Between blows, the sound-board operator repeated his threat over every stolen radio: negotiations had ended, the inside source would be surrendered, no one would approach MedHall.

No one in the laundry suggested surrendering Taylor.

The defense attorney knew the fire code. The injured man's wife was a nurse. Marcus Thorne had stopped taking notes.

The councilwoman knew the original occupancy plan and which guests had been assigned to the mezzanine. The catering supervisor knew which kitchen doors stuck when the floor got wet. Danny knew how frightened people behaved at a union hall when one visible route became a crush point.

Taylor knew where every person inside MedHall was standing.

"Alarm releases every magnetic door," one guard said. "Main exits too."

"They pinned the east doors manually," Taylor said. "Main doors have Menja. Loading route has two armed teams."

"Sprinklers reduce visibility," Ortega said. "Works both directions."

*Not both directions,* McClane corrected. *You've got a few thousand eyes that don't care about water.*

Taylor marked the atrium, mezzanine, kitchen, clinic, basement, and parking bridge.

"The east group needs someone they recognize at the front," Danny said. "Otherwise the first gunshot turns them around."

The councilwoman touched the bruise forming along her cheek. "They know me."

"You just got off a bomb."

"So did you."

Danny considered that and nodded.

The defense attorney took the service route. He had argued with the gunmen twice already, which meant the catering workers and half the atrium had watched him refuse to be quiet. Thorne volunteered for the parking bridge because the guests upstairs knew his face and because, as he put it, someone needed to tell the cameras what the Protectorate had actually done if they survived.

"You aren't carrying the rifle," Taylor said.

"I had gathered that your trust remains conditional."

"You traded my description for an interview."

Thorne looked down at his empty notebook. "Conditional is better than I earned."

"When the alarm starts, the hostages will push toward the main entrance," she said. "Hookwolf can stop that by standing there. We need them divided before they move."

"How do you divide a hundred and sixty frightened people without talking to them?" Thorne asked.

Danny looked at the insects moving along the edge of the map. "Same way she got us here."

Taylor built three trails.

Building them was the easy part. Making strangers understand them was harder.

She ran a test along the laundry baseboard. Roaches formed a line, moved ten feet, then split around a drain. The defense attorney followed the wrong branch. Taylor stopped the insects, made them cluster into an arrowhead, and tried again.

"Better," he said. "Still looks like a nightmare."

"A nightmare pointing toward an exit," Danny said.

Taylor added pulses. Move, stop, move. At every doorway the insects would gather until a route leader crossed, then advance. Anyone who lost the trail could wait for the next wave.

The first gathered beneath the atrium tables and waited pointed toward the east doors. The second climbed the staircase and formed along the parking-bridge corridor. The third occupied the service aisle leading toward the kitchen and loading dock. Not enough insects to resemble arrows yet. Enough to become them when she gave the command.

Miss Militia listened while Taylor described the routes.

Outside units received names rather than directions. East became Engine. Parking bridge became Shield. Loading dock became Ramp. The basement group was Laundry, spoken without irony by everyone except McClane.

*I've worked operations with worse names,* he said. *Not many, but worse.*

"Parking bridge team will breach from outside on your signal," she said. "Fire crews will take the east approach. Armsmaster can direct a suppression pulse at the receiver frequency, but only after the alarm masks the power draw. We cannot guarantee the charges will fail."

"One already did," Taylor said.

"Because you destroyed its receiver," Miss Militia said. "Four of the five remaining locations are outside your safe reach."

The fifth sat behind the basement mechanical-room wall, six feet from the laundry where they were planning. The official blueprints called the space a valve recess. Kaiser's altered corridor had turned it into a protected demolition point.

Ortega looked at Taylor. "If you can open the service panel, I can talk you through that one. Then suppression only has four chances to go wrong."

*Better number,* McClane said. *Still not a good number.*

The older MedHall guard found the panel beneath three coats of paint. The defense attorney and Danny pulled on its recessed handle together. It opened two inches and stopped against a steel conduit added after the plans were filed.

Taylor lay on the floor and reached through the gap with her swarm.

Ortega could not see the receiver. Taylor described it one component at a time. He asked where the antenna entered the case, whether the battery leads shared a harness, whether the red wire reached the explosive block or only the indicator board.

"There are two red wires."

"Of course there are. Follow both."

One ended at the light. The other disappeared beneath the firing relay.

"Do not disturb that one. Is the antenna screwed to the board or soldered?"

Taylor moved beetles over the connection. "Screwed."

"Back the retaining nut off. Slow. If the light changes, stop."

Twenty ants braced against the small metal nut. More joined them. The nut moved less than the width of an insect leg, then again. Taylor felt each thread release through the bodies pushing it.

The green light remained steady.

The antenna separated from the board.

"Now bridge the test contact before you disconnect the battery," Ortega said. "We want the receiver to believe it still has continuity."

Taylor obeyed. The light dimmed but did not change color. The MedHall guard reached through with insulated forceps and removed the battery plug.

Green went dark.

No explosion followed.

Four remained.

The route leaders still had to reach their routes.

The defense attorney could begin from the service corridor with Taylor's group. Thorne's path was narrower: up the unlisted clinic stair, through a maintenance crawlspace, and into a locked closet beside the mezzanine lounge. Taylor packed beetles into the latch until it turned. Thorne climbed alone, stopping every time a guard crossed the hall beyond him.

The councilwoman's route required something worse.

"You can't reach the east side without crossing the atrium," Taylor said.

"Then I cross the atrium."

"They'll see you."

"They already want me where the cameras can see me."

She removed the blanket from her shoulders, straightened her wet jacket, and walked out through the clinic passage with both hands raised. Taylor tracked her through insects along the ceiling.

The first gunman shoved her against the wall and demanded where the others had gone.

"Your bomb went off in my face," she said. "I woke up alone."

He searched her, found no radio or weapon, and returned her to the atrium because Max Anders gave one small nod from the hostage rows. The councilwoman sat where they put her. She did not look toward any insect. She did not look toward the east doors.

Thorne reached the mezzanine closet and waited in darkness.

All three route leaders were in place.

*Better number,* McClane said. *Still not a good number.*

"If the pulse triggers them?" Danny asked.

Miss Militia did not soften the answer. "We lose structural sections of the building."

"And if we do nothing?"

"Kaiser retains the triggers."

"He also has a hostage on the roof," Ortega said.

Roof One remained bound beside the skylight with Cricket between him and every exterior approach. Through insects sheltered in the gravel, Taylor felt him working one wrist against the plastic restraint. Each movement stopped when Cricket turned her head.

Miss Militia answered. "Shield team takes the bridge, then the north stair. We recover him during the evacuation."

"Cricket knows you're coming."

"Yes."

There was no promise that knowing would be enough.

Hearing the name spoken outside Taylor's head changed the room. Max Anders had become Kaiser over an open government channel. Danny absorbed it with one slow breath.

"Then we don't leave him the triggers," he said.

Taylor reached for the detector outside the linen room.

Her insects entered six alarm housings at once. She packed bodies into sensing chambers, bridged test contacts, and held them short of activation.

"Ready," she told Miss Militia.

The word passed outward through three command nets. Firefighters lowered face shields. PRT troopers checked the person ahead of them and fixed gloved hands to the backs of shields. Ambulance crews opened every rear door and laid triage tags across the hoods. Police moved the press line another fifty yards from the glass face of MedHall.

Inside, Taylor moved insects into place on collars, sleeves, table legs, and door frames. She counted the hostages one last time.

One hundred and fifty-seven in the atrium now, including the returned councilwoman. Twenty-two waited on the mezzanine after Thorne joined them. Fourteen catering workers remained in the kitchen. The injured donor and anesthesiologist were locked in the clinic. Eight wounded and hidden personnel occupied the laundry with Ortega. Four people, including Taylor and Danny, were about to leave it.

The totals matched.

"Parking team ready. Fire crews ready. Electronic suppression ready."

Armsmaster entered the channel. "On your initiation, Tower."

No argument now. No dismissal. Only a command structure acknowledging where the useful information lived.

Taylor looked at Danny.

He put one hand over hers. "Bring the roof down if you have to. Just make sure we're not under it."

"That's not comforting."

"Wasn't meant to be."

He tore a clean strip from a tablecloth and wrapped it around Taylor's bleeding left foot. There was no time to improve the splint on her right ankle. He tied the cloth, checked that she could feel her toes, and put her hand on his shoulder when she stood.

"I can move."

"I know. You're still leaning on me until we split."

McClane laughed once inside her head.

Taylor closed the alarm contacts.

MedHall screamed.

The first horn caught everyone unprepared despite the warning. Danny flinched. Ortega grabbed for a weapon he no longer carried. The injured man's wife closed her eyes and whispered her husband's name once.

Then the laundry group moved.

Horns struck every floor. Magnetic locks released. Fire doors swung. Emergency lights strobed while the central panel notified a fire department already waiting at the curb.

The signal also killed the elevators, opened smoke vents, released kitchen gas valves, and unlocked a dozen ordinary doors Kaiser's crew had trusted the hostages not to test. Across Taylor's swarm, MedHall changed shape. Barriers vanished. New barriers dropped. Air began moving through corridors that had been still all night.

The sprinklers followed when Taylor opened the deluge test valve through a mass of beetles packed around its lever. Water hammered the atrium, flattened table linens, and turned polished stone into a mirror broken by running feet.

The hostages rose.

Not together. The people closest to the doors stood first. Those in the center saw movement and followed without knowing why. Someone shouted fire. Someone else shouted bomb. A man near the stage tried to lift his wife before she had her feet beneath her and pulled both of them down.

Anders's gunmen raised their weapons over a crowd that no longer offered clean lines of fire.

"Stay down!" the sound-board operator shouted.

The insects became arrows.

They poured from beneath tables in black streams, gathered at three aisle mouths, and advanced in pulses. Screams broke out near the first trail. Then the councilwoman stepped into the east aisle and followed it.

"East doors! Stay with me! Do not run!"

Her authority did what the insects could not. People recognized her voice. The first rows turned.

The defense attorney climbed onto a fallen chair beside the service aisle. "Kitchen and loading dock! If you can walk, this route! Leave the main doors clear!"

Thorne came out of the mezzanine closet with one hand on the wall because the stun charge still owned part of his balance. "Bridge route! This floor, toward the parking lights!"

Three human voices converted Taylor's arrows into choices.

One third of the crowd followed the east trail. Another surged toward the kitchen. Guests on the mezzanine ran for the parking bridge as the outside team broke its doors inward.

The split was not even. Too many people chose the loading route because it looked farthest from Fenja. A knot formed at the service door. The catering supervisor, still in the laundry, called instructions through Ortega's radio. Danny repeated them into the corridor.

"Both kitchen doors! Use the pantry too!"

Taylor diverted half the insect trail beneath the prep counters and through the second door. A cook saw it, understood, and began pulling people around the bottleneck.

Kaiser's people tried to stop three evacuations at once.

Fenja grew across the east doors. Firefighters on the other side drove a hydraulic spreader between the frames while guests pressed back from her widening feet.

The doors bowed inward around the tool. Fenja planted both hands against the lintel and grew until her shoulders struck the atrium ceiling. Plaster broke around her. The councilwoman stopped ten feet away and threw both arms wide to keep the group from compacting behind her.

"Back three steps! Give them room!"

The first ranks obeyed. The people behind them did not hear. Pressure moved forward through the crowd.

Taylor reversed the nearest insect arrow. Its point turned sideways and crawled toward a secondary conference-room door. The councilwoman saw the change.

"Split left! Left side with the bugs!"

Half the group peeled away before the crush reached the doors.

Menja moved toward the main entrance and left the kitchen route open for four seconds.

Four seconds emptied thirty people into the service corridor.

Then Menja understood the feint and turned. Her enlarged hand swept the aisle at shoulder height.

People dropped beneath it. The defense attorney did not. Danny tackled him behind a banquet table as Menja's fingers took the chair off its feet and sent it skidding across the wet floor.

Taylor sent every beetle in the nearby table linens into Menja's shoes. They could not hurt her. They made the polished floor move beneath her like gravel. Her next step slid six inches.

Six inches gave twelve more people the service door.

Cricket met the parking team in ankle-deep sprinkler water. Miss Militia came through the breached doors behind a shield line, saw Cricket begin moving before the first weapon cleared, and ordered the team to stop advancing.

The order ran backward through the formation. Boots planted. Shields locked. The trooper at the rear nearly drove the line forward before the person ahead absorbed the impact.

Cricket had already shifted left to avoid the attack their momentum predicted.

Nothing entered the space she abandoned.

For the first time, Taylor felt hesitation in the movement of the cape who had spent the night arriving before everyone else.

Cricket adjusted for an attack that did not arrive.

Miss Militia fired into the ceiling above her. Tile and insulation collapsed across the whole corridor, not aimed at Cricket, not readable as a strike on her body. She flinched from the same field of noise Taylor had used.

The shield line did not charge. The lead trooper dropped flat. The second went high over him. A foam projector behind them fired at the floor, the wall, and the ceiling in three separate bursts. None targeted Cricket. Expanding containment foam met in the space around her and removed every route at once.

Cricket jumped for the railing.

Miss Militia's weapon became a hooked launcher between one heartbeat and the next. The line caught the rail ahead of Cricket, not her body, and tore the whole section sideways. Cricket landed where the rail had been and went through the gap to the lower landing.

She hit hard, rolled, and still began moving before the nearest trooper aimed.

The team stopped again.

Taylor filled the landing with insects flying in different directions. Cricket read a thousand false beginnings. Miss Militia advanced through the center of them with her shield raised and drove Cricket away from the bridge doors.

The PRT line moved through it.

Behind them, two troopers broke toward the north stairs and the captive on the roof.

*I definitely like her,* McClane said.

Hookwolf entered the atrium at full size.

Metal consumed the shape of the man beneath it. Blades rolled over one another in a body taller than the stage, broad enough to close the main aisle. He tore through tables and drove the hostages away from the exits by existing in front of them.

The crowd recoiled as one body. People who had been following three routes forgot all of them and moved away from the moving metal. The east group collided with the service group. Thorne's bridge line stopped at the corridor mouth. The insect arrows vanished beneath trampling shoes.

Hookwolf struck the nearest table with one forelimb. It split down the center. Plates, silverware, and broken crystal joined the sprinkler water.

"Back on the routes!" Danny shouted.

No one heard him over the alarms and Hookwolf.

Taylor made the insects climb.

Instead of trails along the floor, they rose up tablecloths, walls, and people's wet clothes. Three columns formed above the crowd, each leaning toward an exit. Heads lifted to follow them. The councilwoman saw hers first and began moving again.

Hookwolf saw the insects too.

His bladed head turned toward the service corridor.

Toward Taylor.

Taylor felt the chandelier above him.

All night she had counted its weight, cables, and position without knowing why.

Now she found the maintenance winch above the third-floor ceiling. A safety pawl held the drum. The electrical release had been disconnected from the building controls during the lockdown, but the manual mechanism remained. Insects entered the housing. They packed beneath the pawl, pushed where one body could not matter and hundreds could.

The winch had not been serviced in years. Grease trapped the first ants before they reached the pawl. Roaches wedged between gear teeth and were crushed when the drum shifted under the chandelier's weight. Taylor replaced every lost insect without looking away from Hookwolf.

He began moving toward her.

Danny saw him turn. "Taylor."

"I know."

The defense attorney pulled two civilians through the kitchen door. Menja recovered her footing and came after them. Taylor held the service trail in place, the evacuation, the winch, the remaining charges, and Hookwolf's route all at once.

Her injured ankle shook. Danny's hand closed around her upper arm.

The pawl lifted one tooth.

The drum moved.

The cable paid out three inches and caught.

Hookwolf crossed the dance floor.

Taylor forced the insects deeper. Another row died between steel teeth. The pawl lifted a second time.

Crystal chimed overhead.

Hookwolf looked up.

*Now that's a distraction,* McClane said.

Taylor released the brake.

The chandelier fell.

Its lights remained on for the first ten feet.

Crystal tiers descended through sprinkler water, every drop turning white in the electric glare. The suspension cables whipped loose above it. Guests beneath the outer edge saw the light moving and threw themselves away.

Hookwolf tried to stop.

His forward blades dug furrows through the marble. Momentum carried the rest of him on.

Not onto the hostages. Not onto Hookwolf. Between him and the crowd, exactly where three tons of crystal and steel could become a wall instead of a weapon.

It struck the dance floor with a sound that erased the alarms for one enormous instant. Crystal exploded outward. The steel frame folded. Hookwolf disappeared behind a mountain of cable and light.

The impact drove air across the atrium. Taylor felt it against her face from the service hall. Windows shook in their frames. Every remaining chandelier bulb went dark at once.

Hookwolf did not stay buried. Blades punched through the fallen lattice before the last crystal stopped moving. He tore at cables wrapped around his body, but three tons of frame had divided him from the hostages and forced him to spend time becoming dangerous again.

The main aisle opened.

People ran.

Taylor led the basement group toward the loading corridor. Ortega stayed behind with the guards and wounded. Danny kept the defense attorney and nurse moving. The defense attorney carried the stolen rifle without being trusted to use it.

The group had grown in the service corridor. Kitchen workers joined them. Guests arrived in wet formal clothes, some barefoot, some carrying strangers' children, all trying to pass through a route built for two catering carts abreast.

The defense attorney moved to the front and began calling the floor hazards. Danny stayed at the rear until Taylor ordered him forward. The injured man's wife kept asking everyone coming from the clinic whether they had seen a gray-haired doctor and a man with a bandaged head.

No one had.

Alabaster waited at the clinic junction.

The fire door had not held him. One shoulder remained crushed from forcing it, then reset while Taylor watched, bone and fabric returning to the state they had occupied seconds before.

The fire extinguisher they had used to trap his arm lay folded against the wall. Alabaster stepped over it. His stolen weapon was gone, but he carried a kitchen knife in each hand.

The civilians behind Taylor stopped. The corridor continued filling.

*He resets,* she thought.

*Then stop trying to damage him. Put him somewhere returning to normal doesn't help.*

The service elevator stood open behind him, recalled by the fire system and disabled at ground level.

Taylor sent the swarm across Alabaster's face.

He charged through it. Taylor retreated toward the elevator on one good foot, let the injured ankle buckle, and turned the fall into something that looked accidental. Alabaster saw a hurt girl trapped against an open elevator and accelerated.

Danny saw the same thing and stepped toward her.

Taylor held up one hand.

Trust cost him more than action would have. He stopped.

Alabaster crossed the threshold after her.

Taylor threw herself sideways between the closing doors.

Alabaster caught her wrist.

His fingers closed hard enough to grind the small bones together. Taylor struck the back of his hand with her free fist. The grip did not loosen. His reset erased the redness before it formed.

Danny seized the back of her dress and pulled. Fabric tore. Taylor came free as the elevator doors closed on Alabaster's arm.

The doors reopened automatically.

He stepped forward.

Taylor backed into Danny. Alabaster smiled once, having learned the trick and believing that meant it would not work twice.

Taylor filled the safety sensor with insects.

The doors closed again. This time the sensor read a clear opening until metal struck metal. They caught Alabaster across the chest and forced him backward into the car.

He drove both knives into the door edges. One blade snapped. The other held the gap at two inches.

Taylor sent beetles over his hand and into the opening. He released the knife to swat them away. Danny kicked the broken blade clear. The doors completed their travel.

Taylor jammed the exterior call button, then packed the door track with roaches after it shut.

Alabaster hit the doors from inside.

The metal bowed. The track held.

Taylor looked at the elevator control cabinet through insects already inside it. The fire recall had opened the main contactor and dropped the brake. Restoring power might let Alabaster move the car. Destroying the brake might drop it with him inside.

She touched neither.

The disabled elevator went nowhere. Neither did he.

"That's disgusting," the defense attorney said.

"Give me the rifle."

He did.

The building lights dimmed.

Ortega's voice came over the radio. "Suppression in three. Two. One."

Armsmaster's suppression pulse struck the charge network.

Every radio in MedHall screamed static. Emergency lights flared blue. Taylor's insects on the receivers felt current jump where no current had been, antennas becoming entry points for a pulse designed to burn out anything listening.

Three receivers died.

Green lights vanished at the east support, beneath the stage, and beside the loading ramp.

The fourth triggered.

The north receiver went red.

Taylor had time to understand which part of the map that meant.

"Down!"

The north support exploded beneath the mezzanine.

Floor lifted. Glass curtain wall bowed outward. A section of balcony dropped six feet and hung from twisted reinforcement while tables, railing, and water poured into the atrium below.

The shock reached the service corridor through walls and floor. Taylor hit the tile beneath Danny. Ceiling panels fell across the civilians ahead. The trapped elevator car slammed against its rails. Alabaster stopped pounding for one second, then began again.

Air rushed toward the atrium and carried smoke back with it.

Taylor's swarm vanished in a rough sphere around the north support. Hundreds of points of awareness ended together. The absence struck harder than the sound. For an instant the building map inside her mind had a hole torn through it.

McClane said her name.

Taylor rebuilt from the edges.

Miss Militia's team had cleared the bridge corridor seconds earlier. The hostages beneath it had followed Taylor's trail toward the east doors. The collapse found empty floor.

Mostly empty.

Two armed men remained beneath the mezzanine. One disappeared completely from Taylor's swarm. The other landed beyond the falling railing and did not rise. A server who had returned for an elderly guest went down beneath a table at the edge of the collapse.

"Civilian down, north atrium," Taylor said into a radio filled with static. "One civilian alive under a table."

Miss Militia answered through interference. "Received. Rescue One diverting."

The councilwoman left her east group in a firefighter's hands and turned toward the fallen server. Two PRT troopers reached the table first. Together they lifted the broken railing and pulled the server free.

The sound-board operator disappeared beneath the edge of the falling stage.

The master transmitter fell with him.

It struck the flooded floor, skipped once, and went dark.

Kaiser rose from the hostage rows with steel growing from both hands.

There was no reason left to perform.

Blades unfolded along his forearms, thinner and cleaner than Hookwolf's mass, catching emergency light as he crossed the ruined atrium toward the service hall where Taylor's group was moving.

People who had spent the night accepting comfort from Max Anders saw the blades emerge from his skin.

The hostage camera saw them too.

Kaiser looked toward it, understood what had just been recorded, and cut the camera from its mount. It fell still transmitting, its last image tilted across his face and the steel extending from both hands.

"Loading dock," Taylor said. "Go!"

Danny moved the civilians ahead. Taylor stayed behind because every insect in the building told her Kaiser was faster than they were.

Her father looked back once.

"Go," Taylor said.

"Not leaving you."

"Then get them to the ramp and come back."

It was a terrible compromise. It was the only one that moved him.

Danny put the injured man's wife in front and drove the group toward the loading dock. The defense attorney hesitated with the rifle.

Taylor held out her hand.

He surrendered it and followed.

She checked the chamber the way McClane had taught her through description. The weapon had rounds remaining. She engaged the safety and laid it across the corridor, directly in Kaiser's path.

*You don't fight him,* McClane said.

*I know.*

*You make him fight the building.*

Taylor opened every fire door between them.

The first door released behind Kaiser instead of ahead of him. It struck his back and forced him into the corridor. The second divided him from two gunmen trying to follow. The third closed between Taylor and the loading ramp.

She had trapped herself on his side.

*That one part of the plan?* McClane asked.

*No.*

*Good. Hate it when the bad part was on purpose.*

Kaiser cut through the first. The second slowed him. He drove a blade into the third and levered it from its track.

He reached the rifle.

Taylor held her breath.

Kaiser kicked it behind him without touching it.

He had seen the bait.

Taylor opened the kitchen gas suppression system.

White vapor filled the corridor. Kaiser emerged through it, suit soaked, blades bright, all the gracious warmth finally gone from his face.

The suppression cloud killed visibility for human eyes. Taylor tracked him through flies on the ceiling, roaches beneath the wall trim, and a beetle carried on the cuff of his ruined jacket. He moved more slowly inside the white, one blade held ahead to find obstacles.

Taylor moved without looking back. Bare feet found water, broken tile, then the rubber surface of the loading ramp. The cut beneath the left foot opened again. Her right ankle stopped supporting weight at all.

She reached the clinic junction and fell behind an overturned instrument cart.

Kaiser heard the impact.

He changed direction.

"You have no idea what you've interrupted," he said.

"You keep saying that like it makes the bombs less yours."

"This city is held together by arrangements you cannot begin to understand. Tonight would have removed a threat to those arrangements without harming anyone who mattered."

Taylor thought of the server under the railing. Ortega hanging in the stairwell. The donor bleeding in the clinic. The dishwasher pointing a gunman away from her. One hundred and ninety people chosen as scenery for Max Anders's survival story.

"That's your problem," she said. "You keep deciding who matters."

"A charity gala?"

The answer carried McClane's cadence and Taylor's anger.

Kaiser lunged.

Taylor dropped beneath the first blade. It cut the wall where her neck had been. She threw the stolen rifle past him instead of trying to fire it. His eyes followed the weapon for one fraction of a second.

The second blade came low. Taylor rolled beneath the instrument cart. Steel cut through its upper tray and sent sealed syringes across the floor. Kaiser kicked the cart aside.

Taylor tried to stand.

Her ankle refused.

Kaiser raised one blade over her.

A cloud of insects struck his face from behind. He had expected them all night. His steel mask formed before they arrived.

That was not the attack.

The beetle on his cuff crawled beneath one overlapping plate and forced him to glance down. Taylor seized the cart's severed frame and drove it between his ankles. It did not knock him over. It made him take one step.

One step put him in the cylinder's path.

Her swarm found the rolling oxygen cylinder beside the clinic door.

She released its brake.

Danny, twenty feet down the ramp, saw it move and kicked it with both feet.

He had come back.

The cylinder rolled into Kaiser's knees. He cut it open by reflex.

Compressed gas erupted. The cylinder became a missile inside the narrow corridor, spinning, striking walls, wrapping its regulator hose around Kaiser's legs. He went down beneath a snarl of steel and white vapor.

The torn cylinder struck the ceiling, rebounded, and hit the floor behind him. Its valve assembly shattered against the baseboard. The remaining pressure drove it down the corridor through the door Kaiser had cut apart.

Steel blades grew from the floor around Taylor.

Kaiser had fallen, not lost control. Narrow fins rose through tile and caged the space around her. One caught the torn hem of her dress. Another formed beside her hand.

Danny reached the top of the ramp.

"Don't," Taylor shouted.

Kaiser pulled the regulator hose from his legs and began to rise.

Taylor sent insects into the sprinkler water pooled around the steel growths. Not to short anything. To find the shallowest path between them. She turned sideways, tore the dress free, and crawled through while the gap remained wide enough.

A blade closed behind her and cut a line across her back.

Danny caught both her wrists and dragged her clear.

Taylor ran.

They reached the loading dock as firefighters forced the outer shutter upward. Cold rain blew through the widening gap. One by one, the civilians crawled beneath it into floodlights, ambulances, and armed PRT lines.

The shutter rose only three feet before its motor failed. Firefighters held it with cribbing while the defense attorney crawled through first and pulled from outside. The injured man's wife followed, then stopped beneath the edge when she saw the ambulance crews.

"My husband is in the clinic! Gray suit, head wound, anesthesiologist with him!"

A paramedic repeated the description into a radio and sent a team toward the east breach.

Across the parking lane, Thorne emerged behind the bridge shield team with his empty hands raised for the cameras. The councilwoman came out through the east breach fifty yards away with someone else's blood down one sleeve. Above them, PRT troopers brought the rescued roof officer down an aerial ladder.

Three routes delivered three fragments of the same hostage population into a perimeter that had prepared for one door.

Ambulance crews adapted. Colored lights moved. Triage tarps opened. Names began replacing Taylor's numbers.

Danny went through, then turned and held out his hand.

Taylor took it.

Her injured leg would not clear the shutter. Danny lay flat in the rain, caught her beneath both arms, and pulled while a firefighter lifted the metal another inch. The torn fabric at Taylor's back snagged on the threshold and released.

She landed against Danny on wet pavement.

Behind her, Kaiser cut himself free.

He reached the loading door with one sleeve gone, steel masking half his face, and the hose still tangled around one leg. Floodlights erased every shadow he might have used. Police cameras, PRT cameras, news cameras beyond the line, and dozens of witnesses watched Max Anders walk out wearing Kaiser.

Miss Militia entered the corridor from the atrium with her weapon trained on him. Armsmaster came from the clinic side. Kaiser stopped between them, blades extended, every route finally owned by somebody else.

"Max Anders," Miss Militia said, loud enough for the loading dock and every open channel. "Retract the blades. Kneel. Put both hands on the floor."

Kaiser looked through the shutter at Taylor.

Steel began growing from the dock frame.

Armsmaster drove the end of his halberd into the wall. A blue pulse crossed the metal Kaiser had formed. The growth fractured at its base before it could close the opening.

Miss Militia's weapon changed into a compact launcher.

"Last instruction."

For a second Taylor believed Kaiser would force them to shoot him in front of the cameras, making one final attempt to choose the story.

Then he looked at the ruined atrium behind Miss Militia, the clinic corridor behind Armsmaster, and the civilians on the pavement beyond Taylor.

The blades withdrew one inch.

Then another.

He went to his knees.

For one second Taylor saw him as McClane had seen him at the beginning: not a gracious host, not an untouchable civic leader, only a man checking exits because he knew what he was.

There were no exits left.

Outside, rain struck Taylor's face. Danny pulled her against him while MedHall burned light and water through its broken glass behind them.

The moment she stopped moving, everything hurt separately. Her ankle. Her cut foot. Her wrist where Alabaster had held it. The line across her back. Her tongue. The deep bruising left by the stun charge. Adrenaline had held them together as one instruction. Now they became a list.

Danny felt her knees give and lowered her to the pavement instead of letting her fall.

"Medic!"

Taylor caught his sleeve. "Count first."

"Taylor."

"Please."

*He's safe,* McClane said.

Taylor watched the remaining hostages emerge through three separate routes, counted until every group matched the numbers on her map, and let herself believe it.

The anesthesiologist appeared at the east doors between two firefighters, one hand holding the dressing against her patient's head. His wife broke from the triage line and reached him before anyone could stop her.

The catering workers came through the loading dock in a group of fourteen. The kitchen dishwasher saw Taylor on the pavement and lifted one hand. She returned the gesture.

Ortega emerged last from the basement route on a rigid litter, furious about the straps and awake enough to give Taylor a thumbs-up.

One hundred and fifty-seven. Twenty-two. Fourteen. Clinic two. Laundry eight. Her extracted group. Roof team two.

No missing pockets remained inside her swarm.

"Yeah," she whispered. "He is."



Chapter 25: What Gets Out



By morning, everybody knew Max Anders was Kaiser.

The first public version of the story had begun assembling before Taylor left the MedHall loading dock.

News helicopters held beyond the PRT cordon and filmed the roof through rain. Reporters shouted questions at people still wearing triage tags. Guests wrapped in silver emergency blankets described giant women at the doors, a metal animal in the atrium, and insects that had shown them where to run. No two accounts agreed on the order. Several disagreed on which floor they had occupied.

All of them agreed about the man with blades coming from his hands.

The news began with the footage that required the least explanation: MedHall's front wall bowed outward, water pouring through broken glass, emergency lights pulsing behind smoke. A section of mezzanine hung into the atrium. The chandelier lay across the dance floor in a glittering ruin large enough to be visible from the street.

Then came Kaiser.

Three separate cameras had caught him in the service corridor with blades along both arms. One angle showed Miss Militia entering behind him. Another showed Armsmaster closing from the clinic. The third, recorded by a firefighter's helmet camera through the rising loading shutter, caught the moment Kaiser looked from one blocked route to the other and understood that the performance was over.

The networks played that moment repeatedly.

They played it while Taylor was lifted into an ambulance. They played it on the waiting-room television while a nurse cut the rest of the green dress away from her ankle. They played it without sound in the imaging department, Kaiser sinking to his knees above a caption bar while a technician checked Taylor's spine for metal fragments.

Every replay made the distance between Max Anders and Kaiser look smaller.

MAX ANDERS ARRESTED AFTER MEDHALL SIEGE, the chyron read.

EMPIRE EIGHTY-EIGHT LEADERSHIP EXPOSED.

PRT CONFIRMS EXPLOSIVES, HOSTAGES, PARAHUMAN INVOLVEMENT.

MEDHALL RECORDS SEIZED IN FALSE-FLAG INVESTIGATION.

That fourth line appeared after sunrise.

Danny sat on the couch wearing borrowed hospital scrubs because his suit remained sealed in an evidence bag. Taylor wore an oversized Dockworkers sweatshirt over bandages at her shoulder and hip. Neither had slept.

Her right ankle was not broken. The doctor had repeated that twice as if the absence of a fracture converted it into good news. Severe sprain, lacerations to both feet, six stitches across her back, bruising from the stun charge, mild hearing loss expected to resolve, no retained glass. She had been given crutches and told not to put weight on the joint.

The crutches leaned against the couch untouched.

Danny had bruised ribs, a cut above one eyebrow he did not remember receiving, and a shoulder strained when he caught the concealed door against Fenja. He kept testing the shoulder when he thought Taylor was watching the television.

She kept noticing.

The television volume stayed low. Every few minutes one of them raised it, listened to a detail, and lowered it again.

"Authorities credit an unidentified person inside the building with providing the first accurate threat assessment," the anchor said. "The caller, referred to over emergency channels only as Tower, warned responding teams that Anders was directing the hostage takers while presenting himself publicly as a captive. The PRT has declined to identify Tower, citing an active protective investigation."

The segment used a silhouette that looked nothing like Taylor. Short hair, broad shoulders, adult proportions. Someone at the PRT had either guessed very badly or protected her on purpose.

Danny looked at Taylor.

Taylor looked at the television.

*Protective investigation,* McClane said. *That's government for “we know exactly who she is and we're trying not to say it where the Nazis can hear.”*

*Comforting.*

*More comforting than them putting your school picture on screen.*

Danny reached for the remote, paused the image, and studied the false silhouette.

"They know?"

"Miss Militia does. Armsmaster. Some of the troopers. Probably more."

"Your name?"

"Yes."

"Your school?"

Taylor had not asked.

McClane answered first. *Assume they know everything that can be put on a form. You'll be pleasantly surprised if they don't.*

"Probably."

Danny set the remote down with exaggerated care. "We're discussing that when they come back."

They had already come once.

Miss Militia had found them between radiology and discharge with her scarf removed and rain drying on her uniform. Without the cloth and weapons she looked younger, which made the exhaustion around her eyes harder to ignore. Armsmaster remained in the hall, visible through the open door and pretending the placement was about guarding it.

"No civilian fatalities," Miss Militia had said before anything else. "The donor from the clinic is stable. The server from the north collapse has a broken pelvis and internal injuries, but surgery went well. Trooper Ortega has a concussion and ligament damage. Roof One has frost exposure and a fractured wrist. Both are expected to recover."

Taylor had counted everyone outside. She still needed the words.

"The people under the mezzanine?"

Miss Militia's pause answered before she did. "Three of Anders's armed personnel died in the collapse. A fourth died during the initial takeover."

The fourth had been listed as MedHall security because he wore a confiscated blazer and carried one of the guards' rifles. Taylor corrected that before Miss Militia finished the sentence.

"He wasn't security. He came through the loading dock with them. He took the rifle from the real guard."

Armsmaster appeared in the doorway. "Location?"

Taylor gave it. Coat-check passage, eleven feet from the north stair, body moved behind the display wall after the takeover.

Armsmaster transmitted the correction immediately. The real guard, Raymond Vale, was located among the wounded in the laundry and removed from the suspect list before sunrise.

He remained in the doorway after the transmission ended.

"My initial assessment of Anders was wrong," he said.

Danny's head came up from the molded hospital chair.

Armsmaster continued before either of them answered. "I treated the camera feed as stronger evidence than the source inside the structure. I allowed the apparent hostage role to outweigh repeated observations that did not fit it. That delayed our response and contributed to the roof team entering an anticipated ambush."

It was not an apology shaped to invite forgiveness. It was an incident finding delivered by a man who intended to put his own name on it.

Taylor preferred that.

"Miss Militia listened," she said.

"Yes. She did."

Miss Militia glanced toward him, then back to Taylor. "The designation Tower stays inside the operational record. Your name will be restricted to the investigation team, medical liaison, and command review. The surviving Empire capes know they were opposed by someone using insects. They may have your physical description. They do not get confirmation from us."

"What about everyone I helped?"

"We cannot stop them describing you. We can ask them not to identify you. Several have already asked us how to protect your name."

Danny leaned forward despite his ribs. "And what do you want from her?"

Miss Militia understood the question beneath the question.

"Tonight? A recorded statement when her doctor clears it, with you present. After that, a secure way to contact her if the people who escaped create an immediate threat. No recruitment conversation today. No decision made while either of you is exhausted or frightened."

"Tomorrow?"

"Tomorrow she is still fifteen, Mr. Hebert. You remain her father. Any conversation includes both of you unless Taylor asks otherwise."

Danny's shoulders lowered by a fraction.

Armsmaster placed a sealed radio handset on the bedside table. "One button reaches Protectorate dispatch under the Tower identifier. It does not transmit until activated."

Taylor looked at it.

*Better than a business card,* McClane said. *Harder to lose in the wash.*

"Thank you," she said.

Armsmaster nodded once. "Trooper Ortega asked us to tell you the same. Less formally."

The casualty count appeared next.

No civilian deaths.

Thirty-two treated at the scene. Eleven hospitalized. Two PRT troopers injured, one seriously. Four members of Kaiser's armed contingent dead, including the sound-board operator caught beneath the stage collapse. An earlier report identifying one body as MedHall security was withdrawn. Nine attackers arrested. Hookwolf, Cricket, Fenja, and Menja escaped during the confusion around the north-support detonation. Alabaster was discovered in the service elevator after striking the doors for forty-three uninterrupted minutes.

The anchor managed to read that last detail without changing expression.

Danny did not.

He laughed once, exhausted and startled by the sound. Taylor joined him. The laughter hurt her shoulder and became something dangerously close to crying before either of them acknowledged the transition.

Danny turned off the television.

Silence settled through the house.

Outside, a truck changed gears on the avenue. Pipes clicked in the kitchen wall. After a night in which every sound had meant a door, a weapon, or a person moving where they should not, the house's ordinary noises arrived with edges.

Taylor extended her swarm through the rooms without meaning to. Three moths rested beneath the porch light. Ants moved through a crack beside the sink. A spider occupied the upper corner of the front window. No one waited in the yard. No radio shifted in the bushes. No steel grew behind the walls.

McClane said nothing about the check.

"You trapped a supervillain in an elevator," he said.

"Temporarily."

"Forty-three minutes sounds pretty committed."

"He kept resetting."

"Of course he did."

Danny looked toward the muted television. "You know that's going to be the part everyone remembers."

"Not the chandelier?"

"Elevator's funnier."

"The chandelier was three tons."

"You counted?"

Taylor gave him a look.

Danny's tired smile appeared and vanished. "Right. Stupid question."

They had not yet had the conversation. They had started it six times at the hospital and been interrupted by doctors, PRT interviews, insurance forms, and one furious Dockworkers representative who arrived prepared to organize a search party before learning Danny was already safe.

The pause now felt different. Nothing remained to interrupt them except choice.

Danny turned the television back on instead.

The next report was not about the capes.

An aerial photograph showed MedHall's western service wing with a red box drawn around the locked room Taylor had found through a fly. A second image showed the loading dock after investigators opened the false wall beside it. Banker boxes, portable drive duplicators, industrial shredders, and two rolling fireproof cabinets had been arranged behind the wall on a track leading directly to the dock.

"Investigators now believe the apparent hostage escape demand concealed an effort to remove and destroy MedHall corporate records," the anchor said. "Sources close to the inquiry confirm that a state forensic audit of MedHall's community-outreach subsidiaries was scheduled to begin this morning. The records were under a preservation order and could not legally be moved or destroyed."

The screen changed to a diagram.

The locked equipment room sat beside the archive. Kaiser's hidden corridor connected both to the sound booth and loading dock. The west and north charges bracketed the archive wing. The armored vehicle demanded during negotiations would have arrived at the only door from which the fireproof cabinets could be rolled out without crossing a public camera.

The terrorists were supposed to leave with the evidence.

Max Anders was supposed to be rescued from the wreckage as their most famous hostage.

If the vehicles were stopped, the charges would destroy the archive and the people positioned beside it. Either outcome erased the audit trail. One preserved Kaiser as a civic survivor. The other gave him a public tragedy from which to demand broader security powers and emergency control over the charities whose records had vanished.

"Authorities have recovered partial ledgers connecting MedHall grants to a network of real-estate and security contractors," the anchor continued. "Several are now alleged Empire Eighty-Eight fronts. Investigators have not announced a total, but one source described the diverted funds as being in the eight figures."

Danny stared at the diagram.

"The loading dock," he said.

Taylor remembered Anders stepping into the cold corridor for a private exchange. Something passed hand to hand while security explained it away as a discreet pledge.

"Access credential," she said. "Or the preservation seal. Whatever opened the archive."

*And you saw the handoff before any of it started,* McClane said. *Didn't know what it meant. Knew it didn't fit.*

The reporter outside MedHall held up a scorched strip of paper inside an evidence sleeve. "Investigators say the operation began earlier than planned after Anders's security team detected unauthorized surveillance inside the gala. As a result, most of the archive had not yet been transferred when the takeover began."

Taylor had not merely interrupted the siege.

She had forced Kaiser to choose between abandoning the reason for it and beginning before he could make the evidence disappear.

He had chosen control.

It had cost him both.

Marcus Thorne appeared outside Brockton Bay General with a bandage across his forehead and a fresh notebook in his hand.

"Mr. Thorne," a reporter called, "you were one of the five hostages placed beside an explosive device. Did Max Anders speak to you before the takeover?"

Thorne looked directly into the cameras.

"I gave his staff information about a young guest I believed had witnessed a newsworthy exchange. I did that in return for access to Anders. I did not understand the danger, but failure to understand does not remove responsibility. That guest and her father later saved my life. I will not identify either of them."

He closed the notebook.

"The Bulletin will publish a full account of MedHall's financial and political relationships. My byline will not appear on it."

The clip ended.

Danny leaned back. "Didn't expect that."

*Neither did I,* McClane admitted.

Taylor watched the next segment assemble the night from public pieces. Guests described insects forming arrows through dark hallways. A firefighter described the east doors opening against a giant's weight. The defense attorney told cameras that a teenage girl in a green dress had understood the building better than every adult inside it and then refused to give her name.

The councilwoman spoke from a hospital entrance with her bruised cheek uncovered. She described returning to the hostage floor on purpose and stopped a reporter who tried to call it courage.

"It was a job somebody had to do," she said. "The young woman inside had already done six impossible jobs. I managed one ordinary one."

The catering supervisor gave an interview beside three employees who had refused transport. He told the cameras about the second kitchen route. The dishwasher stood at the edge of the group and said nothing until a reporter asked whether he had seen Tower.

"Saw a kid trying to survive," he answered. "That's all you need."

The anesthesiologist did not speak to reporters. A hospital representative confirmed that she had refused to leave her patient until firefighters reached the clinic and had continued treatment through the north-support detonation.

Luis Ortega appeared only as a still photograph taken before the operation. The PRT statement credited him with maintaining interior communications after injury. It did not mention that a barefoot teenager had pulled him out of Hookwolf's stairwell.

That omission felt less like theft than shelter.

The footage showed the chandelier falling from four angles.

On the third replay, the network added dramatic music.

*"Oh, now they find a soundtrack department,"* Gladly said.

Fury told him to shut up, but without much force.

Below the spectacle, the city began reacting to what the archive contained.

The MedHall board suspended six executives before breakfast. State investigators sealed three subsidiary offices. Police and PRT teams served warrants on two private-security firms and a waterfront construction company before employees could reach work. A federal agency announced it was joining the financial investigation and declined to explain why its representatives had already been in Brockton Bay before the siege began.

The Dockworkers Association issued a statement demanding an independent review of every MedHall-linked contract on the waterfront. Danny read his own organization's name in the scrolling text and blinked.

"They work fast when I'm unconscious."

"You weren't unconscious."

"Felt like it."

The Empire did not disappear with Kaiser in custody. News footage showed spray-painted threats outside MedHall clinics and police tape around a storefront damaged before dawn. Hookwolf, Cricket, Fenja, and Menja remained free. The respectable structure Anders had built was cracking in public, but the people who had enforced it still had weapons.

Victory did not make the next problem smaller.

It made the next problem visible.

What got out was spectacular and incomplete.

The public knew Kaiser had planned the building control in advance. It knew E88 capes had guarded the hostages. It knew an inside caller had corrected the official response. It knew Max Anders had ordered a charge detonated while pretending to be one of the people it threatened.

It knew the siege had been camouflage for the MedHall archive removal and that Taylor's premature discovery had forced the operation to begin before the records could be taken.

It did not know Taylor controlled the insects.

It did not know John McClane existed behind her eyes.

It did not know Danny had told Kaiser to go through him without ever using those words.

Those parts remained in the living room.

"The PRT will come back," Danny said.

"I know."

"And the Empire people who got away."

"I know."

"You were doing this before last night. The bugs."

Taylor pulled the sweatshirt sleeves over her hands.

"Yeah."

Danny nodded as if confirming something he had already worked out in the hospital corridor.

"And John?"

McClane went still.

Taylor could have lied. The previous night had taught her a hundred better ways to do it.

"Still here."

Danny looked toward the empty chair beside her, guessed wrong, then adjusted his gaze back to Taylor.

"Tell him thank you."

*Tell him he did pretty good himself.*

"He says you did pretty good yourself."

Danny's mouth moved around a smile he was not ready to let become one.

The television returned to MedHall. Dawn illuminated the broken glass and wet stone. Investigators in white coveralls moved through the atrium with evidence markers. The building no longer looked like a stage set waiting to be destroyed.

It looked like a place that had survived being used for what it was built to hide.

Taylor watched workers carry the Max Anders nameplate out through the loading dock.

That, more than the arrest footage, made the ending feel real.



Chapter 26: Danny



Three nights after MedHall, Danny put two mugs of coffee on the back porch and asked Taylor to begin with the bugs.

He did not ask whether there were bugs. He had followed a moving trail through a collapsing building, watched an elevator close on a man who healed every few seconds, and seen his daughter look toward empty corners whenever the voice called John said something useful.

Denial had been available before the chandelier fell. It had not survived the loading dock.

Taylor sat beside him on the top step. The porch light hummed overhead. Moths circled it, ordinary except for the fact that she knew each position without looking.

"How long?" Danny asked.

"Since January."

His hands tightened around the mug. "January."

The month connected to too many things. The hospital. The school saying Taylor had been found ill. The clothes the hospital disposed of before Danny arrived. Blackwell's careful voice explaining that the responsible students had not been identified.

Danny placed his mug on the porch beside him.

"The locker."

Taylor looked down at her bandaged feet.

"That's when it happened."

"You said you didn't remember."

"I remembered. I couldn't tell the doctors without telling them about the bugs."

"What was in it?"

This was the part she had spent months refusing to describe even to herself in complete sentences. The porch gave her no alarms to pull, no injured stranger to prioritize, no corridor to map instead.

McClane stayed silent.

Taylor told Danny.

She did not give him every smell. She gave him enough. The soaked trash. The sealed vents. The laughter outside. The length of time before someone opened the door. The moment every insect in the school arrived inside her awareness at once while she still could not make her own hands reach the latch.

Danny left the step.

For one frightened second Taylor thought he was walking away. He reached the porch rail, gripped it with both hands, and bent his head.

"Who?"

Taylor said nothing.

"Taylor. Who did that?"

"If I give you names right now, what will you do?"

His answer came too slowly.

"I don't know."

"That's why I'm not giving them tonight."

Danny turned. Anger had made his face unfamiliar, but it was not pointed at her.

"You don't get to protect them."

"I'm protecting you."

"From teenage girls?"

"From doing something that lets their parents and the school turn this into you being dangerous instead of them."

He opened his mouth and stopped.

Taylor had spent two years learning exactly how blame moved at Winslow. Danny had spent three nights learning that his daughter understood hostile systems better than he did.

He came back to the step.

"Tomorrow," he said. "We discuss the names tomorrow. With a plan."

"Okay."

It was the first rule they made.

"I didn't know how to tell you."

"Were you going to?"

Taylor looked into the yard. "I kept deciding I would after I understood it better. Then after I had rules. Then after I knew I could keep you out of it."

"You decided that from a hospital bed?"

"I decided not to make you look at me like everyone else would."

"How would everyone else look?"

Taylor moved the moths from the rail. They covered her forearm from wrist to elbow, wings folded, dozens of small bodies making a living sleeve.

Danny did not recoil.

That hurt in a different direction.

"And did you? Understand it?"

She lifted one hand.

Every moth around the porch light landed along the railing in a straight line.

Danny stared.

Taylor spread them into a circle. Two beetles emerged from the grass and took positions at either end. A spider descended from the porch roof until it hung between them, turning slowly on its thread.

"I control them," she said. "All of them inside a few blocks. More if I'm moving. I know where they are. I can use their senses if I concentrate. I can make them work together."

"Can you hear through them?"

"Not like ears. I get vibration, air movement, contact. Enough insects together can build more."

"See?"

"Light and dark. Shapes sometimes. Flies are better than ants."

"Every bug in a few blocks. All the time?"

"They're there whether I pay attention or not. Like knowing where your hands are. If I focus, I can separate them."

Danny looked toward the neighboring houses. "Can you feel inside those?"

"Yes."

"People's bedrooms? Bathrooms?"

"If bugs are there. I don't look."

"But you could."

"Yes."

The answer frightened him more than the straight line of moths had. Power was impressive when it moved. Its quiet reach was harder to fit inside a father's idea of his daughter.

"Does it turn off when you sleep?"

"No. I don't always remember what happens. Sudden movement wakes me sometimes."

Danny glanced toward the back door. "That's how you always knew when I checked on you."

Taylor nodded.

He absorbed one more ordinary memory becoming evidence of something he had missed.

"Can they hurt people?"

"Yes."

The honesty hurt him. She saw it land.

"Have you?"

"Yes. Not at MedHall. Not badly there. Before."

Danny set down the coffee before his hands could spill it.

*He deserves the rest,* McClane said.

*I know.*

Taylor told him about the costume she had made in the basement while pretending the long hours down there were homework and sewing practice. She told him the armor came from spider silk, that the lenses were scavenged and cut by hand, and that every seam had been designed around the possibility of someone trying to stab or shoot her.

Danny stopped her there.

"Shoot you."

"It hasn't happened."

"That is not the reassuring sentence you think it is."

"I didn't think it was reassuring."

She told him about her first night out. The gang members surrounding a man in the yard. Lung changing while he fought. Taylor attacking because she believed children were going to be killed. The Undersiders arriving. Armsmaster meeting her afterward and telling her exactly how badly she had misread parts of the situation.

Danny listened without touching his coffee.

"Lung," he said when she finished that portion.

"Yes."

"Your first night."

"Yes."

"The man who turns into a dragon."

"Eventually."

Danny stood again. This time he walked the length of the porch, reached the steps, came back, and remained standing because sitting required more stillness than he possessed.

"And Armsmaster knew."

"He knew Skitter. He didn't know who I was."

"Skitter."

Taylor had expected embarrassment when she gave her father the name. What arrived instead was the strange distance of hearing him place two versions of her beside each other.

"That's me."

"I have read about you in the paper."

"Not much."

"I remember every cape story involving a teenager, Taylor. I remember thinking somebody's parents were going to see it and lose their minds."

He pressed his palm against his forehead.

"Apparently I was optimistic about the parents seeing it."

Taylor told him about the Undersiders. She told him what she had intended to do, stay near them, learn who funded them, pass the information to the heroes once she had enough. She did not present the plan as noble. Said aloud on a porch, with Danny listening and McClane refusing to help her polish it, it sounded exactly like a lonely fifteen-year-old inventing an undercover operation because asking an adult for help had stopped feeling possible.

"Do they think you're their friend?" Danny asked.

Taylor looked at the moths.

"Some of them might."

"Are they yours?"

"I don't know."

That answer hurt all three people in the conversation.

She finished with every patrol, every meeting, every lie she could remember telling to get out of the house. She did not turn the account into confession or defense. She gave him facts in order, the way McClane had taught her to build a room: entrances first, then obstacles, then every exit she had tried.

Danny listened until she finished.

"I was in the house," he said. "All those nights, I was in the house."

"I know."

"You walked past my bedroom."

"Usually."

"I heard you in the basement."

"Sometimes I was there."

"And the other times?"

"I left the recorder running."

Danny stared at her.

"Your mother's old cassette recorder."

Taylor wished he had shouted. The quiet made each small piece of preparation sound worse.

"Yes."

"You planned what I would hear."

"I planned not to get caught."

"By me."

"By anyone."

"I am not anyone."

Taylor flinched.

Danny saw it and regretted the volume immediately, which did not make the sentence less true.

"No," she said. "You're not. I didn't know how to make you the exception without making you part of it."

"Usually."

There were versions of her father who shouted here. Taylor had rehearsed them. There were versions who demanded the costume, grounded her forever, called the PRT, or broke beneath the accumulated proof that he had not known his daughter for months.

Danny only looked tired.

"I knew school was bad," he said. "I knew you were unhappy. Every time I asked, you said it was handled, and I let wanting that answer count as believing it."

"You tried."

"I made phone calls. I accepted meetings where Blackwell told me there wasn't enough evidence. I let the absence of paperwork become the absence of a problem."

He rubbed both hands over his face.

"Then you ended up in the hospital and I let them tell me nobody knew why."

"You didn't let them. They lied. I lied too."

"You're the child."

"I'm not five."

"No. You're fifteen, which is old enough to make choices and young enough that I was supposed to notice when every available choice had become terrible."

Taylor had no practiced answer for a parent taking responsibility without using it to take hers away.

Danny looked toward the dark kitchen window. Their reflections sat beside each other in the glass, both altered by bandages.

"Your mother would have noticed."

The old sentence entered the space between them. Danny used it against himself often enough that Taylor recognized the shape before he finished speaking.

"Maybe," she said. "She also would have tried to fix everything in one afternoon and terrified the school secretary."

A breath escaped him that might have been a laugh.

"She was very good at terrifying administrators."

"I remember."

They let Annette sit with them for a moment without turning her into either accusation or solution.

"John," he said. "How long has he been there?"

"Since the gala."

"Not January."

"No."

"Did you choose him?"

"No. A man named O'Malley chose him. John agreed before he arrived. I didn't get asked."

Danny's expression hardened again. "Someone put an adult stranger in my daughter's head without asking her."

*Yeah,* McClane said. *Tell him that's on the list of things we're gonna discuss with O'Malley if I ever get him in a room with a door.*

Taylor relayed it.

"Good," Danny said. "Put my name on the list."

Some tension left Danny's shoulders. Not much. Enough to show that he had feared the stranger inside his daughter's head had been there through every earlier secret too.

"Can he control you?"

"No. Never unless I give permission, and I can take it back. I haven't given it."

"Can he hear me now?"

*Every word,* McClane said.

"Yes."

"Can he see through you?"

Taylor considered how to explain a boundary she experienced more than understood.

"He gets what I'm paying attention to. He doesn't sit behind my eyes like a camera. He can tell when I'm hurt. He hears what I hear. Thoughts if I direct them at him, sometimes thoughts I don't mean to direct when they're loud enough."

"Can you make him stop listening?"

*Yes,* McClane answered immediately. *Tell him you can close the door. Not perfect when we're under pressure, but good enough for privacy. And tell him I knock.*

Taylor repeated it.

Danny looked toward the yard. "Do you? Knock?"

*Every time I know there's a door there.*

"He says every time he knows there's a door."

"That's a very police-officer answer."

*He's not wrong.*

"John agrees."

Danny leaned forward, elbows on his knees. "Then I would like to speak to him."

Taylor had imagined this too. None of the imagined versions accounted for having to serve as both sides of the conversation.

"He can hear you, but you can't hear him. I have to repeat it."

"Fine." Danny looked toward the yard rather than at Taylor, perhaps because speaking to open air felt less strange than speaking to his daughter's face. "John McClane."

*That's me.*

"He says that's him."

"Thank you for bringing her out."

McClane took longer to answer.

*Tell him she brought me out too.*

Taylor swallowed. "He says I brought him out too."

Danny nodded once. "I don't know what that means."

*Neither do I, completely. Tell him I was in trouble somewhere else. Tell him helping you stopped me from becoming the only thing in the room I could see.*

Taylor translated.

Danny listened with the same grave concentration he gave a contract negotiation.

"Are you a police officer?"

*NYPD detective. Depending on what year anybody's asking from.*

"NYPD detective. The year is complicated."

"Are you an adult?"

*Last I checked.*

"Yes."

"Where were you before the gala?"

McClane gave Taylor the answer in short pieces. Dulles. A cargo system beneath an airport. Men trying to rescue a prisoner responsible for a country's worth of murder. A knife in his side. O'Malley's offer arriving before the fight reached its final movement.

Taylor translated without describing the waiting room yet. That part belonged to McClane and O'Malley unless McClane chose otherwise.

Danny's gaze moved to Taylor's bandages. "So you came here from your own emergency."

*Yeah.*

"And when you leave?"

*I go back to the instant I left. That's what he promised.*

"He goes back to the same instant."

"Still injured."

*Still fighting.*

Taylor repeated it.

Danny took that in. "Then every day you spend here costs your family nothing."

*Clock time? Nothing. Me? We'll see.*

Taylor hesitated, then translated exactly.

"Fair answer," Danny said.

*Your dad likes answers nobody else likes.*

"John says you like answers nobody else likes."

"Occupational hazard."

Danny finally looked directly at Taylor again. "Has he asked you for control?"

"Once. At MedHall. To show me how to fall through the laundry chute without breaking my neck. I said no. He talked me through it."

*And she did it better than I would've,* McClane added.

Taylor included that too.

"If she says no?" Danny asked the yard.

*Then it's no. I'm a passenger, not the wheel. I have kids. I know the difference between helping and deciding for somebody, even if I've screwed it up often enough to learn it the expensive way.*

Danny waited for Taylor's translation, then nodded.

"You're not her father."

*No. She has one. I'm not auditioning.*

Taylor's throat tightened unexpectedly when she relayed it.

Danny looked at her, not offended and not relieved. Measuring whether the boundary had been spoken because it was real or because he wanted to hear it.

"Then what are you?"

McClane was quiet long enough for a moth to strike the porch light twice.

*Backup,* he said at last. *The kind that can't carry a gun, call a squad car, or fill out paperwork. Mostly I tell her where the exits are and complain when she ignores the good ones.*

"Backup," Taylor said. "Very opinionated backup."

*Kid.*

Danny's mouth almost moved. "I can work with backup."

Danny turned to Taylor. "Then why was my fifteen-year-old daughter the one pulling an armed trooper down three flights of stairs?"

*Because everybody with a badge was outside and everybody with a gun was between her and the door.* McClane's answer came flat, with no attempt to make himself sound better. *Because I couldn't move her body and wouldn't have if I could. Because she decided a man was going to die if she waited. And because she was right.*

Taylor repeated it without softening.

Danny closed his eyes.

"I don't like that answer."

*Neither do I.*

"Neither does he."

"But he let you do it."

"He told me the cost. I chose."

"You're fifteen."

"The trooper was still hanging."

Danny stood and walked to the porch railing. The moths moved away from his hands without Taylor consciously directing them.

"That sentence is going to keep happening, isn't it?" he asked. "You're fifteen, and somebody is still hanging. You're fifteen, and the building is still locked. You're fifteen, and if you don't do it, nobody else will get there in time."

Taylor did not answer.

"That's what scares me," Danny said. "Not the bugs. Not even John, though I'm reserving judgment on the voice in my daughter's head. It's that every time you tell me why, the reason is going to be good enough."

*He's got you there,* McClane said.

"John says you've got me there."

"Glad somebody appreciates it."
