Danny returned to the step. "I can't make this unhappen. I can't keep you home by pretending the rest of the city stops existing outside our door. But no more disappearing without somebody knowing. No more deciding a plan is safe because you're the only person you told it to. If you're going out, we build rules together."

Taylor's first instinct was refusal. Rules became control. Control became permission, and permission could be withdrawn.

Then she remembered a safety line holding a PRT trooper above a stairwell.

*Rules aren't the same as chains,* McClane said. *Good ones tell the people coming after you where the line broke.*

"Okay," Taylor said.

Danny looked surprised.

"Not every rule," she added. "We negotiate."

His mouth twitched. "You are definitely my daughter."

He went inside for paper. Taylor expected a legal pad. He returned with the unopened MedHall medical-bill envelope, a pen, the sealed Tower handset, and her neglected crutches.

"Use these or I call the doctor before we negotiate anything."

"That's coercion."

"That's parenting."

*He's got you there again,* McClane said.

Taylor put the crutches within reach.

Danny wrote **CONTACT** across the envelope.

"If you go out in costume, I know before you leave."

"You don't get the location if knowing it makes you a target."

"Then I get a sealed location. Note, scheduled message, something I can open if you miss contact."

"Two copies. One hidden outside the house in case someone comes here first."

Danny paused, then wrote it down.

They set a check-in interval. Danny began at fifteen minutes. Taylor countered with two hours. They settled on forty-five minutes during an active patrol, ninety if she was stationary and had warned him first.

"If you miss one?" he asked.

"You call the Tower line."

"How long before I call?"

"Fifteen minutes."

"Five."

"Ten."

He wrote ten.

"If you answer under pressure?"

Taylor thought of the false call from Blackwell's office and the way a correct name had nearly made the lie work.

"Challenge phrase," she said. "You ask whether I fed the dog."

"We don't have a dog."

"Exactly. Safe answer is that he ate twice. Any other answer means someone can hear me or I'm being forced."

Danny wrote **DOG ATE TWICE** beneath the contact times.

"And if someone makes you say it?"

McClane supplied the answer. *Words plus action. Have her tap the transmitter twice before speaking. Anyone can steal a phrase. Harder to steal procedure they don't know exists.*

Taylor relayed it. Danny added two circles beside the phrase.

The second heading was **BACKUP**.

Taylor would carry the Tower handset until the PRT provided something smaller. Danny would keep dispatch numbers beside every telephone. If she identified an imminent threat, she called before intervening whenever a call was physically possible. If she could not call, she left a bug marker on the house's rear window in one of three shapes: circle for delayed, line for danger, X for call everyone.

"Everyone means PRT and police," Danny said.

"And the Dockworkers."

"The Dockworkers are not a cape-response team."

"They're a hundred people who know the docks better than the PRT. Information only. Nobody confronts capes."

Danny wrote **INFORMATION ONLY** twice and underlined it.

The third heading was **NO SOLO**.

That argument lasted twenty minutes.

Danny meant Taylor never entered danger without an adult physically beside her. Taylor pointed out that most available adults could not follow her, would not believe her, or had spent MedHall outside obeying the wrong camera. Danny said this did not make a fifteen-year-old the default substitute. Taylor said a rule that could never survive contact with reality was only a promise to lie next time.

The words sat between them.

Danny crossed out **NO SOLO**.

He wrote **NO ONE ELSE KNOWS IS NOT A PLAN**.

Taylor read it twice.

"I can agree to that."

"At least one person knows what you're doing. Me, John, PRT dispatch, someone on your team. Preferably two."

"John always knows."

"John cannot call an ambulance."

*Also fair,* McClane said.

"One person outside my head," Taylor agreed.

The back of the MedHall envelope filled faster than either of them expected. Danny turned it sideways to fit the questions that could not be settled tonight.

Curfew became midnight on weekends, ten on school nights, automatically extended during declared cape emergencies only if Taylor made contact. Danny wanted the costume locked in his room. Taylor refused before he finished asking. They tabled storage after agreeing the mask could not be the only copy and the costume needed an emergency identification card sealed inside it.

Teams stayed unresolved. Danny did not want her returning to the Undersiders. Taylor would not promise to abandon people she had already placed in danger by joining them. McClane refused to break the tie.

*That's not a porch decision,* he said. *That's a whole separate bad idea with names and moving parts. Sleep first.*

They wrote **TEAM TOMORROW**.

The PRT received the same treatment. Danny wanted a formal meeting. Taylor wanted Miss Militia present and Armsmaster not solely in charge. Both wanted every promise written. The heading became **PRT, WITH WITNESS**.

When the page filled, he set down the pen.

"One more question for John."

*Go ahead.*

"Does he have family?"

McClane's answer arrived without hesitation.

*Wife. Two kids. Tell him I'm getting back to them. I just don't know when.*

Taylor repeated it.

Danny looked at her for a long moment, then toward the empty yard.

"Then we'll keep her alive until you do."

*Deal,* McClane said.

"He says deal."

Danny picked up his coffee. It had gone cold.

Taylor leaned against his shoulder, the way she had not done in years. Above them, the moths returned to the porch light one at a time.

Nothing about the night was ordinary anymore.

The quiet still was.



Chapter 27: Something We Keep



A week after the porch, on a slow Tuesday that felt almost aggressively ordinary, Taylor caught herself saying "yippie kay yay" under her breath while she reorganized her locker between classes, and didn't even notice she'd done it until the girl at the next locker over gave her a strange look.

Ordinary required more work than it used to.

Her right ankle remained inside a rigid brace. The crutches had become one crutch after the follow-up appointment, then a folding cane Danny bought at a pharmacy and insisted she carry even when she did not use it. Bandages pulled beneath her shirt when she reached for the upper locker shelf. The hearing in her left ear still faded at the end of crowded days, turning the hallway roar into something distant and underwater.

MedHall played on phones between classes.

Students replayed the chandelier drop beside the drinking fountain. Someone had edited the oxygen cylinder into a bowling clip with Kaiser as the last pin. A sophomore Taylor did not know claimed his uncle had been in the atrium and that Tower was a forty-year-old PRT intelligence officer who controlled rats. Three competing rumors identified Armsmaster, Miss Militia, or an unnamed Case Fifty-Three as the insect cape.

No one looking at Taylor saw farther than Danny Hebert's injured daughter, one of two hundred people unlucky enough to attend.

That protection did not feel permanent.

The defense attorney's description had reached Winslow before Taylor returned. Tall teenage girl. Dark hair. Green dress. At least six students knew she had worn green to the gala. Blackwell had called the house to discuss "media-management concerns" and found Danny, a Dockworkers attorney, and a PRT liaison already on the line.

The second call had been shorter.

At school, Taylor's locker had been moved to a corridor covered by a working camera. Emma, Sophia, and Madison had received a temporary no-contact order while the investigation into January reopened. None had been suspended. Blackwell called that neutrality. Danny called it preserving the school's options.

Taylor called it Tuesday.

*You're doing it again,* McClane said, amused.

*Doing what.*

*Talking like me. You've been doing it for days. Little things. The flat delivery when you're annoyed. That thing where you call something "a hell of a time" for the wrong thing to happen.* Something almost fond in it, underneath the observation. *Didn't figure you'd notice on your own before I said something.*

*Yesterday I told Mr. Quinlan his homework policy had a hostage-negotiation problem.*

*It did.*

*He asked whether I was feeling all right.*

*Reasonable response.*

Taylor shifted her history book to the lower shelf. *Danny says I've started saying “pal” when I'm angry.*

*Could be worse. Could've picked up my taste in undershirts.*

*I don't know what that means.*

*Then O'Malley has spared you one thing, at least.*

Taylor closed her locker, slower than she needed to, turning the thought over. *Is that bad?*

*No.* No hesitation in it at all. *It's just what happens, I think, when two people spend enough time sharing the same space. You pick things up. I've probably picked up a few of yours too, if I'm honest, and I haven't even got a body to notice it in yet.*

*Like what?*

McClane considered.

*I count smaller now.*

*What does that mean?*

*Used to walk into a room and get people, doors, weapons. Big shapes. Now I catch the moth in the light fixture. Ant trail under the vending machine. Which guy keeps scratching one sleeve because there's something moving under it.*

Taylor glanced down the hallway. A boy near the stairwell rubbed his left cuff for the third time. A tiny beetle had become trapped between fabric and skin.

She guided it out.

The boy stopped scratching without ever knowing why.

*Useful?* she asked.

*Annoyingly.*

*You do this thing. Cataloging exits before you catalog how you feel about a room.* A beat, something almost self-deprecating in it. *I used to think that was just me. Cop thing. Turns out it's just a scared-kid thing that never really goes away, whether you're a cop or a teenager with a swarm of bugs and a father helping write her check-in rules.*

Taylor didn't have an answer for that right away, and let the hallway noise fill the silence instead, lockers slamming, the low roar of a school day nobody else in the building knew was anything but ordinary. She thought about the last month, cataloging exits at pep rallies, cataloging exits in the cafeteria, cataloging exits at her own kitchen table, and wondered, not for the first time, how much of that had actually started with him and how much of it had just been waiting inside her all along for someone to finally recognize it out loud.

A month ago, before any of this, she'd have called that same habit anxiety and left it there, one more small, embarrassing thing about herself she didn't examine too closely. It was strange, having a name for it now that didn't sound like a diagnosis. Strange, and a little bit like being handed a tool she hadn't known she'd been missing her whole life.

Tools could still be used badly.

Emma waited at the end of the corridor with Sophia and Madison, technically more than the required twenty feet from Taylor's locker. Madison watched the camera. Sophia watched Taylor's injured leg. Emma watched Taylor's face.

The arrangement would have frightened her two weeks ago. It still did. The difference was that fear arrived with information now.

Camera behind Taylor, working. Faculty-room door open thirty feet left. Stairwell right, empty except for one student descending. Sophia's weight forward on the balls of her feet. Madison holding a phone but not recording yet. Emma expecting Taylor either to retreat or cross the distance first.

*You don't have to win the hallway,* McClane said.

*They're violating the order.*

*Then make that the problem. Don't let them trade it for whatever they want you to say next.*

Taylor took the Tower handset from her bag. It looked like an ugly pager with one recessed button.

Madison lowered her phone.

Sophia shifted her weight back.

Emma smiled. "Really? You're calling the heroes because we stood in a hallway?"

Taylor did not press the emergency button. She held the handset beside the visible camera and read the time from the clock above Emma's shoulder.

"Documenting contact at ten forty-three. North second-floor corridor. All three subjects inside the restricted distance."

There was no recording light. Emma did not know that.

"We didn't speak to you," Madison said.

"You just did."

The faculty-room door opened. Mr. Quinlan stepped into the hall carrying a coffee mug and stopped when he saw the four of them.

Sophia moved first. She turned and walked toward the stairs. Madison followed. Emma remained for one second longer, smile fixed in place while the situation refused to become the one she had arranged.

Then she left too.

Taylor's hands began shaking after they turned the corner.

*You didn't threaten them,* McClane said. *Didn't bluff past what you had. Put time, place, and witnesses on the record. That's how you make the next conversation start with what they did instead of how you reacted.*

*I was terrified.*

*Yeah. Competence doesn't cure that. Mostly it gives your hands something useful to do while you're terrified.*

Mr. Quinlan approached carefully. "Do you need the office?"

Taylor considered the routes. Considered the old instinct to say she was fine and disappear before help could become another problem.

"I need you to write down what you saw," she said.

He looked toward the stairwell, then nodded. "All right."

That answer felt like a victory too small for television and large enough to keep.

*Which one is it, do you think,* she asked. *Something you gave me, or something you just noticed I already had?*

*Honestly? Bit of both, probably.* No certainty pretended where none existed. *I didn't put anything in you that wasn't already there. I just know what to call it, and knowing what to call a thing changes how loud it gets to talk.*

*Can I ask you something,* she said, finally, once she'd found a quiet stretch of hallway to walk without anyone close enough to overhear a girl apparently talking to nobody.

*Always.*

*Holly. Your wife.* She felt him go still at the name, not guarded exactly, just careful, the way a man got when a wound hadn't finished healing all the way. *You don't talk about her much.*

*No,* he agreed. *I don't.*

*Why not?*

He didn't answer right away, and when he did, the dry humor had gone out of it entirely, replaced by something quieter, harder-won. *Because I spent twelve years being real good at my job and real bad at coming home from it. Not bad like I didn't love her. Bad like I never figured out how to leave the job at the door when I walked through it. Brought it inside with me every time. The watching. The cataloging. Never really turned it off, not for her, not for anybody.*

*That sounds lonely.*

*It was.* No self-pity in it, just plain fact, offered flat. *For both of us, probably, though she never once said so out loud, because she wasn't the kind of woman who made a man feel smaller than he already felt about it.*

*What's she like. If you don't mind me asking.*

A pause, and when he answered, something in the flatness had softened into something closer to reverence. *Sharp. Sharper than me, most days, though she'd never say it that plainly herself. Funny in a way that snuck up on you. Stubborn enough to leave New York for a job in California that actually paid her what she was worth, even knowing what it'd cost us. I used to give her grief about that. Took me longer than I'm proud of to figure out she was right to go.*

*I'm telling you this because I don't want you making my mistake, kid.* McClane's voice had gone careful now, deliberate, a man handing over something he clearly didn't hand over easily. *You've got a gift most people never get. You can read a room, work a plan, keep your dad safe from things he doesn't even know exist. That's real, and it matters, and I'm not gonna tell you to stop being good at it. But there's a difference between carrying the job and letting the job carry you clean out of your own life. I didn't learn that difference until it cost me almost everything. I'd like it if you learned it a little cheaper than I did.*

*What would you have done differently?*

The question stopped him longer than asking about Holly had.

*Come home before I had an answer,* he said. *That's the first thing. I kept thinking I had to solve the case, fix the problem, become somebody who deserved to walk through the door. Meanwhile Holly was on the other side of it living with whatever version of me bothered showing up.*

*Danny wants details that could put him in danger.*

*Then tell him that. Give him the choice with the danger attached. Don't make the choice for him and call it protection.*

Taylor thought of every patrol route she had hidden, every lie about homework, every time she had decided Danny was safer because he did not know enough to object.

*What if he says no?*

*Then you argue. Maybe you lose. Maybe he does. Family isn't a command structure, no matter how convenient it'd be if everybody saluted and followed the plan.*

*You were a police officer telling your wife what was dangerous.*

*Yeah. I was also a husband who mistook being right about the danger for being right about everything that followed. Don't copy that part.*

Taylor walked the rest of the hallway in silence, letting that settle, feeling the shape of it land somewhere she hadn't expected advice to reach.

*You still love her,* she said, eventually. Not a question.

*Every day.* Simple, unadorned, the truest thing he'd said all week. *Some jobs you clock out of. That one, you don't. Wouldn't want to, even if I could.*

*Do you think about going back? To her. To your life.*

A long pause, longer than she was used to from him, and when he answered, there was something almost gentle underneath the honesty. *Every day. O'Malley said I go back to the instant I left. He neglected to say when that happens from our side, which is the sort of detail he probably finds funny.* What she felt from him was not doubt that he would return, but the strain of having no date to count toward. *I know I'm here now, with you, and I'd rather spend the time actually being here than turn every day into a waiting room for somewhere else.* Something that might have been a smile crept into the words. *Besides. Somebody's gotta keep teaching you to read a room properly. Can't exactly do that from three thousand miles and one impossible universe away.*

Taylor smiled, actually smiled, the first easy one she'd let herself have in days, and pushed through the doors into the parking lot, into ordinary autumn light, into a life that looked, from the outside, exactly like it had a month ago.

Her bus did not arrive for twelve minutes.

Taylor sat on the low brick wall beside the student lot and took out the Tower handset. The ordinary call function reached home through a number the PRT had routed without putting either Hebert name on the account.

Danny answered on the third ring.

Taylor tapped the transmit button twice before speaking.

"Did you feed the dog?" he asked.

"He ate twice."

The ritual sounded ridiculous in daylight. That was part of why it worked. No one listening casually would hear anything but a father checking on an animal they did not own.

"How was school?"

Taylor looked back at the second-floor windows. "Contact violation at ten forty-three. Quinlan witnessed it. I documented the location. No physical contact."

Danny went silent for one breath. "Are you safe now?"

"Yes."

"Good. We handle the report when you get home."

Not *I'll handle it*. Not *forget it*. We.

Taylor tightened her grip on the handset.

"I'm meeting the Undersiders."

The parking lot continued around her. Engines started. A basketball struck asphalt near the gym. Somewhere across the street, her swarm found two men unloading bread from a truck.

"Today?" Danny asked.

"This afternoon. No job. No patrol. I need to tell them circumstances changed before they hear it from someone else."

"Where?"

Taylor gave him the address. The old instinct objected so sharply that the words almost stopped. She said them anyway.

"Check-in?"

"Five-fifteen. Escalate at five-twenty-five. If I use the dog phrase without two taps, call Tower dispatch immediately."

"John with you?"

*Tell him I'm insulted he has to ask,* McClane said.

"Yes. He's insulted you asked."

Danny exhaled through his nose. "Good. Tell your opinionated backup I remain reserving judgment."

*Fair.*

"He says fair."

"Five-fifteen, Taylor."

"Five-fifteen."

She ended the call.

Nothing about giving Danny the address made her less capable of surviving the meeting. Nothing about his knowing placed him automatically in the room. The information existed outside her head now, attached to a clock and a person who would notice if the line broke.

It felt less like surrender than she had expected.

*"Alright,"* Gladly said, brightening. *"Since we're doing sincere moments, can I say something too?"*

*"No,"* Fury said.

*"I wasn't done asking."*

*"The answer's still no."*

*"I contain multitudes, Fury. Let me multitude at her for one second."*

Taylor laughed out loud this time, quick and surprised, and got a strange look from a passing freshman that she didn't have it in her to care about.

*You good, kid?* McClane asked, quieter now, just for her.

*Yeah,* she said, and meant it, really meant it, for the first time in what felt like a very long stretch of careful, guarded days. *Yeah. I think I actually am.*

Somewhere behind that thought, distant and steady, she felt him settle too, two strangers who'd been thrown together on the worst night of one very strange October, finding, against every reasonable expectation either of them had started with, something that was starting, cautiously, to feel less like an accident and more like a partnership worth keeping.

*Yippie kay yay,* she thought, quiet, almost private, a joke just for the two of them now, worn smooth from use, warm in a way it hadn't been that first terrified night in a cargo hold four thousand miles and one impossible bridge away.

*Mother Fluffer,* McClane finished, and she could hear the smile in it even without a mouth to make one with, and somewhere in the anonymous rows, she was almost certain, though she'd never actually admit to hearing it, somebody started quietly taking bets on which word came next time.



Chapter 28: The Waiting Room



She fell asleep on the couch that night, homework unfinished, television murmuring something she'd stopped following twenty minutes earlier, and found herself, without any sense of transition, standing in a room that hummed with the low electrical hum of every ops center she'd never actually visited, radar scope pulsing its slow green sweep against one wall.

The transition removed the couch but not the body that had fallen asleep on it.

Taylor's ankle brace remained beneath the cuff of her jeans. The stitches across her back pulled when she straightened. Her left ear carried the faint cotton-filled distance that arrived after long days. Even the ink mark on her palm, a reminder to call Danny at five-fifteen, had followed her into the room.

This was not a dream offering escape from consequences.

It was a place where consequences had chairs.

She knew this room. She'd never seen it, not with her own eyes, but she knew it the way you knew a place you'd lived in without ever quite touching, borrowed and familiar all at once.

Consoles formed two curved rows facing the radar display. Paper flight strips waited in plastic racks. A headset rested beside a coffee cup with a brown ring dried around its rim. Beyond the nearest bank of equipment stood a wall of dark windows that reflected the room without showing anything outside it.

Taylor knew which chair McClane had occupied during O'Malley's offer. She knew where he had tested the floor before trusting it. She knew the coffee had been burned hours before either of them arrived.

The memories were his.

Recognizing them with her own eyes made them briefly theirs.

And across from her, solid, real, unmistakably present in a way that made her breath catch, stood a man.

Mid-forties, tired around the eyes with the wear of more than one bad night, undershirt still faintly bloodstained from a fight that had happened, for him, what felt like only hours ago. He looked as disoriented as she felt, turning a slow circle, taking in the consoles and the burnt coffee smell and the wall clock ticking its steady, unhurried hours.

His right hand searched for a weapon before he remembered none had followed him. His left protected the knife wound automatically. Taylor had felt both instincts from inside. Watching muscles perform them made the voice in her head abruptly, uncomfortably human.

"McClane?"

He turned fast, cop-fast, and then went very still, staring at her the way a man stared at something he'd spent weeks knowing only as a voice.

"You're..." He stopped, started over, something working hard behind his eyes. "You're a kid. I mean, I knew that. I knew the number. Didn't stop it from being a whole different thing, seeing it."

His gaze moved over the ankle brace, the careful way she held one shoulder, and the healing cut visible above her collar.

"Jesus, kid."

"You knew about those too."

"Knowing and seeing. Whole different thing. Apparently that's tonight's theme."

"Yeah." Taylor felt suddenly, strangely shy, aware of herself in a way she hadn't been in weeks of talking to a voice with no face attached. "You're taller than I pictured."

"Everybody says that." A ghost of the old dry humor, warm underneath the disorientation. "Guy in the movies was shorter. Long story."

They stood six feet apart with a month's worth of intimacy and no idea what ordinary greeting fit it.

McClane offered his hand.

Taylor took it.

His palm was callused. His grip remained careful until hers tightened, then answered. The contact carried no thoughts, no shared balance, no borrowed instinct. For the first time since MedHall, McClane was entirely outside her.

The absence inside her head lasted only as long as the handshake. It still frightened her.

He saw that too.

"Still here," he said aloud.

His voice also answered from within.

Taylor released a breath. "Good."

"Gentlemen. And lady." A third voice, unhurried, amused, and Taylor turned to find a man in a DULLES OPS windbreaker seated at the console, watching them both with the satisfaction of someone who'd been looking forward to this moment for a while. "I do apologize for the informal venue. I find reunions go better somewhere familiar."

"O'Malley." McClane didn't relax exactly, but something in his stance shifted, recognition settling into wary respect. "You do this a lot? Pull people out of their own heads for a chat?"

"Rarely," O'Malley said. "Only when the partnership's actually earned it. Which, I should say, this one has, rather more thoroughly than most."

Up close, his borrowed face carried the same almost-correct quality McClane remembered. The operations chief from Dulles, but assembled from memory by something that understood every measurement and missed one human fraction. His smile began at the right time and reached his eyes a heartbeat late.

Taylor had promised Danny a conversation.

"You didn't ask me."

O'Malley's smile disappeared.

McClane turned from Taylor to him. "There it is."

"Yes," O'Malley said.

"You asked John," Taylor continued. "You explained the rules to him. You put him in my head while I was standing in a crowded room and didn't give me a choice."

O'Malley rose from the console. The satisfaction left his posture with the smile. "I did."

"Why?"

"Because I believed the moment required speed, and because the design of the connection made refusal after contact possible. I treated the ability to end an imposition as equivalent to consent before it. It is not."

Taylor had prepared for a joke, a cosmic explanation, or a statement that everything had worked out.

The admission left her without the next line.

McClane found one. "You do that often?"

"No answer improves what I did here."

"Wasn't asking for improvement."

"More often than I should have. Less often than I once did."

The wall clock ticked through the silence.

O'Malley faced Taylor. "The connection can be ended here. Entirely. John returns to the instant he left. You retain your own memories and whatever you have learned. No penalty, no erasure, no replacement passenger. If either of you asks, I will do it."

McClane looked at Taylor. Not through her, not from behind her thoughts. Across the room, giving the decision physical space.

"Your call, kid."

For one instant Taylor imagined silence inside her head. Complete privacy. No dry comment when she missed an exit, no second perspective finding the part of a room she overlooked, no witness to the thoughts she could not say aloud.

The idea hurt.

That did not make the decision automatic. Keeping someone because losing him hurt was not the same as choosing the arrangement that placed him there.

Taylor looked at McClane. "Do you want to go?"

He gave the question its full weight.

"I want to get back to my family. O'Malley says that costs them no time. I also want to stay until you don't need backup every time the room gets bad. Maybe longer, if you'll have me and the rules stay the rules. Both things are true."

Taylor turned to O'Malley.

"Then I choose to keep the connection. Now. Not retroactively. You were still wrong."

"Yes," O'Malley said. "I was."

"And if either of us changes our mind?"

"This room remains available. The first clear request ends it."

McClane stepped beside Taylor, not in front of her. "Put that in whatever passes for writing around here."

O'Malley took a blank flight strip from the rack. Words appeared beneath his pen as he wrote them. He turned the strip around.

**EITHER PARTY. FIRST CLEAR REQUEST. IMMEDIATE RETURN. NO PENALTY.**

Taylor read it. McClane read it. O'Malley placed it beneath the glass top of the central console where none of them could pretend it had not been said.

"Danny wants his name on the list of people unhappy with you," Taylor added.

O'Malley's almost-correct smile returned by a cautious degree. "That seems fair."

Taylor looked back at McClane, really looked, cataloging without meaning to, the same way she suspected he was cataloging her: the set of his jaw, the tired kindness in his eyes, a man who'd spent a month of her life as nothing but a voice and was, in the flesh, exactly as solid and exactly as tired as she'd always imagined. There was a scar along one forearm she'd never known about, pale and old, and a newer line of stitches at his ribs that made her chest ache just looking at it, evidence of a fight she'd only ever felt secondhand, filtered through a voice that had never once let the pain slow down what it needed to say.

"That's from the knife," she said, before she could stop herself, nodding at his side. "Isn't it. That first night."

"Yeah." He glanced down at it like he'd half forgotten it was there. "Guess some things follow you into the dream whether you want them to or not."

"You never told me how bad it was."

"Was busy."

"That's not an answer."

"It's the one I've got."

Taylor folded her arms.

McClane stared at the gesture, then laughed. "Okay. That's your dad. You got that whole thing from him."

"He says you talk with your hands when you think you're losing an argument."

"Your father and I need less access to each other's observations."

"He said thank you. Properly."

The humor settled.

"You told me."

"I repeated the words. That's different from seeing you hear them."

McClane nodded. "Then I heard him. Properly. Tell him again when you wake up."

"He also wrote six headings of safety rules on a medical bill."

"Six?"

"Contact. Backup. No one else knows is not a plan. Curfew. Team tomorrow. PRT with witness."

McClane counted them on his fingers. "That's a good list. Curfew's gonna be a problem."

"For me or him?"

"Yes."

Taylor told him about the school hallway, Mr. Quinlan's witness statement, and the call before her Undersiders meeting. McClane had experienced the events from within her, but telling him face to face changed their shape. He interrupted only to ask whether Danny had filed the contact violation and whether the five-fifteen check-in had happened.

"On time," Taylor said.

"Good."

"Lisa knew something had changed before I sat down."

"Also not exactly shocking."

"I told them my father knows I cape. I didn't tell them about you."

McClane glanced toward O'Malley. "Some things stay need-to-know."

"That's what I decided."

"Your decision," he emphasized.

The flight strip beneath the console glass made the words heavier.

"Ortega asked about you," Taylor added. "He sent a message through Miss Militia. It said Tower still owes him a stun charge and one functional knee."

"Tell him the knee was defective when issued."

"I already did."

McClane looked delighted. "See? Talking like me."

"I wanted to say thank you," she said, before she lost her nerve. "Properly. Not just in my head at two in the morning when I'm too tired to say it right."

"Kid, you don't owe me anything..." He stopped himself, huffed something that was almost a laugh. "Force of habit. You're allowed to say thank you. I'm allowed to hear it. Go ahead."

"Thank you." She said it plainly, and meant every word of it. "For not making me be brave alone. For teaching me the difference between a plan and a hope. For never once trying to take the wheel, even when it would've been so much easier for you if you had."

Something moved across his face, complicated, moved. "You're welcome." A beat. "And thank you, right back. For trusting a stranger with the inside of your own head. For being smarter and steadier than most rookies twice your age. For giving a washed-up cop stuck between his own bad night and God knows what else a reason to feel useful again."

"You weren't washed up."

"Felt like it, some nights." He said it without self-pity, just plain fact. "You gave that back to me. Didn't ask me to. Did it anyway."

Taylor stepped forward.

McClane did not assume. He waited until she put both arms around him, then returned the embrace carefully, one hand between her shoulders and the other avoiding the bandaged line beneath her shirt. He smelled like stale airport air, blood, and the soap from a bathroom he had used hours before entering a month of her life.

It lasted only a few seconds.

It was not paternal, romantic, or anything else requiring a name they had not chosen. It was proof that the person who had occupied every frightened silence was real enough to hold once, and disciplined enough to let go when she did.

When Taylor stepped back, McClane cleared his throat.

"All right. That's enough emotional competence for one night."

"You started it."

"I distinctly remember you saying thank you first."

O'Malley, tactfully, occupied himself with a strip of paper that didn't need his attention, giving them room.

"Is Holly okay?" Taylor asked, quieter now. "Do you know? Wherever you actually are right now?"

Something soft and complicated crossed his face. "She's on a plane over Dulles with fuel running out and men on the ground trying to make sure nobody lands. So no. Not okay yet." He looked toward the motionless wall clock. "But O'Malley puts me back in the second I left. Whatever is happening there hasn't moved while I've been here. I go back, and I finish it."

"You sound certain."

"I have to be. Also, I'm very hard to kill. Ask anybody."

Taylor looked at the blood on his undershirt.

"Not evidence in your favor."

"Still standing."

He looked at her, steady. "She'd like you. For what it's worth. She always did have a soft spot for stubborn people who didn't know when to quit."

"Tell her I said thank you too," Taylor said, surprising herself with how much she meant it. "For sharing you for a month. Even if she doesn't know she did."

Something flickered across his face, gratitude and grief tangled too close together to fully separate. "I'll tell her. Someday. However that works, wherever this all actually goes." A beat. "She'd probably ask if you're eating enough vegetables, knowing her."

"I'm not that stubborn."

"Kid, you triggered a fire alarm to out-think a supervillain instead of running. You're exactly that stubborn."

Taylor felt herself smile, unexpectedly, warm all the way through.

"For what it's worth," McClane said, and his voice had gone careful now, deliberate, a man choosing his words the way he'd chosen them all month, "I meant what I said about your dad. About not letting the job carry you clean out of your own life. I know I'm not... I know I don't get to be anybody's father here. That's not what this is. But if a guy who's spent a month riding shotgun in your head gets one piece of advice, it's that. Keep somebody like Danny close. Whatever else this life ends up asking of you."

"I will." She meant it. "I promise."

"That's all I needed to hear."

The room's edges had begun, faintly, to soften, the give of a dream sensing its own ending, and Taylor found she wasn't afraid of it, not the way she might have expected to be.

"This a one-time thing?" she asked, echoing a question she didn't know had ever been asked in this room before, though something in O'Malley's expression suggested it had.

"I find," O'Malley said, mild, not looking up from his paperwork, "that certain doors, once properly opened, have a habit of staying that way. Should you ever need to find your way back here, I suspect you'll manage it well enough."

"Good," Taylor said. "I'd like that."

"So would I," McClane agreed.

Somewhere behind them, in rows that had never once been visible from inside the dream itself, the gallery rose quietly, the way an audience did when a story had earned its ending the hard way. Nobody riffed on it. Some things, even for them, earned the silence instead.

The room dissolved slowly, consoles and radar scope and borrowed face all fading like morning mist burning off a runway, and in the last, soft edges of it, Taylor heard his voice, warm, unmistakably certain, following her back toward waking.

*See you tomorrow, kid,* McClane said. *Same as always. Not goodbye. Just until next time.*

*Yeah,* she thought, feeling the couch cushions solidify back under her, the television still murmuring, her own ordinary living room waiting on the other side of sleep. *Yeah. Until next time.*



Chapter 29: The Honest Trailer



In a world where a New York cop can't even attend a Christmas party without a busload of terrorists showing up, and a teenage girl can't attend a single charity gala without her own personal supervillain infestation, one studio dared to ask the question nobody was asking: what if these were somehow the same movie?

This is Med Hard.

Watch, as our protagonist, a fifteen-year-old insect telepath with abandonment issues and a swarm of bugs she is legally not old enough to be trusted with, gets possessed, sorry, "passenger-seated," by a forty-something New York police officer with a body count and a marriage he can't stop thinking about. It's the story of two people who spend a month knowing each other more intimately than sight allows, meet exactly once in a cosmic air-traffic office, and still take twenty-eight chapters to define the relationship as "opinionated backup."

Because nothing says healthy mentor partnership like an omnipotent stranger obtaining consent from the adult man, forgetting to ask the teenage girl whose mind he plans to renovate, and getting called on it during the emotional epilogue.

*"In my defense,"* O'Malley began.

The entire gallery leaned forward.

*"No,"* he finished. *"I was wrong."*

The gallery leaned back, visibly deprived of a trial it had already prepared snacks for.

Follow along as our reluctant hero turns one charity gala into the world's most aggressive building inspection, using nothing but bugs, a wasp with a grudge, three tons of chandelier, one disabled elevator, two catering carts, an oxygen cylinder, and the improvisational genius of a man whose preferred tactical doctrine is "whatever the room forgot to bolt down." Witness the birth of a franchise catchphrase so committed to bowdlerization it's used four different substitute words before the book remembers the explosives under the mezzanine.

Then discover the terrorists are not terrorists, the hostage is not a hostage, the escape vehicle is actually a moving van for incriminating paperwork, and the charity gala's transparency speech is being delivered by a Nazi supervillain whose financial plan depends on nobody asking why the state-audit archive has its own demolition bracket.

That's right. It isn't merely Die Hard at a gala.

It's Die Hard at a gala with an evidence-preservation order.

*"Excuse me,"* said a voice from the front row, familiar, air-traffic-controller flat. *"I recruited that plot. Some respect."*

This movie contains: one (1) knife fight in a cargo hold, zero (0) actual airplanes, a recruitment scene that is somehow both the most and least bureaucratic thing that happens all book, a gala that runs on chandeliers and chocolate fountains until it starts running on hostage counts, demolition receivers, roof charges, sprinkler mains, and one teenager's hand-drawn floor plan, plus exactly three uses of a sentence construction this narrator has personally sworn a blood oath never to use again.

*"Four,"* Fury corrected.

*"I'm choosing not to count the one in the credits."*

Because let's be honest, this genre never actually stopped being one movie wearing different coats. We got Die Hard on a Plane. Die Hard on a Boat. Die Hard on this other boat, because apparently one boat wasn't enough. Die Hard on a Train. Die Hard in the White House. Die Hard on this other plane, except this time with snakes on it, because by that point the formula had officially eaten its own tail. And now, after four decades of finding new buildings to almost-not-technically remake the same movie in, we've finally arrived at the one nobody saw coming.

*"Somebody was always going to do the gala eventually,"* Gib said. *"I'm just glad it had this much heart in it when they finally got there."*

So do yourself a favor, and enjoy: Die Hard At A Gala, With Superpowers, the crossover nobody commissioned, nobody expected, and, against every reasonable prediction this narrator was prepared to make going in, somehow actually earned its own ending.

Marvel, as a respected charity host turns out to be a literal Nazi supervillain, a twist absolutely nobody in-universe saw coming, despite the man checking hallway sightlines like a hostage negotiator and employing a security detail that, in retrospect, definitely should have set off more alarms than "huh, weird vibe." This is a movie that asks the hard question: what if the real villain wasn't the man with the metal hooks for hands, but the *entire adult social structure of Brockton Bay* that let him throw galas for a decade without anybody checking his tax returns?

Or, as it turns out, while scheduling someone to check his tax returns the next morning.

Gasp, as Kaiser's beautifully overcomplicated false flag uses two hundred civic leaders as witnesses to his own victimhood, demands an armored vehicle to remove court-preserved records through the loading dock, and rigs the archive wing to collapse if anyone notices the vehicle has eight figures of fascist accounting inside. It's a plan with everything: blackmail, insurance, political theater, murdered evidence, emergency powers, and a host whose greatest acting challenge is pretending not to recognize all five supervillains he personally invited.

The only flaw is one teenage girl noticing the archive credential arrive early.

Also the bugs.

Also the cop.

Also Miss Militia.

Also Max Anders being constitutionally incapable of letting an unknown observer exist for ninety minutes without turning his carefully rehearsed crime into the loudest possible version of itself.

So, five flaws.

Featuring an ensemble of Empire Eighty-Eight's finest, who turn MedHall into hostile territory one polite door closure at a time, then spend the rest of the night learning that declining a direct fight does not mean declining to drop the ceiling on them, trap them in elevators, bury them under catering equipment, or guide an entire PRT entry team around their powers using insects as runway lights.

Meet the official response, featuring Armsmaster as a man who sees a camera pointed by the villain and announces, "Finally, objective evidence," then sends a roof team into the ambush Tower warned him about. Watch him complete the rarest maneuver in superhero fiction: identify his own error, change tactics, help solve the problem, and later put his name on the failure without requiring a redemption death.

And meet Miss Militia, the only person outside who hears two radio clicks from a civilian source and immediately invents authentication, compartmentalization, and a code name instead of asking the teenager hiding under a banquet table to complete an intake form.

*"Strong opening,"* McClane said.

*"You judged her after two sentences,"* Gladly reminded him.

*"And I was right."*

*"That's the theme,"* the gallery agreed, warmly, for once without a joke riding shotgun on it. *"Outsmart, don't outfight. Somebody put that on a poster."*

Try to keep track of the running bets on when the signature line was going to land, a betting pool so aggressive it required its own internal economy, its own scandal, and at least one physical assault committed against a man who, fair enough, absolutely deserved it for forgetting he himself was the punchline of a running joke about being the punchline of a running joke.

Watch as an anonymous gallery member, never named, never confirmed, absolutely definitely not a cyberpunk novelist's fictional surveillance van partner, quietly steals every scene he's technically not even supposed to be in, using nothing but a dry wit, an encyclopedic knowledge of a completely different 1994 action movie, and the collective refusal of everyone around him to just say his whole name out loud.

*"I have a name,"* Gib said, wounded. *"It's right there. Everyone knows it now."*

*"Doesn't count if we don't say it in the trailer,"* Fury said. *"Rules are rules."*

Featuring a final act that takes the phrase "use the building" as a personal challenge: a blown roof entrance, a PRT trooper hanging over a stairwell, five hostages placed beside a support charge, a covert radio confession, three evacuation routes with named civilian leaders, one partially collapsed mezzanine, and Kaiser discovering that the exits he spent all night controlling have finally been assigned to people who aren't him.

Thrill, as Taylor rescues a trooper who outweighs her by eighty pounds while barefoot on a damaged stair rail, because apparently the story looked at "walk over broken glass" and decided homage required a more ergonomic distribution of foot trauma.

Cheer, as the hostages do not transform into a perfectly obedient evacuation crowd just because the plan is clever. They jam doors. Choose the wrong route. Freeze at hidden panels. Follow public figures instead of bug arrows. Force Taylor to revise while the building is flooding, the capes are moving, and four demolition receivers are waiting to learn whether Armsmaster's electronics pulse speaks their language.

And applaud the structural engineering department, whose official contribution to the climax is "three receivers successfully disabled" followed immediately by "unfortunately the fourth one has opinions."

This is a movie about a girl who learns to read a room, a cop who learns to help without taking control, and a father who finds out exactly how close he came to losing everything, then responds by negotiating check-in procedures with the teenage supervillain hunter living in his house. Three separate character arcs, twenty-nine chapters, and somehow, against every reasonable prediction, all three land.

Watch Danny Hebert experience the full parental speedrun: fancy gala, hostage, human bomb marker, giant Nazi, secret bug daughter, invisible New York cop, and an argument about whether forty-five minutes is a reasonable patrol check-in interval. Then watch him reject both useless extremes, "you may never leave the house" and "apparently this is fine," in favor of writing **NO ONE ELSE KNOWS IS NOT A PLAN** on the back of a medical bill.

Parenting: now with duress authentication.

*"Don't undersell it,"* McClane said, quiet, and there was nothing dry in it at all. *"That's not nothing. That's the whole reason any of us showed up."*

And ask yourself the two questions this narrator simply cannot get past. One: how does a swarm of insects, controlled telepathically by a fifteen-year-old, consistently build a better threat map than the official command vehicle outside? And two, far more pressingly: how did Max Anders think "pretend to be my own hostage while tapping orders against a councilwoman" was a plan that would survive contact with somebody who could listen through a fly under his collar?

*"To be fair,"* Gladly offered, *"rich people are very good at not looking directly at the help."*

*"That's the most honest thing anyone's said in this entire trailer."*

Try to spot the exact moment this story stops being surveillance at a bad party and becomes a full building siege, a tonal shift announced by locked doors, confiscated phones, armed caterers, and Hookwolf putting his arm through a banquet table because subtlety has officially left the venue. Marvel as the entire architecture, laundry chute, dumbwaiter, hidden stair, fire doors, elevator, ventilation system, sprinkler mains, loading shutter, and deeply overinsured chandelier, becomes load-bearing plot infrastructure, proving once and for all that no detail introduced in Chapter Three is ever, ever wasted.

*"Chekhov's laundry chute,"* the gallery said, delighted with itself. *"Somebody frame that."*

Then, after two hundred hostages, six structural charges, five named cape lieutenants, four rotating euphemisms, three exit routes, two injured fathers, and one extremely judgmental wasp, slow down for the quiet dream sequence where the partners finally meet face to face and the immortal plot facilitator receives his performance review.

Discover that "it worked out" is not a consent policy.

Learn that either partner can end the connection, immediately and without penalty, in terms so binding they are written on an air-traffic flight strip beneath glass.

And watch Taylor keep McClane, not because a crossover premise says she has to, but because once the choice is genuinely hers, she makes it.

*"That's better,"* Fury said.

O'Malley looked at the flight strip. *"Yes. It is."*

Enjoy a soundtrack that exists only in this narrator's own head, because nobody actually budgeted for one, featuring exactly zero original songs and one (1) recurring string quartet that has, by the end of the book, survived more workplace trauma than most emergency responders.

Celebrate the statistically improbable achievement of a building siege with no civilian fatalities, though thirty-two people receive treatment, eleven are hospitalized, two PRT troopers are injured, four attackers die, one real security guard spends several hours misidentified because an attacker stole his blazer, and Alabaster contributes forty-three consecutive minutes of percussion from inside an elevator.

*"He reset every few seconds,"* Gladly said. *"Imagine how fresh the irritation stayed."*

So c'mon, ride along with the cop who can't stop working, the kid who can't stop cataloging exits, and the gallery that can't stop betting on a rotating catchphrase nobody asked for but everybody, somehow, ended up loving anyway.

**Starring:**

Bruce Willis, Back When He Still Cared, as A Man Whose Vacation Insurance Clearly Does Not Cover This

Senator Fred Thompson, Yes, That Kind Of Senator, as The Guy Who Recruits People In A Room That Definitely Isn't A Real Tower

Miss Militia, as The Only Person On The Radio Who Understands That Listening Is An Action

Armsmaster, as Objective Evidence Has Logged On And Would Like To Apologize For The Roof

Danny Hebert, as World's Most Reluctant Mission Control, Operating From The Back Of A Medical Bill

That Guy Everybody Recognizes From A 90s Sitcom About A Blue-Collar Family But Can't Quite Place, Probably, As A Voice In The Cheap Seats Who Would Very Much Like It If Nobody Confirmed He Was Here

A Teenager With No Prior Screen Credits Whatsoever, Whose Extracurricular Activities Now Officially Include Domestic Terrorism Prevention

Max Anders, as Hans Gruber If Hans Owned The Building, Hosted The Party, Controlled The Charity, Scheduled The Audit, And Still Somehow Thought He Was The Subtle One

An Entire Charity Gala's Worth Of Empire Eighty-Eight Cosplaying As Rent-A-Cops, Doing Surprisingly Solid Rent-A-Cop Impressions

One (1) Wasp (Uncredited, Criminally), Doing More Damage To This Plot Than Most Named Characters

One (1) State Evidence-Preservation Order, as The Closest Thing This Movie Has To Bearer Bonds

A Horn Player Whose Union Rep Definitely Needs To Hear About This

A Marcus Thorne-Shaped Cautionary Tale About Sourcing, As Every Journalism Professor's Worst Nightmare

Directed by a Man Who Does Not Exist, As Far As Anyone Can Prove

*"Yippie kay yay,"* the whole gallery said, together, for the first and only time all book, and let the last word hang there, unbowdlerized, unfinished, exactly the way the best endings always did.
